    AWS foundations, and an account you won't regret

    Before a single line of application code: a safe account, a mental model of the platform, and cost guardrails so that a mistake at 2am costs you £4 instead of £4,000. Everything later in this manual assumes this sprint is done.

    Time
    Half a day
    You'll produce
    An AWS org, an admin identity, budgets, a working CLI
    Running cost
    ≈ $0
    Interview value
    High — "how do you set up an account?" is a real senior question

    §1What we are building, end to end

    The product: users upload documents (invoices, contracts, receipts, scanned PDFs). The platform stores them durably, runs OCR to pull out text and structured fields, uses a large language model to classify, summarise and extract key data, and then makes everything searchable — including semantically searchable ("find contracts that mention early termination"). Multiple organisations (tenants) share the platform without ever seeing each other's data.

    Here is the whole thing on one line. Don't worry about the pieces yet; every box gets its own sprint.

    React SPAVite + TS
    →
    CloudFront+ S3 static
    →
    ALBHTTPS, WAF
    →
    Fastify APIECS Fargate
    →
    RDS Postgres+ pgvector
    Browserpresigned PUT
    →
    S3raw bucket
    →
    EventBridgeobject created
    →
    SQS+ DLQ
    →
    Lambdaingest worker
    →
    Textractasync OCR
    →
    Bedrockclassify + embed
    Code you write AWS infrastructure AI services

    Why this project is a good portfolio piece

    • It has a real asynchronous pipeline. Anyone can build CRUD. Event-driven ingestion with retries, dead letters, and idempotency is where senior conversations actually live.
    • It touches every layer. Frontend, API, database, queue, serverless, containers, IAM, networking, observability, CI/CD, cost.
    • The AI is load-bearing but not magic. Textract and Bedrock solve a genuine problem (documents are unstructured) rather than being bolted on for a demo.
    • Multi-tenancy forces you to think about isolation. Cross-tenant leakage is the failure mode reviewers ask about first, and it is hard to bolt on afterwards.

    §2The ten AWS ideas you need before you touch anything

    You said you're a beginner on AWS, so let's front-load the vocabulary. Read this once; it will save you hours of confusion later.

    1. Regions and Availability Zones

    A Region is a geographic area (eu-west-1 = Ireland, us-east-1 = N. Virginia). A Region contains 3+ Availability Zones (AZs), which are physically separate data centres a few miles apart, connected by fast private fibre. Almost every AWS resource lives in exactly one Region, and many live in a specific AZ.

    Two rules that follow from this:

    • Pick one Region and stay in it. Cross-region data transfer costs money and adds latency. For this project: pick a Region that has Bedrock with the models you want and Textract. eu-west-1 (Ireland) and us-east-1 both work; us-east-1 has the widest service and model availability, eu-west-1 is better if you care about EU data residency. This manual assumes eu-west-1 and flags where it matters.
    • Spread across AZs for availability. Your database gets a standby in a second AZ; your containers run in at least two AZs behind a load balancer. An AZ can fail; a well-built app shrugs.
    Beginner trap

    The AWS console has a Region selector in the top right, and it's sticky per browser tab. If your S3 bucket "disappeared", you're almost certainly looking at the wrong Region. Some services are global-ish and always show us-east-1: IAM, CloudFront, Route 53, and ACM certificates used by CloudFront.

    2. The shared responsibility model

    AWS secures the cloud (hardware, hypervisors, physical access, managed-service patching). You secure what you put in the cloud (your data, your IAM policies, your security groups, your application code, your encryption choices). Almost every AWS security breach you read about is the customer's half: a public S3 bucket, a leaked access key, an over-permissive role.

    3. IAM: principals, policies, roles

    IAM (Identity and Access Management) is the single most important service to understand, and the one beginners most often get wrong.

    TermWhat it actually isUse it for
    PrincipalWhoever is making the request — a user, a role session, a serviceThe "who" in every authorisation decision
    PolicyA JSON document listing allowed/denied Actions on Resources, optionally with ConditionsExpressing permission
    Identity policyA policy attached to a user or role"This role can read that bucket"
    Resource policyA policy attached to the resource itself (bucket policy, KMS key policy, SQS queue policy)Cross-account access, restricting everyone (e.g. deny non-TLS)
    RoleAn identity with policies but no long-lived credentials, which principals assume to get temporary keysEverything. Services, humans, CI
    Trust policyThe role's answer to "who is allowed to assume me?"Letting ECS, Lambda, or GitHub assume a role

    Evaluation logic, simplified: an explicit Deny always wins; otherwise you need an explicit Allow; with no matching statement the answer is deny. That's it — everything else is nuance (permission boundaries, SCPs, session policies).

    Habit worth forming

    Nothing in this project uses a long-lived access key except your own laptop, and even that we'll avoid. Applications get roles. CI gets a role via OIDC. Humans get short-lived credentials from IAM Identity Center. If you can say that sentence in an interview and explain each clause, you're ahead of most candidates.

    4. VPC, subnets, security groups

    A VPC is your private network in a Region. Inside it you carve subnets, each pinned to one AZ. A subnet is "public" if its route table sends 0.0.0.0/0 to an Internet Gateway; it's "private" if outbound internet goes through a NAT Gateway (or nowhere at all). Security groups are stateful virtual firewalls attached to resources: you allow inbound traffic, and the return traffic is automatically permitted.

    Our shape: load balancer in public subnets, containers and database in private subnets. Nothing that holds data is directly reachable from the internet.

    Cost landmine

    A NAT Gateway costs roughly $32/month per AZ just to exist, plus ~$0.045 per GB processed. Two AZs of NAT is ~$65/month before any traffic — likely the biggest line on your bill. Sprint 07 shows three ways out: a single shared NAT for dev, VPC endpoints so S3/ECR/Secrets traffic never leaves AWS, or destroying the environment when you're not demoing.

    5. Managed vs serverless vs self-managed

    Three ways to run the same logic, and knowing when to pick which is the senior skill:

    ModelExamples hereGood atBad at
    Serverless functionsLambda ingest/OCR workersBursty, event-driven, scale-to-zero, no capacity planningLong-running work (>15 min), heavy deps, cold starts, per-request cost at high volume
    Managed containersECS Fargate APILong-lived HTTP, warm connection pools, any runtime, no server patchingAlways-on cost, slower deploys than a Lambda zip
    Managed data servicesRDS, S3, SQSDurability, backups, replication done for youLess control, version upgrade windows
    Self-managed EC2(we deliberately avoid it)Total control, odd workloads, licensingYou now own patching, AMIs, scaling, monitoring, and your weekends

    We use EC2 exactly once, in Sprint 07, as a comparison exercise so you can explain why you didn't use it.

    6. Eventual consistency and at-least-once delivery

    Distributed systems truths that will bite you: SQS standard queues deliver at least once and don't guarantee order, so your consumers must be idempotent. S3 is now strongly consistent for reads-after-writes, but event notifications can still arrive twice or out of order. Design for duplicates from day one — Sprint 04 does exactly that with a database-level dedupe key.

    7. Encryption: at rest and in transit

    At rest: S3 encrypts everything by default (SSE-S3); we upgrade to SSE-KMS with a customer-managed key so we control the key policy and get CloudTrail records of every decrypt. RDS gets storage encryption at creation time (you cannot enable it later without a snapshot restore — remember that). In transit: TLS everywhere, enforced with a bucket policy condition and an ALB HTTPS-only listener.

    8. Least privilege, in practice

    Not "attach AmazonS3FullAccess". Real least privilege looks like: this role may s3:GetObject on arn:aws:s3:::idp-raw-prod/* and nothing else, and only if the request came over TLS. You'll write policies like that in Sprint 02, and IAM Access Analyzer will tell you where you over-granted.

    9. Tagging and naming

    Every resource gets Project, Environment, Owner, ManagedBy=terraform, CostCenter. This is not bureaucracy: tags are how you attribute cost, how you find orphaned resources, and how you write "delete everything tagged dev" scripts. Terraform's default_tags makes it one block of code.

    10. Everything is an API call, and CloudTrail records it

    The console, the CLI, Terraform, and the SDKs all hit the same APIs. CloudTrail logs those calls — who did what, when, from where. When something mysterious happens, CloudTrail is the answer. Turn it on now.

    §2bFundamentals: how AWS decides what to charge you

    Beginners are surprised by AWS bills because they assume the cost is "the servers". Almost everything is billed along four axes, and once you can spot which axes a service uses, you can predict the bill before you create the resource.

    AxisWhat it meansExamples in this project
    TimeYou pay per hour or per second while the thing exists, whether or not you use itNAT Gateway, RDS instance, ALB, VPC interface endpoints, KMS keys
    RequestsYou pay per API call or per operationS3 GET and PUT, KMS decrypt, Lambda invocations, SQS messages
    Volume storedYou pay per gigabyte-month, and the rate depends on storage classS3 objects, RDS storage, CloudWatch log retention, EBS
    Data transferYou pay to move bytes across certain boundariesNAT processing, cross-AZ traffic, egress to the internet

    Three rules follow from that table, and they explain most cost surprises.

    1. Time-billed resources are the dangerous ones, because they charge while you sleep. A forgotten NAT Gateway costs $32 a month forever. A forgotten Lambda costs nothing, because it is request-billed and nothing is invoking it. When you are learning, prefer request-billed services and destroy time-billed ones.
    2. Data transfer into AWS is free; moving it around and out is not. Traffic between AZs is billed in both directions. Traffic to the internet is billed. Traffic to an AWS service through a NAT Gateway is billed twice over, once for NAT processing and once as transfer. That last one is why VPC endpoints exist.
    3. Request charges look trivial until they are not. KMS costs about $0.03 per ten thousand requests. Harmless, until you realise a naive setup makes one KMS call per S3 object read, and you are reading millions of objects. S3 Bucket Keys exist precisely to collapse that.
    A useful habit

    Before you create anything, ask which of the four axes it bills on. If the answer includes "time", ask whether you need it running when you are not looking at it. That single question is most of cost engineering, and it is the reason this manual keeps telling you to destroy the dev environment.

    §3Account setup, step by step

    Step 1 — Create the account and lock down root

    1. Sign up at aws.amazon.com. Use an email alias you control long-term (e.g. you+aws-idp@gmail.com).
    2. Enable MFA on the root user immediately. Authenticator app or hardware key.
    3. Do not create root access keys. If you already have any, delete them.
    4. Set alternate contacts (billing/security) in Account Settings.
    5. Never log in as root again except for the handful of tasks that require it (closing the account, changing support plans).

    Step 2 — An admin identity that isn't root

    Two options. Take the first one if you can.

    OptionHowTrade-off
    IAM Identity Center (recommended)Enable AWS Organizations + Identity Center, create a user, assign the AdministratorAccess permission set, then aws configure ssoShort-lived credentials, no keys on disk, scales to multiple accounts. ~15 min extra setup.
    IAM user + access keyCreate user, attach AdministratorAccess, generate an access key, aws configureFast, but you now have a permanent secret on your laptop that can't be automatically rotated. Acceptable for a solo learning account; say so out loud in interviews.
    Shell~/.aws/config (Identity Center path)
    # One-time interactive setup — opens a browser, no long-lived keys stored
aws configure sso
# SSO start URL:  https://d-xxxxxxxxxx.awsapps.com/start
# SSO region:     eu-west-1
# Profile name:   idp-admin

# Refresh credentials whenever they expire (8h default)
aws sso login --profile idp-admin

# Verify who you are — the single most useful AWS command
aws sts get-caller-identity --profile idp-admin
# {
#   "UserId": "AROAX...:you@example.com",
#   "Account": "123456789012",
#   "Arn": "arn:aws:sts::123456789012:assumed-role/AWSReservedSSO_AdministratorAccess_.../you@example.com"
# }

# Make the profile and region the default for this shell
export AWS_PROFILE=idp-admin
export AWS_REGION=eu-west-1
    Get in the habit

    Run aws sts get-caller-identity before anything destructive. "Which account am I in?" is the root cause of a genuinely alarming share of production incidents.

    Step 3 — Cost guardrails (do not skip this)

    You are about to create infrastructure that costs money by the hour. Set up three things now.

    1. A zero-spend alert. Budgets → create a cost budget of $1 with an alert at 100% of actual. You'll get an email the first time you're charged anything at all — invaluable for spotting accidents.
    2. A real monthly budget. Set it to whatever you're willing to lose — $30, $50 — with alerts at 50%, 80%, 100% of forecast. Forecast alerts warn you before the damage, not after.
    3. Cost Explorer on, with tag-based grouping. Activate Project and Environment as cost allocation tags in Billing settings (takes ~24h to start populating).
    Shellzero-spend budget via CLI
    cat > /tmp/budget.json <<'JSON'
{
  "BudgetName": "idp-zero-spend-tripwire",
  "BudgetLimit": { "Amount": "1", "Unit": "USD" },
  "TimeUnit": "MONTHLY",
  "BudgetType": "COST"
}
JSON

cat > /tmp/notify.json <<'JSON'
[{
  "Notification": {
    "NotificationType": "ACTUAL",
    "ComparisonOperator": "GREATER_THAN",
    "Threshold": 100,
    "ThresholdType": "PERCENTAGE"
  },
  "Subscribers": [{ "SubscriptionType": "EMAIL", "Address": "you@example.com" }]
}]
JSON

aws budgets create-budget \
  --account-id "$(aws sts get-caller-identity --query Account --output text)" \
  --budget file:///tmp/budget.json \
  --notifications-with-subscribers file:///tmp/notify.json

    Step 4 — Baseline security settings

    Shellaccount baseline
    # Block ALL public S3 access at the account level. Individual buckets can no
# longer opt into being public — a very good default for a document platform.
aws s3control put-public-access-block \
  --account-id "$(aws sts get-caller-identity --query Account --output text)" \
  --public-access-block-configuration \
    'BlockPublicAcls=true,IgnorePublicAcls=true,BlockPublicPolicy=true,RestrictPublicBuckets=true'

# Default EBS encryption on (harmless, closes a whole class of finding)
aws ec2 enable-ebs-encryption-by-default --region eu-west-1

# Confirm CloudTrail is recording management events (a default trail usually exists)
aws cloudtrail describe-trails --query 'trailList[].{Name:Name,Multi:IsMultiRegionTrail}'

    We'll add GuardDuty, Security Hub, and IAM Access Analyzer in Sprint 11, once there's something worth watching. Turning them on now just generates findings about an empty account.

    Step 5 — Request Bedrock model access

    Bedrock models are opt-in per Region and approval can take minutes to a day. Do it now so you're not blocked in Sprint 06: Bedrock console → Model access → enable the Anthropic Claude models and Amazon Titan Text Embeddings V2. Then verify from the CLI:

    Shellverify bedrock access
    aws bedrock list-foundation-models --region eu-west-1 \
  --query 'modelSummaries[?contains(modelId, `claude`) || contains(modelId, `titan-embed`)].modelId' \
  --output table

# Also list inference profiles — cross-region profiles (ids like eu.anthropic.*)
# are how you get capacity across several Regions with one identifier.
aws bedrock list-inference-profiles --region eu-west-1 \
  --query 'inferenceProfileSummaries[].inferenceProfileId' --output table

    Step 6 — Local toolchain

    Shellprerequisites
    # macOS via Homebrew; adapt for your OS
brew install awscli terraform node@22 postgresql@16 jq
brew install --cask docker

aws --version        # aws-cli/2.x
terraform -version   # >= 1.9  (we use S3 native state locking, 1.10+ preferred)
node -v              # >= 20, 22 LTS ideal
docker --version

    Repository skeleton — create it now, fill it over the next eleven sprints:

    Textrepo layout
    idp-platform/
├─ apps/
│  ├─ api/                 # Fastify + Drizzle (Sprint 01, 03, 04, 06)
│  └─ web/                 # React + Vite + TS (Sprint 10)
├─ services/
│  ├─ ingest-worker/       # Lambda: S3 event → start Textract (Sprint 04-05)
│  ├─ extract-worker/      # Lambda: Textract done → parse → persist (Sprint 05)
│  └─ enrich-worker/       # Lambda: Bedrock classify/summarise/embed (Sprint 06)
├─ packages/
│  ├─ db/                  # Drizzle schema + node-pg-migrate migrations
│  └─ shared/              # zod contracts, types, logger
├─ infra/
│  ├─ bootstrap/           # state bucket (chicken-and-egg, run once)
│  ├─ modules/             # reusable TF modules
│  └─ envs/{dev,prod}/     # root modules per environment
├─ .github/workflows/      # CI/CD (Sprint 09)
├─ docker-compose.yml      # local postgres + localstack
└─ docs/adr/               # architecture decision records
    Write it down

    That docs/adr/ folder is worth real points. An Architecture Decision Record is a one-page markdown file: context, options considered, decision, consequences. Write one per significant choice in this project (Fargate vs Lambda for the API, EventBridge vs direct S3→SQS, pgvector vs OpenSearch). In an interview you can say "here's the written trade-off analysis" instead of reconstructing it from memory.

    §4Trade-offs decided in this sprint

    DecisionChosenRejectedWhy
    Account structureSingle account, environments separated by Terraform workspace + namingOne account per environment via OrganizationsMulti-account is correct at company scale (hard blast-radius boundary, separate limits). For a solo portfolio it triples setup cost and complexity. Know the answer; choose pragmatically and say why.
    Human accessIAM Identity Center, short-lived credsIAM user + long-lived access keyNo permanent secret on disk; matches what real orgs do.
    Regioneu-west-1us-east-1EU residency story for a document platform; both have Textract + Bedrock. us-east-1 has broader model availability and is cheapest for a few services — a legitimate alternative.
    Public accessAccount-level S3 public block onPer-bucket controlDefence in depth: no future bucket can accidentally go public.

    §5Verify and test

    "Testing" infrastructure at this stage means proving the guardrails actually hold.

    Shellsmoke checks
    # 1. I am not root
aws sts get-caller-identity --query Arn --output text | grep -q ':root$' \
  && echo "FAIL: using root" || echo "OK: not root"

# 2. Root has MFA and no access keys
aws iam get-account-summary --query \
  'SummaryMap.{RootMFA:AccountMFAEnabled,RootKeys:AccountAccessKeysPresent}'
# Expect RootMFA: 1, RootKeys: 0

# 3. Public access block is on
aws s3control get-public-access-block \
  --account-id "$(aws sts get-caller-identity --query Account --output text)"

# 4. Budgets exist
aws budgets describe-budgets \
  --account-id "$(aws sts get-caller-identity --query Account --output text)" \
  --query 'Budgets[].BudgetName'

# 5. Bedrock is reachable and a model responds (cheap: a few tokens)
aws bedrock-runtime converse \
  --region eu-west-1 \
  --model-id eu.anthropic.claude-sonnet-4-5-20250929-v1:0 \
  --messages '[{"role":"user","content":[{"text":"Reply with the single word: ready"}]}]' \
  --inference-config '{"maxTokens":10}' \
  --query 'output.message.content[0].text'
    If Bedrock fails

    AccessDeniedException → model access not yet granted, or your IAM principal lacks bedrock:InvokeModel. ValidationException about the model id → that model isn't available in your Region, or you need the cross-region inference profile prefix (eu. / us.) rather than the bare model id. Run the list-inference-profiles command from Step 5 and use an id from that list.

    §6Monitoring you can set up today

    • Budget alerts → email. Already done above.
    • CloudTrail recording management events in all Regions. Verify with the command in Step 4.
    • A weekly habit: open Cost Explorer, group by Service, look at the last 7 days. Ten seconds, and it teaches you what things actually cost — knowledge that shows up immediately in interviews.

    §7Interview questions on this material

    Walk me through how you'd set up a new AWS account for a production workload.

    Structure the answer as layers. Identity: MFA on root, no root keys, root never used operationally; humans access through IAM Identity Center with permission sets, so credentials are short-lived; workloads use IAM roles, never keys. Boundaries: at company scale, AWS Organizations with separate accounts per environment plus Service Control Policies to deny things like disabling CloudTrail or leaving approved Regions — the account is the only hard blast-radius boundary AWS gives you. Guardrails: account-level S3 public access block, default EBS encryption, CloudTrail to a locked S3 bucket, Config rules or Security Hub for drift, budgets with forecast alerts. Operations: everything through Terraform with remote state, mandatory tags for cost allocation, and a tagging policy enforced in CI.

    Then add the honest caveat: "in my portfolio project I used a single account with environment separation by naming and Terraform workspaces, because multi-account tripled setup effort for a solo build. In a team I'd start multi-account from day one — retrofitting it means migrating every resource."

    Explain the difference between an IAM user, a role, and a resource policy.

    A user is a permanent identity with long-lived credentials — a password and/or access keys. A role is an identity with permissions but no permanent credentials; a principal assumes it via STS and receives temporary keys that expire. A role has two policy sides: the trust policy (who may assume it) and its attached identity policies (what it may then do).

    An identity policy hangs off a principal — "this role can read that bucket". A resource policy hangs off the resource — "this bucket can be read by that role", or more usefully "deny anyone not using TLS". Resource policies are the only way to grant cross-account access without the other account's cooperation on both sides, and the only place you can express "deny everybody, including future principals" for a given resource.

    Evaluation: explicit deny wins, then explicit allow, otherwise implicit deny. For same-account access, an allow in either the identity or resource policy is generally enough; cross-account needs an allow on both sides.

    Why should applications use roles rather than access keys?

    Credentials from a role are temporary and auto-rotated by the SDK, so a leak has a time-boxed blast radius. There is no secret to store, so no secret to accidentally commit, log, or bake into an image — the two most common causes of real AWS compromises. Role sessions are attributable in CloudTrail down to the session name. And there's nothing to remember to rotate, which means nothing to forget.

    Concretely in this project: ECS tasks get a task role, Lambdas get an execution role, GitHub Actions assumes a role via OIDC federation with no stored secret at all, and my laptop uses Identity Center. The only long-lived AWS secret in the whole system is zero.

    How do you keep AWS costs under control on a project like this?

    Four levers, in order of impact. Architecture: the biggest wins are structural — scale-to-zero Lambda for bursty ingest instead of idle containers, VPC endpoints to avoid NAT data processing charges, S3 lifecycle rules to move raw scans to Infrequent Access after 30 days and Glacier after 90. Right-sizing: Fargate at 0.25 vCPU / 512MB for dev, a db.t4g.micro single-AZ dev database instead of Multi-AZ. Visibility: mandatory cost-allocation tags, Cost Explorer grouped by tag, budgets with forecast alerts, and Cost Anomaly Detection. Lifecycle: a terraform destroy for the dev environment when I'm not using it, and an --auto-approve teardown script so it's frictionless.

    Know the actual numbers for your own architecture. For this platform: NAT Gateway at roughly $65 a month for two AZs is the largest fixed cost, ahead of RDS. Textract charges about $1.50 per thousand pages for plain text detection and about $50 per thousand with forms and tables analysis. That ratio is why the pipeline runs the expensive analysis only on document classes where the structured fields are actually used.

    What's an Availability Zone, and how does it change your design?

    An AZ is one or more discrete data centres within a Region with independent power, cooling, and networking, connected to sibling AZs by low-latency private links. It's AWS's unit of correlated failure: a power event or flood takes out an AZ, not a Region.

    Design consequences here: private subnets in at least two AZs; the ALB has nodes in both; the ECS service spreads tasks across AZs so losing one halves capacity rather than causing an outage; RDS runs Multi-AZ in production, giving synchronous replication to a standby and automatic failover in typically 60–120 seconds. S3 and SQS are already multi-AZ internally, so nothing to do there. The trade-off worth writing down: cross-AZ data transfer is billed, and a chatty service split across AZs can generate a surprising transfer bill — one reason to keep the database connection pool efficient.

    §8Definition of done

    Sprint 01 · Ground work

    Domain model and a local stack that mirrors production

    Everything that follows is easier if the data model is right. This sprint builds the schema, the migration workflow, and a Fastify API that runs entirely on your laptop — with Postgres and fake AWS services in Docker — so you can iterate in seconds instead of waiting on terraform apply.

    Time
    1–2 days
    You'll produce
    Schema, migrations, Fastify API, docker-compose, integration tests
    Running cost
    $0 — nothing in AWS yet
    Key idea
    The status field is a state machine, not a label

    §1Requirements

    Functional

    • A tenant (organisation) contains users. All data is scoped to a tenant.
    • A user uploads a document; the platform records its metadata, storage location, checksum, and a processing status that advances through a defined lifecycle.
    • OCR produces an extraction: full text, per-page text, confidence scores, and any structured key/value pairs and tables.
    • Enrichment produces a classification (invoice / contract / receipt / other), a summary, and typed entities (amounts, dates, counterparties).
    • Documents are split into chunks with vector embeddings for semantic search.
    • Every state change is recorded in an append-only audit log.

    Non-functional

    • Tenant isolation must be enforced in more than one place (application + database).
    • Processing is asynchronous and idempotent: the same S3 event delivered twice must not create two extractions.
    • All migrations are forward-only, reversible, and run in CI before the new app version starts serving.
    • p95 API latency under 200 ms for reads, excluding upload transfer time.

    §2The domain model

    Nine tables. The interesting decisions are in the notes underneath, not the DDL.

    tenantsorg boundary
    →
    userscognito_sub
    →
    documentsstatus FSM
    →
    extractionsOCR output
    →
    enrichmentsLLM output
    documents1
    →
    doc_chunkstext + vector(1024)
    →
    entitiestyped facts
    →
    audit_logappend-only
    →
    processing_jobsidempotency

    The document lifecycle is an explicit state machine

    Beginners model status as a free-text column and then discover, three sprints later, that documents are stuck in impossible states. Define the transitions up front and enforce them in one place:

    TypeScriptpackages/shared/src/document-status.ts
    export const DOCUMENT_STATUS = [
  'pending_upload',   // row created, presigned URL issued, bytes not yet in S3
  'uploaded',         // S3 confirmed the object exists
  'extracting',       // Textract job submitted
  'extracted',        // OCR complete and persisted
  'enriching',        // Bedrock classification/summary/embeddings in flight
  'ready',            // fully processed and searchable
  'failed',           // terminal failure; failure_reason is populated
  'quarantined',      // rejected: bad type, too large, malware signal
] as const;

export type DocumentStatus = (typeof DOCUMENT_STATUS)[number];

/** The only legal transitions. Anything else is a bug we want to hear about. */
const TRANSITIONS: Record<DocumentStatus, readonly DocumentStatus[]> = {
  pending_upload: ['uploaded', 'quarantined', 'failed'],
  uploaded:       ['extracting', 'quarantined', 'failed'],
  extracting:     ['extracted', 'failed'],
  extracted:      ['enriching', 'failed'],
  enriching:      ['ready', 'failed'],
  ready:          ['enriching'],   // re-enrichment with a newer model is allowed
  failed:         ['uploaded'],    // operator-triggered replay
  quarantined:    [],
};

export function canTransition(from: DocumentStatus, to: DocumentStatus): boolean {
  return TRANSITIONS[from].includes(to);
}

export class IllegalTransitionError extends Error {
  constructor(public readonly from: DocumentStatus, public readonly to: DocumentStatus) {
    super(`Illegal document status transition: ${from} -> ${to}`);
    this.name = 'IllegalTransitionError';
  }
}
    Two reasons this matters later

    Two reasons an interviewer will like it. First, ready → enriching exists on purpose: when you swap to a better model you want to re-process without deleting anything. Second, failed → uploaded is your replay path — combined with the SQS dead-letter queue in Sprint 04, that's a complete story for "how do you recover from a bad deploy that failed 4,000 documents?"

    The schema, in Drizzle

    TypeScriptpackages/db/src/schema.ts
    import {
  pgTable, uuid, text, timestamp, integer, bigint, jsonb, boolean,
  index, uniqueIndex, pgEnum, numeric, vector, primaryKey,
} from 'drizzle-orm/pg-core';
import { sql, relations } from 'drizzle-orm';

export const documentStatus = pgEnum('document_status', [
  'pending_upload', 'uploaded', 'extracting', 'extracted',
  'enriching', 'ready', 'failed', 'quarantined',
]);

export const docClass = pgEnum('doc_class', [
  'invoice', 'contract', 'receipt', 'statement', 'identity', 'other',
]);

export const tenants = pgTable('tenants', {
  id:        uuid('id').primaryKey().defaultRandom(),
  name:      text('name').notNull(),
  slug:      text('slug').notNull(),
  // Per-tenant limits, enforced in the API. Keeps plan logic out of code.
  quotaBytes: bigint('quota_bytes', { mode: 'number' }).notNull().default(5_368_709_120),
  settings:  jsonb('settings').$type<{ autoEnrich: boolean; retentionDays: number }>()
               .notNull().default({ autoEnrich: true, retentionDays: 365 }),
  createdAt: timestamp('created_at', { withTimezone: true }).notNull().defaultNow(),
}, (t) => [uniqueIndex('tenants_slug_key').on(t.slug)]);

export const users = pgTable('users', {
  id:         uuid('id').primaryKey().defaultRandom(),
  tenantId:   uuid('tenant_id').notNull().references(() => tenants.id, { onDelete: 'cascade' }),
  // The Cognito subject claim. Our join key to the identity provider — never the email,
  // because emails change and are not guaranteed unique across pools.
  cognitoSub: text('cognito_sub').notNull(),
  email:      text('email').notNull(),
  displayName: text('display_name'),
  role:       text('role', { enum: ['owner', 'member', 'viewer'] }).notNull().default('member'),
  createdAt:  timestamp('created_at', { withTimezone: true }).notNull().defaultNow(),
}, (t) => [
  uniqueIndex('users_cognito_sub_key').on(t.cognitoSub),
  index('users_tenant_idx').on(t.tenantId),
]);

export const documents = pgTable('documents', {
  id:        uuid('id').primaryKey().defaultRandom(),
  tenantId:  uuid('tenant_id').notNull().references(() => tenants.id, { onDelete: 'cascade' }),
  uploadedBy: uuid('uploaded_by').notNull().references(() => users.id),

  filename:  text('filename').notNull(),
  mimeType:  text('mime_type').notNull(),
  byteSize:  bigint('byte_size', { mode: 'number' }),
  // sha256 of the object. Our natural dedupe key within a tenant.
  checksum:  text('checksum'),

  s3Bucket:  text('s3_bucket').notNull(),
  s3Key:     text('s3_key').notNull(),
  s3VersionId: text('s3_version_id'),

  status:        documentStatus('status').notNull().default('pending_upload'),
  failureReason: text('failure_reason'),
  pageCount:     integer('page_count'),

  createdAt: timestamp('created_at', { withTimezone: true }).notNull().defaultNow(),
  updatedAt: timestamp('updated_at', { withTimezone: true }).notNull().defaultNow(),
  deletedAt: timestamp('deleted_at', { withTimezone: true }),   // soft delete
}, (t) => [
  uniqueIndex('documents_s3_key_key').on(t.s3Bucket, t.s3Key),
  // The workhorse index: "list this tenant's documents, newest first".
  index('documents_tenant_created_idx').on(t.tenantId, t.createdAt.desc()),
  // Partial index for the operational query "what is stuck?"
  index('documents_active_status_idx').on(t.status)
    .where(sql`status NOT IN ('ready', 'failed', 'quarantined')`),
  index('documents_tenant_checksum_idx').on(t.tenantId, t.checksum),
]);

export const extractions = pgTable('extractions', {
  id:         uuid('id').primaryKey().defaultRandom(),
  documentId: uuid('document_id').notNull().references(() => documents.id, { onDelete: 'cascade' }),
  tenantId:   uuid('tenant_id').notNull(),          // denormalised for RLS + cheap filtering
  engine:     text('engine').notNull(),             // 'textract:analyze' | 'textract:detect'
  textractJobId: text('textract_job_id'),
  fullText:   text('full_text'),
  pages:      jsonb('pages').$type<{ page: number; text: string; confidence: number }[]>(),
  keyValues:  jsonb('key_values').$type<{ key: string; value: string; confidence: number }[]>(),
  tables:     jsonb('tables'),
  avgConfidence: numeric('avg_confidence', { precision: 5, scale: 2 }),
  createdAt:  timestamp('created_at', { withTimezone: true }).notNull().defaultNow(),
}, (t) => [
  index('extractions_document_idx').on(t.documentId),
  uniqueIndex('extractions_job_key').on(t.textractJobId),   // idempotency on Textract callbacks
]);

export const enrichments = pgTable('enrichments', {
  id:          uuid('id').primaryKey().defaultRandom(),
  documentId:  uuid('document_id').notNull().references(() => documents.id, { onDelete: 'cascade' }),
  tenantId:    uuid('tenant_id').notNull(),
  modelId:     text('model_id').notNull(),          // exact model id: reproducibility matters
  promptVersion: text('prompt_version').notNull(),  // so you can A/B and roll back prompts
  class:       docClass('class'),
  classConfidence: numeric('class_confidence', { precision: 4, scale: 3 }),
  summary:     text('summary'),
  fields:      jsonb('fields'),                     // model-extracted structured fields
  inputTokens:  integer('input_tokens'),
  outputTokens: integer('output_tokens'),
  latencyMs:   integer('latency_ms'),
  createdAt:   timestamp('created_at', { withTimezone: true }).notNull().defaultNow(),
}, (t) => [index('enrichments_document_idx').on(t.documentId)]);

export const entities = pgTable('entities', {
  id:         uuid('id').primaryKey().defaultRandom(),
  documentId: uuid('document_id').notNull().references(() => documents.id, { onDelete: 'cascade' }),
  tenantId:   uuid('tenant_id').notNull(),
  type:       text('type').notNull(),               // 'total_amount' | 'due_date' | 'counterparty' ...
  valueText:  text('value_text'),
  valueNumeric: numeric('value_numeric'),
  valueDate:  timestamp('value_date', { withTimezone: true }),
  confidence: numeric('confidence', { precision: 4, scale: 3 }),
  sourcePage: integer('source_page'),
}, (t) => [
  index('entities_doc_idx').on(t.documentId),
  index('entities_tenant_type_idx').on(t.tenantId, t.type),
]);

export const docChunks = pgTable('doc_chunks', {
  id:         uuid('id').primaryKey().defaultRandom(),
  documentId: uuid('document_id').notNull().references(() => documents.id, { onDelete: 'cascade' }),
  tenantId:   uuid('tenant_id').notNull(),
  ordinal:    integer('ordinal').notNull(),
  content:    text('content').notNull(),
  tokenCount: integer('token_count'),
  // Titan Text Embeddings V2 emits 1024 dimensions by default.
  embedding:  vector('embedding', { dimensions: 1024 }),
  createdAt:  timestamp('created_at', { withTimezone: true }).notNull().defaultNow(),
}, (t) => [
  uniqueIndex('doc_chunks_doc_ordinal_key').on(t.documentId, t.ordinal),
  index('doc_chunks_tenant_idx').on(t.tenantId),
]);

/** Idempotency ledger: one row per (job type, dedupe key). Insert wins the race. */
export const processingJobs = pgTable('processing_jobs', {
  jobType:    text('job_type').notNull(),
  dedupeKey:  text('dedupe_key').notNull(),
  documentId: uuid('document_id').references(() => documents.id, { onDelete: 'cascade' }),
  attempts:   integer('attempts').notNull().default(1),
  succeededAt: timestamp('succeeded_at', { withTimezone: true }),
  lastError:  text('last_error'),
  createdAt:  timestamp('created_at', { withTimezone: true }).notNull().defaultNow(),
}, (t) => [primaryKey({ columns: [t.jobType, t.dedupeKey] })]);

export const auditLog = pgTable('audit_log', {
  id:         bigint('id', { mode: 'number' }).primaryKey().generatedAlwaysAsIdentity(),
  tenantId:   uuid('tenant_id').notNull(),
  actor:      text('actor').notNull(),              // 'user:' | 'system:ingest-worker'
  action:     text('action').notNull(),             // 'document.status_changed'
  subjectId:  uuid('subject_id'),
  detail:     jsonb('detail'),
  at:         timestamp('at', { withTimezone: true }).notNull().defaultNow(),
}, (t) => [index('audit_tenant_at_idx').on(t.tenantId, t.at.desc())]);

export const documentsRelations = relations(documents, ({ one, many }) => ({
  tenant:      one(tenants,  { fields: [documents.tenantId],   references: [tenants.id] }),
  uploader:    one(users,    { fields: [documents.uploadedBy], references: [users.id] }),
  extractions: many(extractions),
  enrichments: many(enrichments),
  entities:    many(entities),
  chunks:      many(docChunks),
}));

    Four schema decisions worth defending

    DecisionReasoningCost of the alternative
    tenant_id denormalised onto every child tableLets a query filter by tenant without joining up the chain, and makes row-level security policies trivial and cheap.Joining doc_chunks → documents on every vector search hurts, and RLS on a joined column is painful.
    UUID primary keys (v4 via gen_random_uuid())IDs are generated client-side or in Lambda without a round-trip; no sequential enumeration of another tenant's documents.Serial IDs leak volume and enable ID-guessing. Downside acknowledged: random UUIDs fragment B-tree inserts. UUIDv7 would be better for insert locality — a good thing to mention.
    Soft delete (deleted_at) rather than DELETEDocuments are business records; users ask for undo; regulators ask for provenance.Hard deletes make GDPR erasure easier but accidental loss permanent. We handle erasure explicitly in Sprint 11.
    jsonb for Textract output, columns for what we queryTextract's block structure is deep and changes; storing it raw preserves fidelity while promoted columns (avg_confidence, page_count) stay indexable.Fully normalising Textract blocks means dozens of tables and a migration every time AWS adds a block type.

    §2bFundamentals: ORMs, query builders and migration tools

    Three categories of tool, often confused, doing different jobs. Understanding the split is why this project uses two libraries where many tutorials use one.

    • A full ORM (Hibernate, older TypeORM, Django's ORM) maps rows to objects and manages their identity and lifecycle for you. It hides SQL. That is comfortable at first and expensive later, because you eventually need to reason about the SQL it generates and you have lost direct access to it.
    • A query builder with types (Drizzle, Kysely) gives you SQL semantics with TypeScript checking. You still write joins and where clauses; the library checks that the columns exist and infers the shape of the result. There is no hidden lazy loading and no surprise N+1 query.
    • A migration tool (node-pg-migrate, Flyway, Liquibase) does one thing: apply ordered, recorded schema changes to a database, with a lock so two runners cannot collide, and a record of which migrations have run.

    Drizzle can generate migrations from your schema definition, which is convenient in development. It is the wrong shape for deploying to a shared database, because the generated file is derived from a diff rather than authored by you, and reviewing a diff of a diff is unpleasant. So this project takes the typed query builder from Drizzle and the migration discipline from node-pg-migrate, and accepts one cost: the schema is declared twice, and a check in CI verifies the two agree.

    Connection pooling, and why it matters more than you expect

    Every Postgres connection is a separate operating-system process on the server with its own memory. A db.t4g.micro allows on the order of 80 connections. That number, not CPU, is usually what breaks first.

    A pool keeps a small number of connections open and lends them to requests. Ten connections can serve hundreds of requests per second, because each request holds a connection for only a few milliseconds. Two consequences shape the design of this system:

    • A long-lived server is the ideal pool holder. The Fargate API opens ten connections at boot and reuses them, which is one reason the API is a container rather than a Lambda.
    • Serverless breaks the assumption. A hundred concurrent Lambdas each want their own connection, and there is no shared pool between them. That is the problem RDS Proxy solves, by holding the real pool outside your functions. At portfolio scale the Lambda concurrency cap does the same job for free, which is why Sprint 04 sets one.

    The number to remember: pool size per instance, multiplied by number of instances, must stay comfortably under max_connections, leaving room for migrations, your own psql session and the monitoring agent.

    §3Migrations with node-pg-migrate

    Drizzle can generate SQL, but we use node-pg-migrate as the source of truth for schema changes. The reason is operational: node-pg-migrate gives you numbered, reversible, transaction-wrapped migrations with a lock, which is exactly what you want running from CI against a shared database. Drizzle stays as the typed query layer.

    JavaScriptpackages/db/migrations/1700000000000_init.cjs
    /* eslint-disable camelcase */
exports.shorthands = undefined;

exports.up = async (pgm) => {
  // pgcrypto: gen_random_uuid(). pgvector: the vector type + HNSW index.
  pgm.createExtension('pgcrypto', { ifNotExists: true });
  pgm.createExtension('vector',   { ifNotExists: true });

  pgm.createType('document_status', [
    'pending_upload', 'uploaded', 'extracting', 'extracted',
    'enriching', 'ready', 'failed', 'quarantined',
  ]);
  pgm.createType('doc_class', [
    'invoice', 'contract', 'receipt', 'statement', 'identity', 'other',
  ]);

  pgm.createTable('tenants', {
    id:          { type: 'uuid', primaryKey: true, default: pgm.func('gen_random_uuid()') },
    name:        { type: 'text', notNull: true },
    slug:        { type: 'text', notNull: true, unique: true },
    quota_bytes: { type: 'bigint', notNull: true, default: 5368709120 },
    settings:    { type: 'jsonb', notNull: true,
                   default: pgm.func(`'{"autoEnrich":true,"retentionDays":365}'::jsonb`) },
    created_at:  { type: 'timestamptz', notNull: true, default: pgm.func('now()') },
  });

  pgm.createTable('users', {
    id:           { type: 'uuid', primaryKey: true, default: pgm.func('gen_random_uuid()') },
    tenant_id:    { type: 'uuid', notNull: true,
                    references: 'tenants', onDelete: 'CASCADE' },
    cognito_sub:  { type: 'text', notNull: true, unique: true },
    email:        { type: 'text', notNull: true },
    display_name: { type: 'text' },
    role:         { type: 'text', notNull: true, default: 'member' },
    created_at:   { type: 'timestamptz', notNull: true, default: pgm.func('now()') },
  });
  pgm.addConstraint('users', 'users_role_check',
    "CHECK (role IN ('owner','member','viewer'))");
  pgm.createIndex('users', 'tenant_id');

  pgm.createTable('documents', {
    id:             { type: 'uuid', primaryKey: true, default: pgm.func('gen_random_uuid()') },
    tenant_id:      { type: 'uuid', notNull: true, references: 'tenants', onDelete: 'CASCADE' },
    uploaded_by:    { type: 'uuid', notNull: true, references: 'users' },
    filename:       { type: 'text', notNull: true },
    mime_type:      { type: 'text', notNull: true },
    byte_size:      { type: 'bigint' },
    checksum:       { type: 'text' },
    s3_bucket:      { type: 'text', notNull: true },
    s3_key:         { type: 'text', notNull: true },
    s3_version_id:  { type: 'text' },
    status:         { type: 'document_status', notNull: true, default: 'pending_upload' },
    failure_reason: { type: 'text' },
    page_count:     { type: 'integer' },
    created_at:     { type: 'timestamptz', notNull: true, default: pgm.func('now()') },
    updated_at:     { type: 'timestamptz', notNull: true, default: pgm.func('now()') },
    deleted_at:     { type: 'timestamptz' },
  });
  pgm.addConstraint('documents', 'documents_s3_key_key', { unique: ['s3_bucket', 's3_key'] });
  pgm.sql(`CREATE INDEX documents_tenant_created_idx
             ON documents (tenant_id, created_at DESC)`);
  pgm.sql(`CREATE INDEX documents_active_status_idx ON documents (status)
             WHERE status NOT IN ('ready','failed','quarantined')`);
  pgm.sql(`CREATE INDEX documents_tenant_checksum_idx ON documents (tenant_id, checksum)`);

  // ... extractions, enrichments, entities, doc_chunks, processing_jobs, audit_log
  // follow the same shape; full file in the repo.

  pgm.createTable('processing_jobs', {
    job_type:     { type: 'text', notNull: true },
    dedupe_key:   { type: 'text', notNull: true },
    document_id:  { type: 'uuid', references: 'documents', onDelete: 'CASCADE' },
    attempts:     { type: 'integer', notNull: true, default: 1 },
    succeeded_at: { type: 'timestamptz' },
    last_error:   { type: 'text' },
    created_at:   { type: 'timestamptz', notNull: true, default: pgm.func('now()') },
  });
  pgm.addConstraint('processing_jobs', 'processing_jobs_pkey',
    { primaryKey: ['job_type', 'dedupe_key'] });

  // updated_at maintenance in the database, so no ORM can forget it
  pgm.sql(`
    CREATE OR REPLACE FUNCTION touch_updated_at() RETURNS trigger AS $$
    BEGIN NEW.updated_at = now(); RETURN NEW; END;
    $$ LANGUAGE plpgsql;
    CREATE TRIGGER documents_touch BEFORE UPDATE ON documents
      FOR EACH ROW EXECUTE FUNCTION touch_updated_at();
  `);
};

exports.down = async (pgm) => {
  pgm.dropTable('processing_jobs');
  pgm.dropTable('documents', { cascade: true });
  pgm.dropTable('users');
  pgm.dropTable('tenants');
  pgm.dropType('doc_class');
  pgm.dropType('document_status');
  pgm.sql('DROP FUNCTION IF EXISTS touch_updated_at()');
};
    The migration rule that saves you

    Migrations must be backward compatible with the currently running app version, because for a minute or two during a rolling ECS deploy, old and new tasks run against the same database. That means: add columns nullable or with a default; never rename in one step (add new → dual-write → backfill → switch reads → drop old, across separate deploys); never drop a column in the same release that stops using it. Create indexes with CONCURRENTLY on large tables — and note that CONCURRENTLY cannot run inside a transaction, so that migration needs module.exports.up.noTransaction = true in node-pg-migrate.

    The vector index is deliberately a second migration, because HNSW build time grows with row count and you want it separately controllable:

    JavaScriptpackages/db/migrations/1700000001000_vector_index.cjs
    exports.up = (pgm) => {
  // HNSW: fast approximate nearest-neighbour, good recall, higher build cost than IVFFlat
  // but no need to pick a list count or retrain as data grows.
  // vector_cosine_ops because Titan embeddings are compared by cosine similarity.
  pgm.sql(`
    CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY doc_chunks_embedding_hnsw
      ON doc_chunks USING hnsw (embedding vector_cosine_ops)
      WITH (m = 16, ef_construction = 64)
  `);
};
exports.down = (pgm) => { pgm.sql('DROP INDEX CONCURRENTLY doc_chunks_embedding_hnsw'); };
exports.up.noTransaction = true;
exports.down.noTransaction = true;

    §4The Fastify API

    Fastify because it is fast, has first-class schema validation and serialisation, a clean plugin/encapsulation model, and structured logging built in via pino. Structure it so that HTTP concerns, business logic, and data access are separable — that's what makes it testable.

    TypeScriptapps/api/src/app.ts
    import Fastify, { type FastifyInstance } from 'fastify';
import sensible from '@fastify/sensible';
import helmet from '@fastify/helmet';
import cors from '@fastify/cors';
import rateLimit from '@fastify/rate-limit';
import {
  serializerCompiler, validatorCompiler, type ZodTypeProvider,
} from 'fastify-type-provider-zod';
import { randomUUID } from 'node:crypto';

import { config } from './config.js';
import { dbPlugin } from './plugins/db.js';
import { authPlugin } from './plugins/auth.js';
import { awsPlugin } from './plugins/aws.js';
import { documentRoutes } from './routes/documents.js';
import { searchRoutes } from './routes/search.js';
import { healthRoutes } from './routes/health.js';

export async function buildApp(): Promise<FastifyInstance> {
  const app = Fastify({
    logger: {
      level: config.LOG_LEVEL,
      // One JSON object per line -> CloudWatch Logs Insights can query fields directly.
      formatters: { level: (label) => ({ level: label }) },
      // Never log a token, a cookie, or a presigned URL (they contain a signature).
      redact: {
        paths: ['req.headers.authorization', 'req.headers.cookie', 'res.headers["set-cookie"]', '*.uploadUrl'],
        censor: '[redacted]',
      },
      serializers: {
        req: (r) => ({ method: r.method, url: r.url, requestId: r.id }),
      },
    },
    // Trust the ALB's X-Forwarded-* headers so req.ip is the real client.
    trustProxy: true,
    // Correlation id: reuse the ALB trace header if present so one id spans
    // load balancer access logs, app logs, and X-Ray.
    genReqId: (req) =>
      (req.headers['x-amzn-trace-id'] as string | undefined) ??
      (req.headers['x-request-id'] as string | undefined) ??
      randomUUID(),
    bodyLimit: 1_048_576,           // 1 MB: file bytes never go through the API
    requestTimeout: 30_000,
  }).withTypeProvider<ZodTypeProvider>();

  app.setValidatorCompiler(validatorCompiler);
  app.setSerializerCompiler(serializerCompiler);

  await app.register(helmet, { contentSecurityPolicy: false });
  await app.register(cors, { origin: config.CORS_ORIGINS, credentials: true });
  await app.register(sensible);
  await app.register(rateLimit, { max: 300, timeWindow: '1 minute' });

  await app.register(dbPlugin);
  await app.register(awsPlugin);
  await app.register(authPlugin);

  await app.register(healthRoutes);
  await app.register(documentRoutes, { prefix: '/v1/documents' });
  await app.register(searchRoutes,   { prefix: '/v1/search' });

  // One error shape for the whole API. Never leak a stack trace or a driver message.
  app.setErrorHandler((err, req, reply) => {
    const status = err.statusCode ?? 500;
    if (status >= 500) {
      req.log.error({ err, requestId: req.id }, 'unhandled request error');
    } else {
      req.log.warn({ err: { message: err.message, code: err.code }, requestId: req.id }, 'request rejected');
    }
    reply.status(status).send({
      error: {
        code: err.code ?? (status >= 500 ? 'internal_error' : 'bad_request'),
        message: status >= 500 ? 'An internal error occurred.' : err.message,
        requestId: req.id,
      },
    });
  });

  return app;
}

    Config is validated at boot. A container that is missing an environment variable should refuse to start, loudly, rather than fail on the first request at 3am:

    TypeScriptapps/api/src/config.ts
    import { z } from 'zod';

const schema = z.object({
  NODE_ENV:  z.enum(['development', 'test', 'production']).default('development'),
  PORT:      z.coerce.number().int().default(8080),
  LOG_LEVEL: z.enum(['fatal','error','warn','info','debug','trace']).default('info'),

  DATABASE_URL: z.string().url(),
  DB_POOL_MAX:  z.coerce.number().int().min(1).max(50).default(10),

  AWS_REGION:      z.string().default('eu-west-1'),
  RAW_BUCKET:      z.string().min(3),
  DERIVED_BUCKET:  z.string().min(3),
  KMS_KEY_ID:      z.string().optional(),
  INGEST_QUEUE_URL: z.string().url().optional(),

  COGNITO_USER_POOL_ID: z.string().min(1),
  COGNITO_CLIENT_ID:    z.string().min(1),

  BEDROCK_TEXT_MODEL_ID:  z.string().default('eu.anthropic.claude-sonnet-4-5-20250929-v1:0'),
  BEDROCK_EMBED_MODEL_ID: z.string().default('amazon.titan-embed-text-v2:0'),

  // Local development only: point the SDK at LocalStack.
  AWS_ENDPOINT_URL: z.string().url().optional(),

  CORS_ORIGINS: z.string().default('http://localhost:5173')
    .transform((s) => s.split(',').map((x) => x.trim())),

  MAX_UPLOAD_BYTES: z.coerce.number().int().default(52_428_800),   // 50 MB
});

const parsed = schema.safeParse(process.env);
if (!parsed.success) {
  console.error('Invalid configuration:', z.treeifyError(parsed.error));
  process.exit(1);
}
export const config = parsed.data;
export type Config = typeof config;

    Tenant context: isolation enforced twice

    Application-level scoping is necessary but a single forgotten WHERE tenant_id = ? is a cross-tenant data leak. So we also run every request's queries inside a transaction that sets a Postgres session variable, and let row-level security backstop us.

    TypeScriptapps/api/src/plugins/db.ts
    import fp from 'fastify-plugin';
import { Pool } from 'pg';
import { drizzle, type NodePgDatabase } from 'drizzle-orm/node-postgres';
import { sql } from 'drizzle-orm';
import * as schema from '@idp/db/schema';
import { config } from '../config.js';

export type Db = NodePgDatabase<typeof schema>;

declare module 'fastify' {
  interface FastifyInstance {
    db: Db;
    /** Run a callback in a transaction scoped to one tenant (RLS enforced). */
    withTenant: <T>(tenantId: string, fn: (tx: Db) => Promise<T>) => Promise<T>;
  }
}

export const dbPlugin = fp(async (app) => {
  const pool = new Pool({
    connectionString: config.DATABASE_URL,
    max: config.DB_POOL_MAX,
    idleTimeoutMillis: 30_000,
    connectionTimeoutMillis: 5_000,
    // RDS requires TLS; in production verify against the RDS CA bundle.
    ssl: config.NODE_ENV === 'production' ? { rejectUnauthorized: true } : undefined,
  });

  pool.on('error', (err) => app.log.error({ err }, 'idle pg client error'));

  const db = drizzle(pool, { schema });

  app.decorate('db', db);
  app.decorate('withTenant', async (tenantId, fn) =>
    db.transaction(async (tx) => {
      // set_config(..., true) => scoped to this transaction only, so a pooled
      // connection can never carry one tenant's context into another's request.
      await tx.execute(sql`SELECT set_config('app.tenant_id', ${tenantId}, true)`);
      return fn(tx as unknown as Db);
    }),
  );

  app.addHook('onClose', async () => { await pool.end(); });
});
    SQLmigration: row-level security
    -- Run as the migration role (which is the table owner and BYPASSRLS),
-- then have the application connect as a *different*, non-owner role.
ALTER TABLE documents   ENABLE ROW LEVEL SECURITY;
ALTER TABLE extractions ENABLE ROW LEVEL SECURITY;
ALTER TABLE enrichments ENABLE ROW LEVEL SECURITY;
ALTER TABLE entities    ENABLE ROW LEVEL SECURITY;
ALTER TABLE doc_chunks  ENABLE ROW LEVEL SECURITY;

CREATE POLICY tenant_isolation ON documents
  USING      (tenant_id = current_setting('app.tenant_id', true)::uuid)
  WITH CHECK (tenant_id = current_setting('app.tenant_id', true)::uuid);
-- repeat per table

-- The app role owns nothing and cannot bypass policies.
CREATE ROLE idp_app LOGIN PASSWORD :'app_password' NOBYPASSRLS;
GRANT SELECT, INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE ON ALL TABLES IN SCHEMA public TO idp_app;
GRANT USAGE ON SCHEMA public TO idp_app;
    Read the fine print

    current_setting('app.tenant_id', true) returns NULL when unset, and tenant_id = NULL is never true — so a query that forgets to set the tenant returns zero rows rather than everything. The query fails closed, which is the behaviour you want from a security control. Note that a table's owner bypasses RLS by default, which is why the app must connect as a separate non-owner role; this is the single most common way people implement RLS and get no protection at all.

    A representative route

    TypeScriptapps/api/src/routes/documents.ts (excerpt)
    import type { FastifyPluginAsyncZod } from 'fastify-type-provider-zod';
import { z } from 'zod';
import { and, desc, eq, isNull, lt } from 'drizzle-orm';
import { documents } from '@idp/db/schema';

const DocumentDto = z.object({
  id: z.string().uuid(),
  filename: z.string(),
  mimeType: z.string(),
  byteSize: z.number().nullable(),
  status: z.string(),
  pageCount: z.number().nullable(),
  createdAt: z.string().datetime(),
});

export const documentRoutes: FastifyPluginAsyncZod = async (app) => {
  // Every route in this plugin requires a valid JWT (see plugins/auth.ts)
  app.addHook('preHandler', app.requireAuth);

  app.get('/', {
    schema: {
      querystring: z.object({
        limit:  z.coerce.number().int().min(1).max(100).default(25),
        cursor: z.string().datetime().optional(),   // keyset pagination, not OFFSET
        status: z.string().optional(),
      }),
      response: {
        200: z.object({ items: z.array(DocumentDto), nextCursor: z.string().datetime().nullable() }),
      },
    },
  }, async (req) => {
    const { limit, cursor, status } = req.query;

    const rows = await app.withTenant(req.tenant.id, (tx) =>
      tx.select().from(documents).where(and(
        eq(documents.tenantId, req.tenant.id),         // belt
        isNull(documents.deletedAt),                    // ...and braces (RLS is the belt)
        cursor ? lt(documents.createdAt, new Date(cursor)) : undefined,
        status ? eq(documents.status, status as never)  : undefined,
      ))
      .orderBy(desc(documents.createdAt))
      .limit(limit + 1),
    );

    const page = rows.slice(0, limit);
    return {
      items: page.map(toDto),
      // Keyset pagination: O(1) regardless of depth, and stable under inserts.
      nextCursor: rows.length > limit ? page.at(-1)!.createdAt.toISOString() : null,
    };
  });

  app.get('/:id', {
    schema: {
      params: z.object({ id: z.string().uuid() }),
      response: { 200: DocumentDto },
    },
  }, async (req) => {
    const [doc] = await app.withTenant(req.tenant.id, (tx) =>
      tx.select().from(documents)
        .where(and(eq(documents.id, req.params.id), isNull(documents.deletedAt)))
        .limit(1),
    );
    // RLS already made another tenant's document invisible, so this is a
    // genuine 404 rather than a 403 — and it doesn't confirm the id exists.
    if (!doc) throw app.httpErrors.notFound('Document not found');
    return toDto(doc);
  });
};

function toDto(d: typeof documents.$inferSelect) {
  return {
    id: d.id, filename: d.filename, mimeType: d.mimeType, byteSize: d.byteSize,
    status: d.status, pageCount: d.pageCount, createdAt: d.createdAt.toISOString(),
  };
}

    §5Local stack: Docker Compose

    Two containers: Postgres with pgvector, and LocalStack to emulate S3, SQS and SNS. This means the whole ingest pipeline is developable offline. LocalStack does not emulate Textract or Bedrock usefully, so those calls go to real AWS even in development — cheap, and worth being explicit about.

    YAMLdocker-compose.yml
    services:
  db:
    # Includes pgvector, so no extension compilation locally.
    image: pgvector/pgvector:pg16
    environment:
      POSTGRES_USER: idp
      POSTGRES_PASSWORD: idp_local_dev
      POSTGRES_DB: idp
    ports: ['5432:5432']
    volumes: ['pgdata:/var/lib/postgresql/data']
    healthcheck:
      test: ['CMD-SHELL', 'pg_isready -U idp -d idp']
      interval: 5s
      timeout: 3s
      retries: 12

  localstack:
    image: localstack/localstack:3
    environment:
      SERVICES: s3,sqs,sns,secretsmanager,events
      DEBUG: 0
      # Real IAM enforcement is off by default; keep it that way locally, but
      # never treat "it worked in LocalStack" as proof your IAM policy is right.
    ports: ['4566:4566']
    volumes: ['./scripts/localstack-init.sh:/etc/localstack/init/ready.d/init.sh']
    healthcheck:
      test: ['CMD-SHELL', 'curl -sf http://localhost:4566/_localstack/health | grep -q "\"s3\": \"available\""']
      interval: 5s
      retries: 20

volumes:
  pgdata:
    Shellscripts/localstack-init.sh
    #!/usr/bin/env bash
set -euo pipefail
export AWS_DEFAULT_REGION=eu-west-1
alias al="awslocal"

awslocal s3 mb s3://idp-raw-local
awslocal s3 mb s3://idp-derived-local
awslocal s3api put-bucket-versioning --bucket idp-raw-local \
  --versioning-configuration Status=Enabled

awslocal sqs create-queue --queue-name idp-ingest-dlq-local
DLQ_ARN=$(awslocal sqs get-queue-attributes \
  --queue-url http://localhost:4566/000000000000/idp-ingest-dlq-local \
  --attribute-names QueueArn --query 'Attributes.QueueArn' --output text)

awslocal sqs create-queue --queue-name idp-ingest-local --attributes "$(cat <<JSON
{
  "VisibilityTimeout": "300",
  "RedrivePolicy": "{\"deadLetterTargetArn\":\"${DLQ_ARN}\",\"maxReceiveCount\":\"3\"}"
}
JSON
)"

# S3 -> SQS notification, so the local flow matches the deployed one closely enough
awslocal s3api put-bucket-notification-configuration --bucket idp-raw-local \
  --notification-configuration "$(cat <<JSON
{
  "QueueConfigurations": [{
    "QueueArn": "arn:aws:sqs:eu-west-1:000000000000:idp-ingest-local",
    "Events": ["s3:ObjectCreated:*"]
  }]
}
JSON
)"
echo "LocalStack ready."
