    §6Testing strategy

    Three layers, with a deliberate bias toward the middle one. Unit tests for pure logic, integration tests against a real Postgres in a container, and a thin end-to-end layer later.

    LayerToolWhat it coversSpeed
    UnitVitestState machine, chunking, prompt builders, DTO mappingms
    IntegrationVitest + TestcontainersMigrations, RLS enforcement, real SQL, route handlers via app.inject()seconds
    ContractVitest + aws-sdk-client-mockWorker logic with AWS calls stubbed at the SDK command levelms
    End to endPlaywright (Sprint 10)Upload → processed → searchable, against a deployed dev envminutes
    TypeScriptapps/api/test/tenant-isolation.it.test.ts
    import { describe, it, expect, beforeAll, afterAll } from 'vitest';
import { PostgreSqlContainer, type StartedPostgreSqlContainer } from '@testcontainers/postgresql';
import { execa } from 'execa';
import { buildApp } from '../src/app.js';
import type { FastifyInstance } from 'fastify';

let pg: StartedPostgreSqlContainer;
let app: FastifyInstance;

beforeAll(async () => {
  pg = await new PostgreSqlContainer('pgvector/pgvector:pg16')
    .withDatabase('idp').withUsername('idp').withPassword('test')
    .start();

  process.env.DATABASE_URL = pg.getConnectionUri();
  // Migrations run against the throwaway database: this test also proves the
  // migrations themselves work from empty, every single time.
  await execa('node-pg-migrate', ['up', '-m', 'packages/db/migrations'], {
    env: { DATABASE_URL: process.env.DATABASE_URL },
  });

  app = await buildApp();
  await app.ready();
}, 120_000);

afterAll(async () => { await app?.close(); await pg?.stop(); });

describe('tenant isolation', () => {
  it('returns zero rows when no tenant context is set', async () => {
    const { tenantA, docA } = await seedTwoTenants(app);
    // Deliberately query outside withTenant(): RLS must hide everything.
    const rows = await app.db.execute(`SELECT id FROM documents`);
    expect(rows.rows).toHaveLength(0);
    expect(docA).toBeDefined();
    expect(tenantA).toBeDefined();
  });

  it('never exposes tenant B documents to tenant A', async () => {
    const { tenantA, docB } = await seedTwoTenants(app);
    const res = await app.inject({
      method: 'GET',
      url: `/v1/documents/${docB.id}`,
      headers: { authorization: `Bearer ${signTestJwt({ tenantId: tenantA.id })}` },
    });
    expect(res.statusCode).toBe(404);          // not 403 — do not confirm existence
  });

  it('rejects an illegal status transition', async () => {
    const { docA } = await seedTwoTenants(app);
    await expect(
      transitionDocument(app, docA.id, 'ready'),   // from pending_upload
    ).rejects.toThrow(/Illegal document status transition/);
  });
});
    How to describe this

    "My integration tests run migrations from empty against a throwaway Postgres container on every CI run, so a broken migration fails the build rather than production. And I have an explicit test that queries without tenant context and asserts zero rows — that's the test that proves the isolation control actually works, rather than assuming it."

    §7AWS concepts introduced here

    • RDS vs Aurora vs DynamoDB. We chose RDS for PostgreSQL: relational data, joins, ACID transactions, and pgvector for embeddings all in one place. Aurora Serverless v2 is the natural upgrade if traffic is spiky (it scales capacity in ~0.5 ACU steps, though it never truly hits zero and has a higher floor cost). DynamoDB would be wrong here — our access patterns include ad-hoc filtering and vector similarity, which is exactly what a document-key store is bad at.
    • Connection pooling matters more in serverless. A Fargate task holds a warm pool of 10 connections. A hundred concurrent Lambdas each opening their own connection will exhaust max_connections on a small instance. That's why our Lambdas write through short transactions and, at scale, would go via RDS Proxy — mention this before an interviewer asks.
    • Secrets Manager, previewed. Locally the password is in .env.local. In AWS it lives in Secrets Manager with automatic rotation, and the container reads it at boot (Sprint 07).
    • LocalStack's honest limits. Excellent for S3/SQS/SNS shapes; it does not validate IAM the way AWS does, and it doesn't emulate Textract or Bedrock. Use it to iterate on wiring, and use a real dev environment to validate permissions.

    §8Trade-offs decided in this sprint

    DecisionChosenAlternativeReasoning
    Migration toolnode-pg-migratedrizzle-kit push/generateNumbered reversible migrations with an advisory lock are what you want running from CI against a shared database. Drizzle stays as the typed query builder — using each tool for what it's best at is itself a defensible decision.
    Tenant isolationShared schema + tenant_id + RLSSchema-per-tenant; database-per-tenantShared schema scales to thousands of tenants with one migration run and one connection pool. Database-per-tenant gives the hardest isolation and per-tenant restore, at the cost of migration fan-out and connection sprawl. RLS narrows the gap cheaply.
    PaginationKeyset (cursor on created_at)LIMIT/OFFSETOFFSET degrades linearly with depth and skips or repeats rows when data changes mid-scroll. Keyset is O(1) and stable.
    Vector storepgvector in the same RDS instanceOpenSearch Serverless; KendraOne database, one backup, one connection, transactional consistency between a document and its embeddings. Honest limit: pgvector on a small instance is fine to ~1M chunks; past that, or if you need hybrid BM25+vector ranking with rich relevance tuning, OpenSearch starts to pay for itself.
    FrameworkFastifyExpress; NestJSSchema-first validation and serialisation, pino logging, and a plugin encapsulation model that makes testing easy — without NestJS's decorator and DI overhead for a service this size.

    §9Interview questions on this material

    How do you enforce multi-tenant isolation, and how do you prove it works?

    Layered, so no single mistake is a breach. Layer 1 — identity: the tenant comes from a verified JWT claim, never from a request parameter or header the client controls. Layer 2 — application: every query goes through a helper that requires a tenant id; there is no raw pool handle exposed to route code. Layer 3 — database: row-level security policies on every tenant-scoped table, with the app connecting as a non-owner role that cannot bypass them; the tenant id is set per-transaction with set_config(..., true) so a pooled connection can't leak context between requests. Layer 4 — storage: S3 keys are prefixed tenant/<uuid>/… and presigned URLs are scoped to a single key, so even a compromised URL reaches exactly one object.

    Proving it is the part people skip. I have a test that queries the documents table with no tenant context set and asserts zero rows — because current_setting returns NULL and tenant_id = NULL is never true, RLS fails closed. And a test that fetches tenant B's document id with tenant A's token and asserts 404, not 403, so the response doesn't confirm the row exists.

    Why did you choose RDS Postgres over DynamoDB?

    Access patterns drove it. This platform needs: filtered listings with sorting, joins across documents/extractions/entities, aggregate reporting, and vector similarity search. DynamoDB is superb when you know your access patterns up front and they're key-based — single-digit millisecond reads at any scale, no connection management. But ad-hoc filtering means either a table scan or a proliferation of GSIs, and vector search would need a second system entirely.

    Postgres gives me relational integrity, real transactions across documents and their derived data, and pgvector for embeddings in the same engine — one backup, one restore, one consistency model. The costs I accept: I have to manage connections (relevant because Lambda concurrency can exhaust a small instance's max_connections — RDS Proxy is the answer at scale), I have a maintenance window, and vertical scaling has a ceiling. If write volume grew past what a single primary handles, I'd reach for Aurora with read replicas before I'd reach for DynamoDB — the data is relational, and that doesn't change with scale.

    How do you run database migrations safely against a live system?

    The core constraint is that during a rolling deploy, old and new application versions run simultaneously against one database — so every migration must be compatible with the currently deployed code. That gives concrete rules: add columns as nullable or with a default; never rename in a single step (add the new column, dual-write, backfill in batches, switch reads, then drop the old one in a later release); never drop a column in the same release that stops writing it.

    Mechanically: migrations run as a separate step in the pipeline before the new task definition is deployed, as a one-off ECS task inside the VPC using a migration role with DDL rights the application role does not have. node-pg-migrate takes an advisory lock so concurrent runners can't collide. Indexes on large tables are built CONCURRENTLY, which means that migration can't be in a transaction. And every migration is exercised from empty in CI against a Testcontainers Postgres, so "the migration doesn't apply cleanly" is a build failure rather than an outage.

    The failure mode I plan for explicitly: a migration that succeeds but the deploy that follows it fails. Because migrations are backward compatible, the old version keeps working, so rollback is just redeploying the previous image — no schema rollback needed. That's why backward compatibility is the rule rather than relying on down migrations, which are genuinely dangerous under load.

    Your document status is an enum with a transition table. Why bother?

    Because an async pipeline with retries will absolutely try to make illegal transitions, and without a guard you get silent corruption. Concretely: SQS delivers at least once, so a duplicate Textract completion can arrive after enrichment has already finished — without a check, that would drag a ready document back to extracted and re-run enrichment, billing Bedrock twice. With the transition table, that attempt throws, the handler logs it, acknowledges the message, and moves on.

    It also makes the operational surface explicit. failed → uploaded is a documented replay path used by the DLQ redrive runbook. ready → enriching is how I re-process everything when I upgrade the model, without deleting data. And the partial index on non-terminal statuses makes "what's stuck right now?" a millisecond query, which is what the stuck-document CloudWatch alarm is built on.

    What are the trade-offs of pgvector versus a dedicated vector database?

    pgvector's advantage is that it isn't a separate system. A document and its embeddings are written in one transaction, so there's no dual-write inconsistency, no second thing to back up, no second thing to secure, and I can filter by tenant, date, and document class in the same SQL as the similarity search — pre-filtering is where standalone vector stores often get awkward.

    The limits are real. HNSW indexes want to live in memory: at 1024 dimensions, each vector is ~4KB, so a million chunks is ~4GB of vectors plus index overhead — that dictates instance sizing. Index build time grows with rows, and HNSW inserts are more expensive than plain B-tree ones. Approximate search means recall tuning via ef_search, traded against latency. And pgvector alone gives you vector similarity, not hybrid ranking — I combine it with Postgres full-text search and blend the scores, which works well but is less tunable than OpenSearch's relevance framework.

    My switching threshold: past a few million chunks, or when I need sophisticated hybrid relevance tuning, or when vector query load starts competing with transactional load for the same buffer cache. Until then, the operational simplicity wins.

    §10Definition of done

    Sprint 02 · Ground work

    Terraform foundations, S3, KMS and IAM

    First real AWS resources. The goal isn't to create a bucket — it's to establish the infrastructure patterns you'll repeat nine more times: remote state, module boundaries, per-environment roots, mandatory tags, and IAM policies tight enough to survive a security review.

    Time
    1–2 days
    You'll produce
    State backend, S3 buckets, KMS key, IAM roles, tagging strategy
    Running cost
    ~$1/mo KMS key $1 + pennies of S3
    Key idea
    State is the most valuable file you own — protect it accordingly

    §1Requirements

    • Terraform state stored remotely, encrypted, versioned, and locked against concurrent applies.
    • Two S3 buckets: raw (originals, never mutated) and derived (thumbnails, extracted text, exports).
    • Customer-managed KMS key encrypting both buckets, with a key policy scoped to the services that need it.
    • No public access, TLS-only, versioning on, lifecycle rules that reduce storage cost over time.
    • IAM roles for the API and the workers, each with the minimum permissions to do its job.
    • Every resource tagged; every environment reproducible from code.

    §1bFundamentals: what Terraform actually does

    Terraform has a small number of moving parts. Once they click, error messages start making sense.

    The three-way comparison

    Every terraform plan compares three things: your configuration (the .tf files), the state (what Terraform believes it created) and reality (what the AWS API reports right now). The plan is the set of API calls needed to make reality match configuration, and state is what lets Terraform know which real resource each block in your code refers to.

    That framing explains the common confusions:

    • Drift is reality differing from state. Someone changed something in the console. The plan shows it because Terraform refreshes state from the API before comparing.
    • "Resource already exists" means reality has it but state does not. Either it was created outside Terraform, or state was lost. The fix is terraform import, or an import block in newer versions.
    • Losing state does not delete anything. Your infrastructure keeps running. Terraform simply no longer knows it owns it, and the next apply tries to create duplicates. This is why the state bucket is versioned.

    The dependency graph

    Terraform does not run your file top to bottom. It builds a directed graph from the references between resources and walks it, creating independent resources in parallel. When you write subnet_id = aws_subnet.app[0].id, that reference is the dependency declaration, which is why explicit depends_on is rarely needed. You need it only when the dependency is real but invisible to Terraform, such as an IAM policy that must exist before a service can assume a role.

    Providers, and why versions get pinned

    A provider is a plugin that translates Terraform resources into a specific API's calls. The AWS provider is a large program with its own release cycle, and its releases change defaults. A minor provider upgrade genuinely can produce a plan that wants to replace a resource. Pin with ~>, commit the lock file, and upgrade deliberately while reading the changelog.

    Read the plan properly

    Plan output uses symbols that carry very different consequences. + create. - destroy. ~ update in place, which is safe. -/+ destroy then create, which for a database or a bucket means data loss. Terraform prints # forces replacement next to the attribute responsible. Search for that string in every plan before applying, and understand why it appeared.

    §2Repository and state layout

    The bootstrap problem: Terraform needs an S3 bucket to store state, but you want to create that bucket with Terraform. Solution — a tiny bootstrap root module with local state, applied once, committed, then never touched.

    Textinfra/ layout
    infra/
├─ bootstrap/                  # local state, run once, creates the state bucket
│  └─ main.tf
├─ modules/                    # no provider blocks, no backends: reusable only
│  ├─ storage/                 # S3 + KMS + lifecycle + policies
│  ├─ network/                 # VPC, subnets, endpoints        (Sprint 07)
│  ├─ database/                # RDS + Secrets Manager           (Sprint 07)
│  ├─ queue/                   # SQS + DLQ + EventBridge rule    (Sprint 04)
│  ├─ ecs-service/             # cluster, task def, service, ALB (Sprint 07)
│  ├─ lambda-worker/           # function, role, event source    (Sprint 04)
│  ├─ identity/                # Cognito                         (Sprint 03)
│  └─ observability/           # alarms, dashboards, SNS         (Sprint 08)
└─ envs/
   ├─ dev/                     # root module: backend + provider + module calls
   │  ├─ backend.tf
   │  ├─ main.tf
   │  ├─ variables.tf
   │  └─ terraform.tfvars
   └─ prod/
    Why root-per-environment, not workspaces

    Terraform workspaces share one root module and one backend key prefix, so dev and prod are one terraform apply typo apart and cannot differ structurally (dev single-AZ, prod Multi-AZ) without conditionals everywhere. Separate root directories give each environment its own state file, its own variables, and its own plan — and let dev legitimately be a smaller shape rather than a flag-riddled version of prod. The cost is a little duplication in the root modules, which is exactly where duplication is cheapest.

    HCLinfra/bootstrap/main.tf
    terraform {
  required_version = ">= 1.10"
  required_providers {
    aws = { source = "hashicorp/aws", version = "~> 5.70" }
  }
  # Deliberately local state. Commit terraform.tfstate for this module only —
  # it contains no secrets, and it's the one place the chicken-and-egg is unavoidable.
}

provider "aws" {
  region = var.region
  default_tags {
    tags = {
      Project   = "idp-platform"
      ManagedBy = "terraform"
      Component = "bootstrap"
    }
  }
}

variable "region" {
  type    = string
  default = "eu-west-1"
}

variable "project" {
  type    = string
  default = "idp"
}

data "aws_caller_identity" "current" {}

resource "aws_s3_bucket" "tfstate" {
  bucket = "${var.project}-tfstate-${data.aws_caller_identity.current.account_id}"
  # Refuse to delete a bucket holding state, even if someone removes the resource.
  lifecycle { prevent_destroy = true }
}

# Versioning is the actual disaster-recovery mechanism for state: a corrupted or
# truncated state file is recoverable by restoring the previous object version.
resource "aws_s3_bucket_versioning" "tfstate" {
  bucket = aws_s3_bucket.tfstate.id
  versioning_configuration { status = "Enabled" }
}

resource "aws_s3_bucket_server_side_encryption_configuration" "tfstate" {
  bucket = aws_s3_bucket.tfstate.id
  rule {
    apply_server_side_encryption_by_default { sse_algorithm = "AES256" }
    bucket_key_enabled = true
  }
}

resource "aws_s3_bucket_public_access_block" "tfstate" {
  bucket                  = aws_s3_bucket.tfstate.id
  block_public_acls       = true
  block_public_policy     = true
  ignore_public_acls      = true
  restrict_public_buckets = true
}

# State contains resource attributes — including, sometimes, sensitive values.
# Deny any request that isn't over TLS.
resource "aws_s3_bucket_policy" "tfstate_tls_only" {
  bucket = aws_s3_bucket.tfstate.id
  policy = jsonencode({
    Version = "2012-10-17"
    Statement = [{
      Sid       = "DenyInsecureTransport"
      Effect    = "Deny"
      Principal = "*"
      Action    = "s3:*"
      Resource  = [aws_s3_bucket.tfstate.arn, "${aws_s3_bucket.tfstate.arn}/*"]
      Condition = { Bool = { "aws:SecureTransport" = "false" } }
    }]
  })
}

# Expire noncurrent state versions so the bucket doesn't grow without bound,
# but keep enough history to recover from a bad apply.
resource "aws_s3_bucket_lifecycle_configuration" "tfstate" {
  bucket = aws_s3_bucket.tfstate.id
  rule {
    id     = "expire-old-state-versions"
    status = "Enabled"
    filter {}
    noncurrent_version_expiration { noncurrent_days = 90 }
    abort_incomplete_multipart_upload { days_after_initiation = 7 }
  }
}

output "state_bucket" { value = aws_s3_bucket.tfstate.id }
    HCLinfra/envs/dev/backend.tf
    terraform {
  required_version = ">= 1.10"
  required_providers {
    aws    = { source = "hashicorp/aws",    version = "~> 5.70" }
    random = { source = "hashicorp/random", version = "~> 3.6" }
  }

  backend "s3" {
    bucket = "idp-tfstate-123456789012"
    key    = "envs/dev/terraform.tfstate"
    region = "eu-west-1"
    encrypt = true

    # Terraform 1.10+ locks state with a lockfile object in the same bucket.
    # This replaces the old DynamoDB lock table — one fewer resource to manage.
    use_lockfile = true
  }
}
    If you're on Terraform < 1.10

    use_lockfile won't exist. Use the classic pattern instead: create a DynamoDB table with partition key LockID (string) and set dynamodb_table = "idp-tflock" in the backend block. Know both — interviewers who last touched Terraform in 2023 will expect the DynamoDB answer, and being able to say "that's the legacy approach; S3 native locking supersedes it" reads well.

    §3The storage module

    This module owns the KMS key and both buckets. It's the template for every module that follows: variables in, hardened resources, outputs that other modules consume.

    HCLinfra/modules/storage/main.tf
    locals {
  name = "${var.project}-${var.environment}"
}

data "aws_caller_identity" "current" {}
data "aws_region" "current" {}

# ───────────────────────── KMS ─────────────────────────
# Customer-managed key rather than the AWS-managed aws/s3 key, because we need:
#  (a) a key policy we control (cross-service grants),
#  (b) CloudTrail visibility of every Decrypt with our own key id,
#  (c) the ability to revoke access by editing one policy.
# Cost: $1/month per key + $0.03 per 10k requests.
resource "aws_kms_key" "documents" {
  description             = "${local.name} document encryption"
  enable_key_rotation     = true      # annual automatic rotation, free
  deletion_window_in_days = var.environment == "prod" ? 30 : 7
  policy                  = data.aws_iam_policy_document.kms.json
}

resource "aws_kms_alias" "documents" {
  name          = "alias/${local.name}-documents"
  target_key_id = aws_kms_key.documents.key_id
}

data "aws_iam_policy_document" "kms" {
  # Without this statement, the key becomes unmanageable — a genuinely
  # unrecoverable situation. Always keep an account-root admin statement.
  statement {
    sid       = "AllowAccountAdministration"
    effect    = "Allow"
    actions   = ["kms:*"]
    resources = ["*"]
    principals {
      type        = "AWS"
      identifiers = ["arn:aws:iam::${data.aws_caller_identity.current.account_id}:root"]
    }
  }

  # Let S3 use the key on our behalf, but only for requests originating in
  # this account — this closes the "confused deputy" hole.
  statement {
    sid       = "AllowS3ServiceUse"
    effect    = "Allow"
    actions   = ["kms:GenerateDataKey*", "kms:Decrypt", "kms:Encrypt", "kms:DescribeKey"]
    resources = ["*"]
    principals {
      type        = "Service"
      identifiers = ["s3.amazonaws.com"]
    }
    condition {
      test     = "StringEquals"
      variable = "aws:SourceAccount"
      values   = [data.aws_caller_identity.current.account_id]
    }
  }

  # Textract and the workload roles need to decrypt objects they read.
  statement {
    sid       = "AllowWorkloadUse"
    effect    = "Allow"
    actions   = ["kms:GenerateDataKey*", "kms:Decrypt", "kms:DescribeKey"]
    resources = ["*"]
    principals {
      type        = "AWS"
      identifiers = var.workload_role_arns
    }
  }
}

# ───────────────────────── raw bucket ─────────────────────────
resource "aws_s3_bucket" "raw" {
  bucket = "${local.name}-raw-${data.aws_caller_identity.current.account_id}"
  # Buckets holding customer documents are never casually destroyed.
  force_destroy = var.environment != "prod" ? var.allow_force_destroy : false
}

resource "aws_s3_bucket_versioning" "raw" {
  bucket = aws_s3_bucket.raw.id
  versioning_configuration { status = "Enabled" }
}

resource "aws_s3_bucket_server_side_encryption_configuration" "raw" {
  bucket = aws_s3_bucket.raw.id
  rule {
    apply_server_side_encryption_by_default {
      sse_algorithm     = "aws:kms"
      kms_master_key_id = aws_kms_key.documents.arn
    }
    # S3 Bucket Keys reduce KMS API calls by ~99% for high-volume prefixes.
    # This is a real cost lever, not a micro-optimisation.
    bucket_key_enabled = true
  }
}

resource "aws_s3_bucket_public_access_block" "raw" {
  bucket                  = aws_s3_bucket.raw.id
  block_public_acls       = true
  block_public_policy     = true
  ignore_public_acls      = true
  restrict_public_buckets = true
}

resource "aws_s3_bucket_ownership_controls" "raw" {
  bucket = aws_s3_bucket.raw.id
  rule { object_ownership = "BucketOwnerEnforced" }   # ACLs disabled entirely
}

# Browsers PUT directly to S3 with a presigned URL, so CORS must permit it.
# Allowed origins are explicit — never "*" on a bucket that accepts writes.
resource "aws_s3_bucket_cors_configuration" "raw" {
  bucket = aws_s3_bucket.raw.id
  cors_rule {
    allowed_methods = ["PUT", "GET", "HEAD"]
    allowed_origins = var.cors_origins
    allowed_headers = ["content-type", "content-md5", "x-amz-server-side-encryption",
                       "x-amz-server-side-encryption-aws-kms-key-id", "x-amz-checksum-sha256"]
    expose_headers  = ["ETag", "x-amz-version-id"]
    max_age_seconds = 3000
  }
}

resource "aws_s3_bucket_policy" "raw" {
  bucket = aws_s3_bucket.raw.id
  policy = data.aws_iam_policy_document.raw_bucket.json
}

data "aws_iam_policy_document" "raw_bucket" {
  statement {
    sid       = "DenyInsecureTransport"
    effect    = "Deny"
    actions   = ["s3:*"]
    resources = [aws_s3_bucket.raw.arn, "${aws_s3_bucket.raw.arn}/*"]
    principals {
      type        = "*"
      identifiers = ["*"]
    }
    condition {
      test     = "Bool"
      variable = "aws:SecureTransport"
      values   = ["false"]
    }
  }

  # Reject any upload that isn't encrypted with *our* key. Belt and braces:
  # default encryption already applies, but this blocks an explicit override.
  statement {
    sid       = "DenyWrongEncryptionKey"
    effect    = "Deny"
    actions   = ["s3:PutObject"]
    resources = ["${aws_s3_bucket.raw.arn}/*"]
    principals {
      type        = "*"
      identifiers = ["*"]
    }
    condition {
      test     = "StringNotEqualsIfExists"
      variable = "s3:x-amz-server-side-encryption-aws-kms-key-id"
      values   = [aws_kms_key.documents.arn]
    }
  }
}

# Lifecycle: the single biggest storage cost lever. Raw scans are read heavily
# for a few days, then almost never — but must be retained.
resource "aws_s3_bucket_lifecycle_configuration" "raw" {
  bucket = aws_s3_bucket.raw.id

  rule {
    id     = "tier-down-originals"
    status = "Enabled"
    filter { prefix = "tenant/" }

    transition {
      days          = 30
      storage_class = "STANDARD_IA"        # ~45% cheaper than Standard
    }
    transition {
      days          = 120
      storage_class = "GLACIER_IR"         # ~68% cheaper, millisecond retrieval
    }
    noncurrent_version_expiration { noncurrent_days = 30 }
    abort_incomplete_multipart_upload { days_after_initiation = 3 }
  }

  # Presigned uploads that were never completed leave orphan objects under a
  # staging prefix. Sweep them.
  rule {
    id     = "purge-abandoned-staging"
    status = "Enabled"
    filter { prefix = "staging/" }
    expiration { days = 2 }
  }
}

# EventBridge notifications for the whole bucket. Sprint 04 explains why this
# rather than a direct S3 -> SQS notification.
resource "aws_s3_bucket_notification" "raw_eventbridge" {
  bucket      = aws_s3_bucket.raw.id
  eventbridge = true
}

# ───────────────────── derived bucket ─────────────────────
# Same hardening, different lifecycle: derived artefacts are regenerable,
# so we can expire them aggressively rather than archiving them.
resource "aws_s3_bucket" "derived" {
  bucket        = "${local.name}-derived-${data.aws_caller_identity.current.account_id}"
  force_destroy = var.environment != "prod" ? var.allow_force_destroy : false
}

resource "aws_s3_bucket_lifecycle_configuration" "derived" {
  bucket = aws_s3_bucket.derived.id
  rule {
    id     = "expire-regenerable"
    status = "Enabled"
    filter { prefix = "thumbnails/" }
    expiration { days = 180 }
  }
}
    HCLinfra/modules/storage/variables.tf & outputs.tf
    # variables.tf
variable "project"     { type = string }
variable "environment" {
  type = string
  validation {
    condition     = contains(["dev", "staging", "prod"], var.environment)
    error_message = "environment must be dev, staging or prod."
  }
}
variable "cors_origins" {
  type        = list(string)
  description = "Exact origins allowed to PUT via presigned URL."
  validation {
    condition     = !contains(var.cors_origins, "*")
    error_message = "Wildcard CORS origin is not permitted on a writable bucket."
  }
}
variable "workload_role_arns" {
  type    = list(string)
  default = []
}

variable "allow_force_destroy" {
  type    = bool
  default = false
}

# outputs.tf
output "raw_bucket_id"      { value = aws_s3_bucket.raw.id }
output "raw_bucket_arn"     { value = aws_s3_bucket.raw.arn }
output "derived_bucket_id"  { value = aws_s3_bucket.derived.id }
output "derived_bucket_arn" { value = aws_s3_bucket.derived.arn }
output "kms_key_arn"        { value = aws_kms_key.documents.arn }
output "kms_key_id"         { value = aws_kms_key.documents.key_id }
    A detail that pays for itself

    That validation block rejecting a wildcard CORS origin is the sort of detail that makes a reviewer trust the rest of your code. It encodes a security rule in the module, so no future environment can get it wrong. Terraform variable validation, precondition/postcondition blocks, and check blocks are underused — mention them.

    §4IAM policies that would pass review

    Here is the API's task role. Notice how narrow the resources are, and the conditions.

    HCLinfra/modules/storage/iam.tf
    # Policy for the API: it issues presigned URLs and reads document metadata.
# It must NOT be able to delete objects — deletion goes through a separate,
# audited path, so a compromised API token cannot destroy customer documents.
data "aws_iam_policy_document" "api_storage" {
  statement {
    sid    = "ReadWriteTenantObjects"
    effect = "Allow"
    actions = [
      "s3:GetObject",
      "s3:GetObjectVersion",
      "s3:PutObject",
      "s3:AbortMultipartUpload",
    ]
    # Only under the tenant prefix — not the bucket root, not other prefixes.
    resources = ["${aws_s3_bucket.raw.arn}/tenant/*", "${aws_s3_bucket.derived.arn}/tenant/*"]
  }

  statement {
    sid       = "ListOnlyWithinPrefix"
    effect    = "Allow"
    actions   = ["s3:ListBucket"]
    resources = [aws_s3_bucket.raw.arn]
    condition {
      test     = "StringLike"
      variable = "s3:prefix"
      values   = ["tenant/*"]
    }
  }

  statement {
    sid       = "UseDocumentKey"
    effect    = "Allow"
    actions   = ["kms:Decrypt", "kms:GenerateDataKey"]
    resources = [aws_kms_key.documents.arn]
    # Restrict key use to S3 traffic in our region: the role cannot use this
    # key to decrypt anything else, anywhere else.
    condition {
      test     = "StringEquals"
      variable = "kms:ViaService"
      values   = ["s3.${data.aws_region.current.name}.amazonaws.com"]
    }
  }

  statement {
    sid       = "ExplicitlyDenyDestructive"
    effect    = "Deny"
    actions   = ["s3:DeleteObject", "s3:DeleteObjectVersion", "s3:PutBucketPolicy"]
    resources = ["*"]
  }
}

resource "aws_iam_policy" "api_storage" {
  name   = "${local.name}-api-storage"
  policy = data.aws_iam_policy_document.api_storage.json
}

    And the trust policy that lets an ECS task assume a role — with the source-ARN condition that most tutorials omit:

    HCLtrust policy with confused-deputy protection
    data "aws_iam_policy_document" "ecs_task_assume" {
  statement {
    effect  = "Allow"
    actions = ["sts:AssumeRole"]
    principals {
      type        = "Service"
      identifiers = ["ecs-tasks.amazonaws.com"]
    }

    # Without these, any ECS task in ANY account that AWS routes through the
    # service principal could theoretically assume this role. Scope it.
    condition {
      test     = "StringEquals"
      variable = "aws:SourceAccount"
      values   = [data.aws_caller_identity.current.account_id]
    }
    condition {
      test     = "ArnLike"
      variable = "aws:SourceArn"
      values   = ["arn:aws:ecs:${data.aws_region.current.name}:${data.aws_caller_identity.current.account_id}:*"]
    }
  }
}

    The IAM mental checklist

    1. Which principal? One role per workload, never a shared "app role".
    2. Which actions? Enumerate them. If you typed s3:*, you haven't finished thinking.
    3. Which resources? ARNs with prefixes, not "*". Note that s3:ListBucket acts on the bucket ARN while s3:GetObject acts on object ARNs — mixing these up is the classic beginner error.
    4. Which conditions? aws:SecureTransport, kms:ViaService, aws:SourceAccount, s3:prefix.
    5. What should be explicitly denied? Deny beats allow, so a targeted deny is a durable guardrail.

    §5Wiring the dev environment

    HCLinfra/envs/dev/main.tf
    provider "aws" {
  region = var.region

  # Applied to every taggable resource created by this provider. One block,
  # complete cost attribution.
  default_tags {
    tags = {
      Project     = var.project
      Environment = var.environment
      ManagedBy   = "terraform"
      Owner       = var.owner
      Repo        = "github.com/you/idp-platform"
    }
  }
}

module "storage" {
  source = "../../modules/storage"

  project             = var.project
  environment         = var.environment
  cors_origins        = ["http://localhost:5173", "https://dev.idp.example.com"]
  allow_force_destroy = true          # dev only: makes teardown painless
  workload_role_arns  = []            # filled in once roles exist (Sprint 04/07)
}

output "raw_bucket"  { value = module.storage.raw_bucket_id }
output "kms_key_arn" { value = module.storage.kms_key_arn }
    Shellthe apply loop
    # One time, from infra/bootstrap
cd infra/bootstrap
terraform init && terraform apply
terraform output state_bucket        # paste into envs/*/backend.tf

# Every time after that, from an env root
cd ../envs/dev
terraform init
terraform fmt -check -recursive      # formatting is not optional in CI
terraform validate
terraform plan -out=tf.plan          # ALWAYS save the plan...
terraform apply tf.plan              # ...and apply exactly that plan

# What did it actually create?
terraform state list
aws s3api get-bucket-encryption --bucket "$(terraform output -raw raw_bucket)"
    Habit worth building now

    terraform apply without a saved plan file re-plans, which means what you reviewed and what you apply can differ if something changed in between. Always plan -out then apply <file>. This becomes the CI pattern in Sprint 09: plan on pull request, apply the stored plan artefact after approval.

    §6Testing infrastructure code

    Four levels, cheapest first. Do the first three now; terraform test is worth adding once modules stabilise.

    LevelToolCatches
    Staticterraform fmt -check, terraform validateSyntax, formatting, bad references, type mismatches
    Policy / securitytfsec or checkov, tflintUnencrypted resources, public access, missing logging, over-broad IAM
    Plan reviewterraform plan + terraform show -json | jqUnintended destroys, replacements, drift
    Behaviouralterraform test (native, HCL) or Terratest (Go)"Does the module actually produce a private, encrypted bucket?"
    HCLinfra/modules/storage/tests/hardening.tftest.hcl
    variables {
  project      = "idp"
  environment  = "dev"
  cors_origins = ["http://localhost:5173"]
}

# A plan-only test: fast, free, no AWS resources created.
run "buckets_are_hardened" {
  command = plan

  assert {
    condition     = aws_s3_bucket_public_access_block.raw.block_public_policy == true
    error_message = "raw bucket must block public bucket policies"
  }
  assert {
    condition = aws_s3_bucket_server_side_encryption_configuration.raw
                  .rule[0].apply_server_side_encryption_by_default[0].sse_algorithm == "aws:kms"
    error_message = "raw bucket must use SSE-KMS, not SSE-S3"
  }
  assert {
    condition     = aws_s3_bucket_versioning.raw.versioning_configuration[0].status == "Enabled"
    error_message = "versioning is required for recoverability"
  }
}

# The security rule the module is supposed to make impossible.
run "wildcard_cors_is_rejected" {
  command = plan
  variables { cors_origins = ["*"] }
  expect_failures = [var.cors_origins]
}
    Shellverification after apply
    B=$(terraform output -raw raw_bucket)

# 1. Not public
aws s3api get-bucket-policy-status --bucket "$B" \
  --query 'PolicyStatus.IsPublic'            # false

# 2. Confirm TLS is enforced (this request must FAIL)
curl -s -o /dev/null -w '%{http_code}\n' "http://$B.s3.eu-west-1.amazonaws.com/"   # 403

# 3. Upload, confirm encryption context came back
echo hello > /tmp/t.txt
aws s3api put-object --bucket "$B" --key tenant/test/t.txt --body /tmp/t.txt
aws s3api head-object --bucket "$B" --key tenant/test/t.txt \
  --query '{SSE:ServerSideEncryption,Key:SSEKMSKeyId}'    # aws:kms + our key arn

# 4. Lifecycle rules present
aws s3api get-bucket-lifecycle-configuration --bucket "$B" \
  --query 'Rules[].{Id:ID,Status:Status}' --output table

# 5. Would IAM allow what I think it allows? Simulate before you trust.
aws iam simulate-principal-policy \
  --policy-source-arn "arn:aws:iam::$(aws sts get-caller-identity --query Account --output text):role/idp-dev-api-task" \
  --action-names s3:DeleteObject \
  --resource-arns "arn:aws:s3:::$B/tenant/test/t.txt" \
  --query 'EvaluationResults[].{Action:EvalActionName,Decision:EvalDecision}'
# Expect implicitExplicitDeny — our explicit Deny statement working.
    Tool most people miss

    aws iam simulate-principal-policy answers "will this role be allowed to do X?" without performing X. Combined with IAM Access Analyzer's unused-access findings, it's how you tighten policies with evidence instead of guesswork. Very few candidates mention it.

    §7AWS concepts introduced here

    • S3 storage classes. Standard → Standard-IA (30 days) → Glacier Instant Retrieval (120 days). Each transition has a per-object request cost, so tiering thousands of tiny objects can cost more than it saves — tier by size and access pattern, not reflexively. Intelligent-Tiering automates this for a small monitoring fee per object and is a defensible choice when access patterns are unknown.
    • S3 versioning. Overwrites and deletes create versions/delete markers rather than destroying data — your protection against a buggy worker and against ransomware. It also means noncurrent versions accrue cost, hence the lifecycle rule.
    • KMS: envelope encryption. S3 doesn't encrypt your object with the KMS key directly; it asks KMS for a data key, encrypts locally, and stores the wrapped key alongside the object. That's why S3 Bucket Keys matter — they let S3 reuse one data key across many objects in a prefix, cutting KMS request volume (and cost) by ~99%.
    • Terraform state. A JSON map linking each resource in your configuration to the real resource ID it created, along with that resource's attribute values. It can contain sensitive data, so encrypt it, version it, restrict access, and never commit it (except the bootstrap module's, which contains nothing secret). Locking prevents two applies corrupting it simultaneously.
    • Drift. Someone clicks something in the console; reality diverges from state. terraform plan shows it; a scheduled plan in CI (Sprint 09) alerts on it.

    §8Trade-offs decided in this sprint

    DecisionChosenAlternativeReasoning
    Environment separationDirectory per environmentTerraform workspacesIndependent state, independent variables, structurally different environments without conditional soup. Workspaces are fine for near-identical copies.
    State lockingS3 native use_lockfileDynamoDB lock tableOne less resource; supported from Terraform 1.10. Know the DynamoDB pattern because most existing codebases use it.
    EncryptionCustomer-managed KMS keySSE-S3 (free)SSE-S3 costs nothing and meets "encrypted at rest" for most compliance frameworks. We pay $1/month for a key policy we control, per-key CloudTrail auditability, and the ability to revoke by policy — a reasonable premium for customer documents. Bucket Keys keep request costs negligible.
    Bucket countTwo (raw, derived)One bucket with prefixesDifferent lifecycle policies, different blast radius, and the API's write permission on derived data doesn't imply write permission on originals.
    NotificationsEventBridge on the bucketDirect S3 → SQSExplained fully in Sprint 04: fan-out to multiple targets, content-based filtering, replay, and schema-registry tooling — at the cost of one extra hop.

    §9Interview questions on this material

    How do you manage Terraform state for a team, and what can go wrong?

    Remote backend in S3 with encryption, versioning, a TLS-only bucket policy, and locking — S3 native lockfiles on Terraform 1.10+, or a DynamoDB table on older versions. One state file per environment per component, so the blast radius of a bad apply is bounded and plans stay fast.

    Failure modes I plan for: concurrent applies corrupting state (locking); a lock left behind by a crashed run (terraform force-unlock, used carefully after confirming nothing is actually running); state loss or corruption (bucket versioning — restore the previous object version); secrets in state (they can be, so treat the bucket as sensitive and restrict who can read it — this is why I don't put real secrets in Terraform variables, and why RDS passwords are generated into Secrets Manager rather than passed in); and drift from console changes, which a scheduled plan in CI surfaces as an alert.

    The one I'd emphasise: state is not a backup. It's a mapping. If I lose it, resources keep running but Terraform no longer knows about them, and re-importing a large environment takes days. Hence prevent_destroy on the state bucket and versioning as the actual recovery mechanism.

    Walk me through securing an S3 bucket that holds customer documents.

    Six layers. Access: account-level public access block plus per-bucket block, ACLs disabled with BucketOwnerEnforced, and no bucket policy granting anything to Principal: "*" except denies. Encryption: SSE-KMS with a customer-managed key, Bucket Keys on to control KMS cost, and a bucket policy statement denying PutObject with any other key id. Transport: a deny statement on aws:SecureTransport = false. Integrity: versioning on, so overwrites and deletes are recoverable; MFA delete or Object Lock in governance mode if the data warrants it. Least privilege: the API role can read and write only under tenant/*, can only list with that prefix, and is explicitly denied DeleteObject — deletion happens through a separate audited path. Audit: CloudTrail data events for the bucket, server access logs to a separate log bucket, and IAM Access Analyzer to flag any policy that grants external access.

    Then the access pattern itself: the browser never gets AWS credentials. It gets a presigned PUT URL scoped to one exact key, with a short expiry and a content-length range condition, so the worst case for a leaked URL is one object overwritten in one tenant's prefix.

    What does least privilege actually look like in an IAM policy? Show me the difference.

    The naive version is {"Effect":"Allow","Action":"s3:*","Resource":"*"}, which means a compromised task can read and delete every object in the account, including Terraform state.

    The real version constrains four dimensions. Actions enumerated: GetObject, PutObject, AbortMultipartUpload — and notably not DeleteObject. Resources narrowed to arn:aws:s3:::idp-prod-raw-123/tenant/*, with a separate statement for ListBucket because that action targets the bucket ARN, not object ARNs — a distinction people get wrong constantly. Conditions: s3:prefix restricting what can be listed, and on the KMS grant, kms:ViaService = s3.eu-west-1.amazonaws.com so the role can't use that key to decrypt anything outside S3. An explicit deny on destructive actions, because deny beats allow — even if someone later attaches a broader policy by mistake, that deny holds.

    Getting there in practice: start restrictive, run the workload, read CloudTrail AccessDenied events, add exactly what's needed. Then let IAM Access Analyzer's unused-access findings tell you what to remove three months later.

    How do you structure Terraform for multiple environments?

    Modules contain no provider or backend blocks — they're pure, composable, and independently testable. Each environment is a root module directory with its own backend key, its own provider configuration including default_tags, and its own tfvars. Environments call the same modules with different inputs.

    What this buys you is environments that differ structurally rather than only numerically. Dev is single-AZ RDS on a t4g.micro with one NAT gateway and seven-day KMS deletion; prod is Multi-AZ with deletion protection, cross-AZ NAT, and thirty-day KMS deletion. With workspaces you'd express that as conditionals threaded through shared code, which gets unreadable fast.

    I'd add: pin the provider version with ~>, commit the lock file, use variable validation blocks to encode policy in the module (my storage module refuses a wildcard CORS origin), and keep modules small enough that the plan for one is reviewable in a pull request. For genuinely shared values across environments I'd use SSM Parameter Store with a data source rather than terraform_remote_state, which couples state files together.

    KMS customer-managed key versus SSE-S3 — when is the extra cost justified?

    SSE-S3 is free, AES-256, fully managed, and meets "encrypted at rest" for most compliance frameworks. If that's the requirement, taking it is the correct engineering call and I'd say so.

    A customer-managed key buys three things worth $1/month plus request charges. First, a key policy you control: you decide which principals and which services can decrypt, and you can revoke access by editing one document — including making data cryptographically inaccessible without touching the objects. Second, audit granularity: every Decrypt call appears in CloudTrail with your key id, so you can answer "who read customer documents last Tuesday". Third, separation of duties: someone with S3 read permission but no KMS grant still cannot read the plaintext, which is a meaningful control for a document platform.

    The cost trap to name: without S3 Bucket Keys, every single object operation is a KMS API call at $0.03 per 10,000. On a bucket doing millions of reads, that's real money. Bucket Keys let S3 reuse a data key across a prefix and cut those calls by roughly 99%, which turns KMS from a line item back into a rounding error.

    §10Definition of done

    Sprint 03 · The product

    Identity: Cognito, JWTs, and where the tenant comes from

    Authentication is where the multi-tenant model becomes real. The critical decision: the tenant id must arrive in a cryptographically verified token claim, never in a header or body the client can edit. Everything downstream depends on getting this right.

    Time
    1–2 days
    You'll produce
    User pool, custom claims, JWT verification, RBAC, JIT provisioning
    Running cost
    $0 under 50k MAU then ~$0.0055/MAU
    Key idea
    Verify tokens locally against JWKS — never call the IdP per request

    §1Requirements

    • Users sign in with email + password; MFA available, enforced for the owner role.
    • Every access token carries custom:tenant_id and the user's role.
    • The API verifies tokens offline (no network call per request) and rejects expired, wrong-audience, or wrong-issuer tokens.
    • Three roles: owner (billing, members, delete), member (upload, read), viewer (read only).
    • First login provisions the user row in our database automatically (just-in-time provisioning).
    • Tokens are short-lived (15 min access, 30 day refresh with rotation).

    §2How OIDC actually works (the five-minute version)

    Cognito is an OAuth 2.0 / OpenID Connect provider. The flow you want for a single-page app is Authorization Code with PKCE. Here is what happens, in order:

    1. SPAredirect + code_challenge
    →
    2. Cognitohosted login page
    →
    3. SPA?code=… callback
    →
    4. /oauth2/tokencode + verifier
    →
    5. SPAid + access + refresh
    →
    6. APIverify offline

    Why PKCE? A browser app cannot keep a client secret — anything shipped to the browser is public. PKCE replaces the secret with a per-request proof: the app generates a random code_verifier, sends its SHA-256 hash as code_challenge at step 1, and presents the original verifier at step 4. An attacker who intercepts the authorization code cannot exchange it without the verifier.

    Three tokens, three purposes

    TokenContainsSend it toCommon mistake
    ID tokenWho the user is: sub, email, name, custom attributes. Audience = your app client.Your own frontend, for displayUsing it as an API credential. It's an identity assertion for the client, not an authorization token for a resource server.
    Access tokenWhat the bearer may do: scope, client_id, cognito:groups, plus claims you add. Audience = the resource server.Your API, as Authorization: BearerNot checking token_use, so an ID token is accepted where an access token was required.
    Refresh tokenAn opaque handle used to mint new access tokens.Only Cognito's token endpointStoring it in localStorage. It is the long-lived credential — treat it like a password.
    Historical Cognito quirk

    Cognito's access token historically did not include arbitrary custom attributes — they lived on the ID token. Modern pre-token-generation triggers (V2_0 and later) can add claims to the access token, which is what we use. If you find yourself unable to see custom:tenant_id in the access token, check that your Lambda trigger version supports access-token customisation and that the trigger is actually wired to the pool. This exact issue trips people up constantly — it's a good war story to have.

    §2bFundamentals: what a JWT actually is

    A JSON Web Token is three base64url-encoded strings joined by dots. You can decode one by hand, and doing so once removes most of the mystery.

    Shelldecode a token yourself
    # Split on dots, decode the first two parts. The third is the signature,
# which is binary and will not decode to anything readable.
TOKEN="eyJraWQiOiJ..."

# Header: which algorithm, and which key signed it
echo "$TOKEN" | cut -d. -f1 | base64 -d 2>/dev/null | jq .
# { "kid": "abc123...", "alg": "RS256" }

# Payload: the claims
echo "$TOKEN" | cut -d. -f2 | base64 -d 2>/dev/null | jq .
# {
#   "sub": "9f8e7d6c-...",         <- stable user id, our join key
#   "token_use": "access",          <- MUST be checked
#   "iss": "https://cognito-idp.eu-west-1.amazonaws.com/eu-west-1_AbC",
#   "client_id": "1h57kf5...",
#   "exp": 1735689600,              <- expiry, seconds since epoch
#   "iat": 1735688700,
#   "custom:tenant_id": "..."
# }

    Four things follow from that structure, and each one matters.

    1. A JWT is signed, not encrypted. Anyone holding the token can read every claim. Never put a secret in one. The signature guarantees the claims have not been altered; it does not hide them.
    2. The kid in the header selects the key. Identity providers rotate signing keys and publish several at once, so the verifier fetches the key set and picks the matching key by id. That is why key rotation does not break running services.
    3. Verification is local and cheap. RSA signature verification takes microseconds. No network call, which is why your API does not ask Cognito anything per request.
    4. A valid signature is the only thing consulted. There is no lookup, so there is no way to invalidate one token before exp without adding shared state. That is the trade you accept for statelessness, and it is why token lifetime is a security decision rather than a convenience one.
    The checks people skip

    Verifying the signature is necessary and not sufficient. A correctly signed token from a different user pool, or minted for a different app client, or an ID token presented where an access token was required, all have perfectly valid signatures. So the verifier must also check iss matches your pool, aud or client_id matches your app client, exp has not passed and token_use is what you expect. The aws-jwt-verify library does all of this when configured properly, which is a reason to use it rather than a generic JWT library.

    §3Terraform: the user pool

    HCLinfra/modules/identity/main.tf
    locals {

}

resource "aws_cognito_user_pool" "main" {
  name = local.name

  # Email as the sign-in identifier. alias_attributes is the alternative if you
  # want username OR email; username_attributes makes email the username outright.
  username_attributes      = ["email"]
  auto_verified_attributes = ["email"]

  username_configuration {
    case_sensitive = false
  }

  password_policy {
    minimum_length                   = 12
    require_lowercase                = true
    require_uppercase                = true
    require_numbers                  = true
    require_symbols                  = true
    temporary_password_validity_days = 3
    # Blocks the top-N compromised passwords. Free, no reason not to.
    password_history_size            = 3
  }

  mfa_configuration = "OPTIONAL"
  software_token_mfa_configuration {
    enabled = true
  }

  # Advanced security: adaptive authentication and compromised-credential
  # detection. Costs extra per MAU — enable in prod only.
  user_pool_add_ons {
    advanced_security_mode = var.environment == "prod" ? "ENFORCED" : "OFF"
  }

  account_recovery_setting {
    recovery_mechanism {
      name     = "verified_email"
      priority = 1
    }
  }

  # Only administrators create users: this is a B2B platform, not open signup.
  admin_create_user_config {

    invite_message_template {
      email_subject = "Your ${var.project} account"
      email_message = "Your username is {username} and temporary password is {####}"
      sms_message   = "Username {username}, temporary password {####}"
    }
  }

  # The tenant binding. Immutable: a user cannot be moved between tenants by
  # editing an attribute — that would be a data-isolation bypass.
  schema {
    name                     = "tenant_id"
    attribute_data_type      = "String"
    mutable                  = false
    required                 = false

    attribute_data_type = "String"
    mutable             = true
    required            = false
    string_attribute_constraints {
      min_length = 3
      max_length = 16
    }
  }

  lambda_config {
    # V2_0 lets us add claims to the ACCESS token, not just the ID token.
    pre_token_generation_config {
      lambda_arn     = aws_lambda_function.pre_token.arn
      lambda_version = "V2_0"
    }
  }

  deletion_protection = var.environment == "prod" ? "ACTIVE" : "INACTIVE"

  tags = {
    Component = "identity"
  }
}

# Public client for the SPA: NO client secret (a browser cannot keep one),
# PKCE enforced by using the code grant without a secret.
resource "aws_cognito_user_pool_client" "web" {
  name         = "${local.name}-web"
  user_pool_id = aws_cognito_user_pool.main.id

  generate_secret = false

  allowed_oauth_flows                  = ["code"]
  allowed_oauth_flows_user_pool_client = true
  allowed_oauth_scopes = concat(
    ["openid", "email", "profile"],
    [for s in ["documents.read", "documents.write", "tenant.admin"] :
      "${aws_cognito_resource_server.api.identifier}/${s}"]
  )

  supported_identity_providers = ["COGNITO"]
  callback_urls                = var.callback_urls
  logout_urls                  = var.logout_urls

  # Short access tokens limit the damage from a stolen token; the refresh token
  # does the work of keeping the session alive.
  access_token_validity  = 15
  id_token_validity      = 15
  refresh_token_validity = 30
  token_validity_units {
    access_token  = "minutes"
    id_token      = "minutes"
    refresh_token = "days"
  }

  # Rotate refresh tokens on use: a replayed old refresh token is detectable.
  refresh_token_rotation {
    feature                     = "ENABLED"
    retry_grace_period_seconds  = 30
  }

  # SRP for the SDK path, refresh for renewal. USER_PASSWORD_AUTH is
  # deliberately absent: it sends the raw password to the API.
  explicit_auth_flows = ["ALLOW_USER_SRP_AUTH", "ALLOW_REFRESH_TOKEN_AUTH"]

  prevent_user_existence_errors = "ENABLED"   # don't leak whether an email exists
  enable_token_revocation       = true
}

  user_pool_id = aws_cognito_user_pool.main.id
