  name         = each.key
  user_pool_id = aws_cognito_user_pool.main.id
  precedence   = index(["owner", "member", "viewer"], each.key)
}

    The pre-token-generation trigger

    This Lambda runs inside the login flow and injects our claims. Keep it tiny and fast — it is on the critical path of every authentication.

    TypeScriptservices/pre-token/src/handler.ts
    import type { PreTokenGenerationV2TriggerHandler } from 'aws-lambda';

/**
 * Adds tenant_id and role to BOTH the id and access tokens so the API can
 * authorise without a database lookup on every request.
 *
 * Runs synchronously inside login. Budget: single-digit milliseconds. Do not
 * call a database here — read from the user attributes Cognito already loaded.
 */
export const handler: PreTokenGenerationV2TriggerHandler = async (event) => {
  const attrs = event.request.userAttributes;
  const tenantId = attrs['custom:tenant_id'];
  const role = attrs['custom:role'] ?? 'member';
  const groups = event.request.groupConfiguration?.groupsToOverride ?? [];

  // A user without a tenant is a provisioning bug. Fail the login loudly
  // rather than issuing a token the API will have to reject confusingly.
  if (!tenantId) {
    throw new Error(`User ${event.userName} has no custom:tenant_id`);
  }

  // Effective role: the group is authoritative if present, since group
  // membership is what an admin manipulates in the console.
  const effectiveRole =
    groups.find((g) => g === 'owner') ??
    groups.find((g) => g === 'member') ??
    groups.find((g) => g === 'viewer') ??
    role;

  event.response = {
    claimsAndScopeOverrideDetails: {
      idTokenGeneration: {
        claimsToAddOrOverride: {
          'custom:tenant_id': tenantId,
          'custom:role': effectiveRole,
        },
      },
      accessTokenGeneration: {
        claimsToAddOrOverride: {
          'custom:tenant_id': tenantId,
          'custom:role': effectiveRole,
        },
      },
    },
  };

  return event;
};

    §4Verifying tokens in Fastify

    The core insight: you do not call Cognito to validate a token. A JWT is signed with a private key; Cognito publishes the matching public keys at a well-known JWKS URL. You fetch those keys once, cache them, and verify signatures locally. That's an in-process cryptographic operation taking microseconds, versus a network round-trip that would add latency to every request and make Cognito a hard dependency for reads.

    TypeScriptapps/api/src/plugins/auth.ts
    import fp from 'fastify-plugin';
import { CognitoJwtVerifier } from 'aws-jwt-verify';
import { eq } from 'drizzle-orm';
import { users, tenants } from '@idp/db/schema';
import { config } from '../config.js';

export type Role = 'owner' | 'member' | 'viewer';

export interface AuthPrincipal {
  userId: string;          // our users.id
  cognitoSub: string;
  email: string;
  role: Role;
}

declare module 'fastify' {
  interface FastifyInstance {
    requireAuth: (req: FastifyRequest, reply: FastifyReply) => Promise<void>;
    requireRole: (...allowed: Role[]) =>
      (req: FastifyRequest, reply: FastifyReply) => Promise<void>;
  }
  interface FastifyRequest {
    principal: AuthPrincipal;
    tenant: { id: string; name: string };
  }
}

const ROLE_RANK: Record<Role, number> = { viewer: 0, member: 1, owner: 2 };

export const authPlugin = fp(async (app) => {
  // The verifier fetches and caches the JWKS on first use, refreshes on key
  // rotation, and verifies signature + exp + iss + aud + token_use offline.
  const verifier = CognitoJwtVerifier.create({
    userPoolId: config.COGNITO_USER_POOL_ID,
    clientId: config.COGNITO_CLIENT_ID,
    tokenUse: 'access',        // reject ID tokens presented as bearer credentials
  });

  // Warm the JWKS cache at boot so the first real request isn't slow, and so a
  // misconfigured pool fails at startup rather than under traffic.
  try {
    await verifier.hydrate();
  } catch (err) {
    app.log.error({ err }, 'failed to hydrate Cognito JWKS');
    throw err;
  }

  // Small in-process cache: sub -> our user row. Avoids a DB round-trip per
  // request while keeping the window for a revoked user short.
  const userCache = new Map<string, { p: AuthPrincipal; t: { id: string; name: string }; exp: number }>();
  const USER_TTL_MS = 60_000;

  app.decorate('requireAuth', async (req, reply) => {
    const header = req.headers.authorization;
    if (!header?.startsWith('Bearer ')) {
      return reply.code(401).send({
        error: { code: 'unauthenticated', message: 'Missing bearer token', requestId: req.id },
      });
    }

    let claims: Awaited<ReturnType<typeof verifier.verify>>;
    try {
      claims = await verifier.verify(header.slice(7));
    } catch (err) {
      req.log.warn({ err: (err as Error).message }, 'token verification failed');
      return reply.code(401).send({
        error: { code: 'invalid_token', message: 'Token is invalid or expired', requestId: req.id },
      });
    }

    // THE critical line: tenant comes from a signed claim. Never from
    // req.headers['x-tenant-id'], never from the body, never from a query param.
    const tenantId = claims['custom:tenant_id'] as string | undefined;
    const sub = claims.sub;
    if (!tenantId) {
      return reply.code(403).send({
        error: { code: 'no_tenant', message: 'Token carries no tenant', requestId: req.id },
      });
    }

    const cached = userCache.get(sub);
    if (cached && cached.exp > Date.now() && cached.t.id === tenantId) {
      req.principal = cached.p;
      req.tenant = cached.t;
      return;
    }

    // Just-in-time provisioning: an authenticated user we've never seen gets a
    // row on first request. Keeps Cognito as the source of truth for identity
    // and avoids a separate sync job.
    const [row] = await app.db
      .select({
        userId: users.id,
        sub: users.cognitoSub,
        email: users.email,
        role: users.role,
        tenantId: tenants.id,
        tenantName: tenants.name,
      })
      .from(users)
      .innerJoin(tenants, eq(users.tenantId, tenants.id))
      .where(eq(users.cognitoSub, sub))
      .limit(1);

    let principal: AuthPrincipal;
    let tenant: { id: string; name: string };

    if (row) {
      // Defence in depth: if the token's tenant disagrees with the database,
      // something is very wrong. Refuse rather than guess.
      if (row.tenantId !== tenantId) {
        req.log.error({ sub, tokenTenant: tenantId, dbTenant: row.tenantId },
          'tenant mismatch between token and database');
        return reply.code(403).send({
          error: { code: 'tenant_mismatch', message: 'Access denied', requestId: req.id },
        });
      }
      principal = {
        userId: row.userId, cognitoSub: sub, email: row.email, role: row.role as Role,
      };
      tenant = { id: row.tenantId, name: row.tenantName };
    } else {
      const provisioned = await provisionUser(app, {
        sub,
        email: (claims.email as string) ?? `${sub}@unknown`,
        tenantId,
        role: ((claims['custom:role'] as Role) ?? 'member'),
      });
      if (!provisioned) {
        return reply.code(403).send({
          error: { code: 'unknown_tenant', message: 'Access denied', requestId: req.id },
        });
      }
      principal = provisioned.principal;
      tenant = provisioned.tenant;
    }

    userCache.set(sub, { p: principal, t: tenant, exp: Date.now() + USER_TTL_MS });
    req.principal = principal;
    req.tenant = tenant;
  });

  app.decorate('requireRole', (...allowed: Role[]) => async (req, reply) => {
    const need = Math.min(...allowed.map((r) => ROLE_RANK[r]));
    if (ROLE_RANK[req.principal.role] < need) {
      req.log.warn({ have: req.principal.role, need: allowed }, 'insufficient role');
      return reply.code(403).send({
        error: { code: 'forbidden', message: 'Insufficient permissions', requestId: req.id },
      });
    }
  });
});

    Usage in a route — role checks compose with the auth hook:

    TypeScriptroute-level authorisation
    // Any authenticated user in the tenant
app.get('/', { preHandler: app.requireAuth }, listDocuments);

// member or owner
app.post('/', { preHandler: [app.requireAuth, app.requireRole('member')] }, createUpload);

// owner only
app.delete('/:id', { preHandler: [app.requireAuth, app.requireRole('owner')] }, deleteDocument);
    The one-sentence version to memorise

    "The tenant id is read from a signed access-token claim verified offline against the pool's JWKS, then passed into a transaction-scoped Postgres session variable that row-level security policies enforce — so a missing tenant context returns zero rows rather than everything."

    §5Frontend auth flow (preview of Sprint 10)

    TypeScriptapps/web/src/lib/auth.ts (excerpt)
    /**
 * Token storage decision, stated explicitly because interviewers ask:
 *
 *  - Access token: in memory only (a module-scoped variable). Lost on refresh,
 *    which is fine — we re-acquire it silently with the refresh token.
 *  - Refresh token: httpOnly, Secure, SameSite=Strict cookie set by a tiny
 *    backend token-exchange endpoint. NOT localStorage: anything in
 *    localStorage is readable by any XSS payload on the page.
 *
 * The pure-SPA alternative (refresh token in localStorage) is simpler and very
 * common, but accepts that one XSS becomes a persistent account takeover.
 */
let accessToken: string | null = null;
let expiresAt = 0;

export async function getAccessToken(): Promise<string> {
  if (accessToken && Date.now() < expiresAt - 30_000) return accessToken;

  // The refresh cookie travels automatically; the browser never sees the token.
  const res = await fetch('/auth/refresh', { method: 'POST', credentials: 'include' });
  if (!res.ok) {
    window.location.href = buildLoginUrl();
    throw new Error('session expired');
  }
  const body = (await res.json()) as { accessToken: string; expiresIn: number };
  accessToken = body.accessToken;
  expiresAt = Date.now() + body.expiresIn * 1000;
  return accessToken;
}

/** PKCE: generate a verifier, store it, send only its hash. */
export async function buildLoginUrl(): Promise<string> {
  const verifier = base64url(crypto.getRandomValues(new Uint8Array(32)));
  sessionStorage.setItem('pkce_verifier', verifier);
  const digest = await crypto.subtle.digest('SHA-256', new TextEncoder().encode(verifier));
  const challenge = base64url(new Uint8Array(digest));

  const u = new URL(`https://${import.meta.env.VITE_COGNITO_DOMAIN}/oauth2/authorize`);
  u.searchParams.set('response_type', 'code');
  u.searchParams.set('client_id', import.meta.env.VITE_COGNITO_CLIENT_ID);
  u.searchParams.set('redirect_uri', `${location.origin}/auth/callback`);
  u.searchParams.set('scope', 'openid email profile');
  u.searchParams.set('code_challenge', challenge);
  u.searchParams.set('code_challenge_method', 'S256');
  // CSRF protection for the redirect itself
  u.searchParams.set('state', crypto.randomUUID());
  return u.toString();
}

    §6Testing authentication

    You cannot call real Cognito in unit tests, and you should not want to. Generate tokens with a local key pair and point the verifier at a fake JWKS.

    TypeScriptapps/api/test/helpers/jwt.ts
    import { generateKeyPairSync, createPublicKey } from 'node:crypto';
import { SignJWT, exportJWK } from 'jose';

const { privateKey, publicKey } = generateKeyPairSync('rsa', { modulusLength: 2048 });
const KID = 'test-key-1';

/** Serve this from a local http server (or msw) as the pool's JWKS endpoint. */
export async function testJwks() {
  const jwk = await exportJWK(createPublicKey(publicKey));
  return { keys: [{ ...jwk, kid: KID, alg: 'RS256', use: 'sig' }] };
}

export async function signTestToken(opts: {
  sub?: string;
  tenantId: string;
  role?: 'owner' | 'member' | 'viewer';
  expiresIn?: string;
  tokenUse?: 'access' | 'id';
}) {
  return new SignJWT({
    token_use: opts.tokenUse ?? 'access',
    client_id: process.env.COGNITO_CLIENT_ID,
    'custom:tenant_id': opts.tenantId,
    'custom:role': opts.role ?? 'member',
    scope: 'documents.read documents.write',
  })
    .setProtectedHeader({ alg: 'RS256', kid: KID })
    .setSubject(opts.sub ?? crypto.randomUUID())
    .setIssuer(`https://cognito-idp.eu-west-1.amazonaws.com/${process.env.COGNITO_USER_POOL_ID}`)
    .setIssuedAt()
    .setExpirationTime(opts.expiresIn ?? '15m')
    .sign(privateKey);
}
    TypeScriptapps/api/test/auth.it.test.ts
    describe('authentication', () => {
  it('rejects a request with no token', async () => {
    const res = await app.inject({ method: 'GET', url: '/v1/documents' });
    expect(res.statusCode).toBe(401);
    expect(res.json().error.code).toBe('unauthenticated');
  });

  it('rejects an expired token', async () => {
    const token = await signTestToken({ tenantId: TENANT_A, expiresIn: '-1m' });
    const res = await app.inject({
      method: 'GET', url: '/v1/documents',
      headers: { authorization: `Bearer ${token}` },
    });
    expect(res.statusCode).toBe(401);
  });

  it('rejects an ID token used as a bearer credential', async () => {
    const token = await signTestToken({ tenantId: TENANT_A, tokenUse: 'id' });
    const res = await app.inject({
      method: 'GET', url: '/v1/documents',
      headers: { authorization: `Bearer ${token}` },
    });
    expect(res.statusCode).toBe(401);
  });

  it('ignores a client-supplied tenant header entirely', async () => {
    const token = await signTestToken({ tenantId: TENANT_A });
    const res = await app.inject({
      method: 'GET', url: '/v1/documents',
      headers: { authorization: `Bearer ${token}`, 'x-tenant-id': TENANT_B },
    });
    expect(res.statusCode).toBe(200);
    // Every returned document belongs to A, despite the header claiming B.
    for (const d of res.json().items) {
      expect(await tenantOf(d.id)).toBe(TENANT_A);
    }
  });

  it('denies delete to a viewer', async () => {
    const token = await signTestToken({ tenantId: TENANT_A, role: 'viewer' });
    const res = await app.inject({
      method: 'DELETE', url: `/v1/documents/${DOC_A}`,
      headers: { authorization: `Bearer ${token}` },
    });
    expect(res.statusCode).toBe(403);
  });
});

    §7Trade-offs

    DecisionChosenAlternativeReasoning
    Identity providerCognito user poolAuth0 / Clerk / roll your ownFree to 50k MAU, native IAM integration, no vendor to bill separately. Honest downsides: the developer experience is rough, the hosted UI is hard to brand, and there is no easy export of password hashes if you want to leave. Auth0 is nicer to use and considerably more expensive; building your own auth is almost always the wrong call.
    Tenant in the tokenCustom claim via pre-token triggerDatabase lookup per requestNo DB round-trip on the hot path, and the claim is tamper-proof. Cost: changing a user's tenant requires token expiry (which is why the attribute is immutable — tenant moves are a re-provision, not an edit).
    Access token lifetime15 minutes1 hour (Cognito default)JWTs cannot be revoked mid-life without a shared blocklist, so lifetime is your revocation window. 15 minutes bounds the damage from a stolen token; refresh-token rotation keeps the experience smooth.
    Refresh token storagehttpOnly Secure cookie via a tiny BFF endpointlocalStoragelocalStorage is XSS-readable, turning one script injection into persistent account takeover. The cookie approach costs one small backend route and needs CSRF consideration — worth it.
    Authorisation modelRole hierarchy + OAuth scopesFull ABAC / policy engineThree roles cover the product today. If entitlements got genuinely complex I'd reach for Cedar or OPA rather than growing conditionals — the path I would take if entitlements got complex.

    §8Interview questions on this material

    How does your API validate a JWT, and why not just ask Cognito?

    Cognito signs tokens with an RSA private key and publishes the corresponding public keys as a JWKS document at https://cognito-idp.{region}.amazonaws.com/{poolId}/.well-known/jwks.json. My API fetches that once at boot, caches it, and verifies each token locally: check the kid header to select the key, verify the RS256 signature, then validate the claims — exp and nbf, iss matches my pool, aud or client_id matches my app client, and critically token_use === 'access' so an ID token can't be substituted.

    Calling Cognito per request would add a network round-trip to every API call, make Cognito a hard availability dependency for reads, and consume request quota. Offline verification is microseconds and works even if Cognito is having a bad day. The trade-off I accept: I can't revoke an individual token mid-life. A bearer token is valid because its signature is valid, and nothing else is consulted. My mitigations are a 15-minute access-token lifetime, refresh-token revocation for sign-out and compromise, and — if I needed instant revocation — a small deny-list in ElastiCache checked by jti, which reintroduces a lookup but only for the revocation case.

    What's the difference between the ID token and the access token, and which does your API accept?

    The ID token is an authentication artefact: it asserts who the user is, and its audience is the client application. The access token is an authorization artefact: it says what the bearer may do — scopes, groups — and its audience is the resource server.

    My API accepts only the access token, and enforces that by checking token_use. This matters more than it sounds. If an API accepts ID tokens, it's authorising based on a token that was minted for a different audience, which breaks the OAuth trust model — and in a system with multiple clients, an ID token issued for client A would be accepted by an API that only ever intended to trust client B's tokens. Accepting either is one of the most common real-world OIDC implementation bugs, and I have an explicit test asserting that an ID token presented as a bearer credential returns 401.

    How do you handle multi-tenancy in the identity layer?

    One user pool for all tenants, with the tenant id as an immutable custom attribute on the user, injected into both the ID and access tokens by a pre-token-generation Lambda. The API reads the tenant from that verified claim only — never from a header, query parameter, or body field. I have a test that sends a valid token for tenant A alongside an X-Tenant-Id: B header and asserts that only tenant A's data comes back.

    Why the attribute is immutable matters: if a tenant id could be edited, an admin-console mistake — or an attribute-update API path — becomes a cross-tenant data breach. Moving a user between tenants is a deliberate re-provision, not a field edit.

    The alternative designs, and why not: a user pool per tenant gives the hardest isolation and per-tenant branding and federation, but you hit a soft limit of 1,000 pools per account, your API needs dynamic issuer resolution, and cross-tenant admin tooling becomes painful. Tenant encoded in the username is fragile and leaks. One pool with a verified claim, plus row-level security in the database as the backstop, gives me isolation at two independent layers for a fraction of the operational cost.

    A user's access is revoked. How quickly does that take effect, and is that acceptable?

    Honest answer: up to the remaining life of their current access token, so at most 15 minutes, plus whatever my user cache TTL is — which is why that cache is 60 seconds, not an hour. Revoking the refresh token via RevokeToken or a global sign-out prevents renewal immediately, so the session cannot extend past the current token.

    Whether that's acceptable is a product question, and framing it that way is the point. For document access in a B2B platform, a 15-minute window on a deliberate offboarding is generally fine, and it's the standard trade-off every stateless-JWT system makes. If the requirement were immediate revocation — say, for a compromised account or a regulated environment — the options are: shorten access tokens further and pay the refresh traffic; maintain a revocation list in ElastiCache keyed by jti or user id and check it per request, accepting a cache lookup on the hot path; or move to opaque tokens with introspection, which is really just choosing stateful sessions and paying the latency.

    What I would not do is pretend the window doesn't exist. Knowing your revocation window and being able to justify it is the senior answer.

    What is PKCE and why does a single-page app need it?

    PKCE — Proof Key for Code Exchange — replaces the client secret for public clients. A browser app can't hold a secret: view-source, or read the bundle. Without PKCE, an attacker who intercepts the authorization code from the redirect (a malicious browser extension, a logged referrer, a registered custom URL scheme on mobile) could exchange it for tokens themselves.

    With PKCE, the app generates a high-entropy random code_verifier, sends SHA256(verifier) as the code_challenge when starting the flow, and must present the original verifier when redeeming the code. The authorization server checks that the hash matches. An intercepted code is useless without the verifier, which never leaves the originating app.

    Worth adding: PKCE is now recommended for confidential clients too, and the implicit flow — which returned tokens directly in the URL fragment — is deprecated precisely because tokens ended up in browser history, referrer headers, and server logs. If someone's OAuth implementation uses response_type=token, that's a finding.

    §9Definition of done

    Sprint 04 · The product

    The upload and event pipeline

    The heart of the system, and the sprint that most distinguishes a portfolio project from a tutorial. Bytes go browser → S3 directly, never through your API. An event fans out through EventBridge to SQS, and a Lambda picks it up — idempotently, with retries, with a dead-letter queue, and with a story for every failure mode.

    Time
    2–3 days
    You'll produce
    Presigned uploads, EventBridge rule, SQS + DLQ, ingest Lambda, alarms
    Running cost
    ≈ $0 free tiers cover a portfolio's volume
    Key idea
    At-least-once delivery means idempotency is not optional

    §1Requirements

    • Files up to 50 MB (200 MB via multipart later) upload directly to S3 without transiting the API.
    • Only allowed MIME types; oversize or wrong-type uploads are rejected or quarantined.
    • The API records the document row before the upload, so an abandoned upload is visible and sweepable.
    • When S3 confirms the object, processing starts automatically within seconds.
    • Duplicate events must not produce duplicate work or duplicate database rows.
    • Transient failures retry with backoff; permanent failures land in a DLQ with enough context to replay.
    • Operators can see queue depth, message age, and DLQ count, and get paged when they're wrong.

    §2Architecture, and the two decisions in it

    Browser1. POST /uploads
    →
    API2. row + presigned PUT
    →
    Browser3. PUT bytes
    →
    S3 raw4. object created
    S3 event5.
    →
    EventBridge6. rule + filter
    →
    SQS ingest7. buffer
    →
    Lambda8. validate, dedupe
    →
    Textract9. start job
    ↘
    DLQafter 3 attempts

    Decision 1: presigned PUT, not upload-through-API

    ApproachProsConsVerdict
    Bytes through the APISimple; you can scan/validate content inlineYour containers carry the bandwidth; memory pressure; ALB has a hard 60s idle default; you pay for compute during transfer; scaling is coupled to upload volumeNo
    Presigned PUTBytes never touch your compute; S3 absorbs the load and the durability; simplest client codeOne URL per object; conditions are limited to headers you signChosen
    Presigned POST policyRich conditions — content-length range, key prefix constraint, exact content-typeMultipart form encoding is fiddlier in the clientGood alternative; strictly more expressive
    S3 Transfer Acceleration / multipartNecessary above ~100 MB or for far-away clientsMore client complexity; acceleration costs extra per GBSprint 11 extension

    Decision 2: EventBridge in the middle, not S3 → SQS directly

    S3 can notify SQS directly, which is one fewer moving part. We add EventBridge anyway, and the reasoning is the interesting part:

    • Fan-out without touching the bucket. S3 bucket notifications are configured on the bucket; adding a second consumer means editing bucket config (and historically, overlapping-prefix rules conflicted). With EventBridge, a new consumer is a new rule — the bucket never changes.
    • Content-based filtering. Rules can match on key prefix/suffix, object size, or any event field, so junk never reaches your queue and you don't pay Lambda invocations to discard it.
    • Archive and replay. EventBridge archives let you replay a window of events after fixing a bug — genuinely valuable, and hard to retrofit.
    • Uniform event envelope. Later events (Textract completion via SNS, scheduled sweeps, custom domain events) all flow through the same bus with the same shape.

    What it costs: one extra hop (tens of milliseconds), $1 per million events, and one more thing that can be misconfigured. For a system that will grow more consumers, it's worth it. If asked, say both — "direct S3→SQS is correct for a single consumer and I'd use it there".

    §2bFundamentals: queues, and the guarantees they do and do not give

    A queue looks simple and has sharp edges. Four properties determine how you have to write the consumer.

    Delivery semantics

    SQS standard queues are at-least-once. A message can be delivered twice, occasionally more. This is not a bug or a rare edge case; it is the documented behaviour, and it happens for mundane reasons: your consumer succeeded but crashed before acknowledging, or the visibility timeout expired while work was still in progress, or an internal retry occurred.

    Exactly-once delivery is not something a distributed queue can provide. What you can build is effectively-once processing, by making the consumer idempotent. That is why the ingest worker claims a dedupe key before doing anything else.

    FIFO queues offer ordering and deduplication within a five-minute window, at a much lower throughput ceiling and with a required message group id. We do not use one, because our documents are independent, ordering does not matter and throughput does.

    Visibility timeout

    When a consumer receives a message, the queue hides it from other consumers for the visibility timeout rather than deleting it. If the consumer deletes it in time, it is gone. If the consumer dies, the timeout expires and the message reappears for someone else. That is the retry mechanism, and it is why the timeout must exceed your processing time with margin. With a Lambda event source mapping, the guidance is at least six times the function timeout.

    The redrive policy

    maxReceiveCount is how many times a message may be received before the queue moves it to the dead-letter queue. It counts receives, not failures, so a message that times out repeatedly consumes attempts without your code ever completing. Three is a reasonable default: enough to ride out a transient failure, few enough that a permanently broken message surfaces quickly instead of being retried for hours.

    Long polling

    With receive_wait_time_seconds at zero, a poll returns immediately even when the queue is empty, so you pay for empty receives and get more of them. Setting it to 20 makes the request wait until a message arrives or the time expires. Fewer requests, lower cost, lower latency. There is no reason to leave it at zero.

    The mental model

    A queue is not a pipe that transports messages. It is a durable store with a lease protocol. Consumers take a lease on a message, and the message returns to the pool if the lease expires without a delete. Once you picture it that way, duplicate delivery stops being surprising and idempotency stops feeling like defensive programming.

    §3Issuing the presigned URL

    TypeScriptapps/api/src/routes/uploads.ts
    import type { FastifyPluginAsyncZod } from 'fastify-type-provider-zod';
import { z } from 'zod';
import { PutObjectCommand } from '@aws-sdk/client-s3';
import { getSignedUrl } from '@aws-sdk/s3-request-presigner';
import { and, eq, isNull, sql } from 'drizzle-orm';
import { documents } from '@idp/db/schema';
import { config } from '../config.js';

const ALLOWED = new Map<string, string>([
  ['application/pdf', 'pdf'],
  ['image/png', 'png'],
  ['image/jpeg', 'jpg'],
  ['image/tiff', 'tiff'],
]);

export const uploadRoutes: FastifyPluginAsyncZod = async (app) => {
  app.post('/', {
    preHandler: [app.requireAuth, app.requireRole('member')],
    schema: {
      body: z.object({
        filename: z.string().min(1).max(255),
        mimeType: z.string().refine((m) => ALLOWED.has(m), 'Unsupported file type'),
        byteSize: z.number().int().positive().max(config.MAX_UPLOAD_BYTES),
        // The browser computes sha256 before uploading. Gives us dedupe and
        // lets S3 verify integrity on arrival.
        checksumSha256: z.string().regex(/^[A-Za-z0-9+/]{43}=$/).optional(),
      }),
      response: {
        201: z.object({
          documentId: z.string().uuid(),
          uploadUrl: z.string().url(),
          expiresInSeconds: z.number(),
          requiredHeaders: z.record(z.string(), z.string()),
        }),
        409: z.object({ error: z.object({ code: z.string(), message: z.string(),
                                          documentId: z.string().uuid().optional() }) }),
      },
    },
  }, async (req, reply) => {
    const { filename, mimeType, byteSize, checksumSha256 } = req.body;
    const tenantId = req.tenant.id;

    // ── Quota check. Cheap aggregate; index on (tenant_id, created_at) covers it.
    const [{ used }] = await app.withTenant(tenantId, (tx) =>
      tx.select({ used: sql<number>`coalesce(sum(${documents.byteSize}), 0)::bigint` })
        .from(documents)
        .where(and(eq(documents.tenantId, tenantId), isNull(documents.deletedAt))),
    );
    if (Number(used) + byteSize > req.tenant.quotaBytes) {
      throw app.httpErrors.forbidden('Storage quota exceeded');
    }

    // ── Content-addressed dedupe: same bytes, same tenant, don't store twice.
    if (checksumSha256) {
      const [existing] = await app.withTenant(tenantId, (tx) =>
        tx.select({ id: documents.id, status: documents.status })
          .from(documents)
          .where(and(
            eq(documents.tenantId, tenantId),
            eq(documents.checksum, checksumSha256),
            isNull(documents.deletedAt),
          ))
          .limit(1),
      );
      if (existing && existing.status !== 'pending_upload') {
        return reply.code(409).send({
          error: { code: 'duplicate_document',
                   message: 'This file has already been uploaded',
                   documentId: existing.id },
        });
      }
    }

    const documentId = crypto.randomUUID();
    // Key structure carries the tenant, which is what lets IAM policies and
    // EventBridge rules reason about ownership from the key alone.
    const ext = ALLOWED.get(mimeType)!;
    const key = `tenant/${tenantId}/${documentId}.${ext}`;

    // ── Row first, bytes second. An abandoned upload leaves a pending_upload
    // row we can sweep, rather than an orphan object nobody knows about.
    await app.withTenant(tenantId, (tx) =>
      tx.insert(documents).values({
        id: documentId,
        tenantId,
        uploadedBy: req.principal.userId,
        filename,
        mimeType,
        byteSize,
        checksum: checksumSha256 ?? null,
        s3Bucket: config.RAW_BUCKET,
        s3Key: key,
        status: 'pending_upload',
      }),
    );

    // ── The presigned URL. Every signed header becomes a constraint the
    // client MUST match exactly, or S3 rejects the PUT with 403.
    const command = new PutObjectCommand({
      Bucket: config.RAW_BUCKET,
      Key: key,
      ContentType: mimeType,
      ContentLength: byteSize,
      ChecksumSHA256: checksumSha256,
      ServerSideEncryption: 'aws:kms',
      SSEKMSKeyId: config.KMS_KEY_ID,
      // Metadata travels with the object and reappears in the S3 event, so the
      // worker knows the tenant and document without a database lookup first.
      Metadata: {
        'tenant-id': tenantId,
        'document-id': documentId,
        'uploaded-by': req.principal.userId,
      },
    });

    const uploadUrl = await getSignedUrl(app.s3, command, { expiresIn: 900 });

    // NOTE: uploadUrl contains a signature. It is in the pino redact list —
    // never log it, never put it in an error message.
    req.log.info({ documentId, tenantId, byteSize, mimeType }, 'upload authorised');

    return reply.code(201).send({
      documentId,
      uploadUrl,
      expiresInSeconds: 900,
      requiredHeaders: {
        'Content-Type': mimeType,
        'x-amz-server-side-encryption': 'aws:kms',
        'x-amz-server-side-encryption-aws-kms-key-id': config.KMS_KEY_ID!,
        ...(checksumSha256 ? { 'x-amz-checksum-sha256': checksumSha256 } : {}),
      },
    });
  });
};
    The #1 presigned-URL support ticket

    403 SignatureDoesNotMatch. Cause, almost always: the client sent a header set that differs from what was signed — a missing Content-Type, an extra header the browser added, or a Content-Length mismatch. Every header you include in the command becomes part of the signature. Return the exact required headers to the client (as above) and have the client send exactly those. Second most common cause: clock skew > 15 minutes on the signing machine.

    §4Terraform: queue, DLQ, and the rule

    HCLinfra/modules/queue/main.tf
    locals {
  name = "${var.project}-${var.environment}-${var.queue_name}"
}

# Dead-letter queue first: the main queue references it.
# 14 days is the maximum SQS retention — you want the full window to
# investigate and replay a bad batch.
resource "aws_sqs_queue" "dlq" {
  name                      = "${local.name}-dlq"
  message_retention_seconds = 1209600
  sqs_managed_sse_enabled   = true
}

resource "aws_sqs_queue" "main" {
  name = local.name

  # ── The single most important number here ──────────────────────────────
  # Visibility timeout MUST be >= 6x the Lambda function timeout when using
  # an event source mapping. Lambda's own retry behaviour plus the batch
  # window means a shorter value causes the same message to be delivered to a
  # second Lambda while the first is still processing it — duplicate work,
  # and if you're not idempotent, duplicate data.
  visibility_timeout_seconds = var.consumer_timeout_seconds * 6

  message_retention_seconds  = 345600      # 4 days
  receive_wait_time_seconds  = 20          # long polling: fewer empty receives, lower cost
  sqs_managed_sse_enabled    = true

  redrive_policy = jsonencode({
    deadLetterTargetArn = aws_sqs_queue.dlq.arn
    # 3 attempts. Enough to ride out a transient blip, few enough that a
    # broken message reaches the DLQ within a minute or two.
    maxReceiveCount = 3
  })
}

# Let the DLQ be redriven back into the main queue (the console "redrive" button
# and the StartMessageMoveTask API both need this).
resource "aws_sqs_queue_redrive_allow_policy" "dlq" {
  queue_url = aws_sqs_queue.dlq.id
  redrive_allow_policy = jsonencode({
    redrivePermission = "byQueue"
    sourceQueueArns   = [aws_sqs_queue.main.arn]
  })
}

# Queue policy: only our EventBridge rule may send. Without SourceArn, any
# EventBridge rule in the account could target this queue.
data "aws_iam_policy_document" "queue" {
  statement {
    sid     = "AllowEventBridgeRule"
    effect  = "Allow"
    actions = ["sqs:SendMessage"]
    resources = [aws_sqs_queue.main.arn]

    principals {
      type        = "Service"
      identifiers = ["events.amazonaws.com"]
    }
    condition {
      test     = "ArnEquals"
      variable = "aws:SourceArn"
      values   = [aws_cloudwatch_event_rule.object_created.arn]
    }
  }
}

resource "aws_sqs_queue_policy" "main" {
  queue_url = aws_sqs_queue.main.id
  policy    = data.aws_iam_policy_document.queue.json
}

# ───────────────────────── EventBridge rule ─────────────────────────
resource "aws_cloudwatch_event_rule" "object_created" {
  name        = "${local.name}-object-created"
  description = "Raw bucket object created under a tenant prefix"

  event_pattern = jsonencode({
    source      = ["aws.s3"]
    detail-type = ["Object Created"]
    detail = {
      bucket = {
        name = [var.raw_bucket_name]
      }
      object = {
        # Only real tenant uploads. Staging junk and anything outside the
        # prefix never costs us a Lambda invocation.
        key = [{ prefix = "tenant/" }]
        # Reject empty objects at the bus, not in code.
        size = [{ numeric = [">", 0] }]
      }
    }
  })
}

resource "aws_cloudwatch_event_target" "to_sqs" {
  rule      = aws_cloudwatch_event_rule.object_created.name
  target_id = "ingest-queue"
  arn       = aws_sqs_queue.main.arn

  # If EventBridge cannot deliver to SQS (throttling, permissions), retry then
  # park the *event* in its own DLQ. Without this, the event is silently lost.
  dead_letter_config {
    arn = aws_sqs_queue.dlq.arn
  }
  retry_policy {
    maximum_event_age_in_seconds = 3600
    maximum_retry_attempts       = 20
  }
}

# Archive so we can replay a time window after fixing a consumer bug.
resource "aws_cloudwatch_event_archive" "s3_events" {
  name             = "${local.name}-archive"
  event_source_arn = data.aws_cloudwatch_event_bus.default.arn
  retention_days   = 30
  event_pattern = jsonencode({
    source      = ["aws.s3"]
    detail-type = ["Object Created"]
  })
}

data "aws_cloudwatch_event_bus" "default" {
  name = "default"
}
    HCLinfra/modules/lambda-worker/main.tf (excerpt)
    resource "aws_lambda_function" "worker" {
  function_name = "${var.project}-${var.environment}-${var.worker_name}"
  role          = aws_iam_role.worker.arn

  # Container image keeps the runtime identical to local dev and sidesteps
  # the 250MB unzipped layer limit. Zip is fine and faster to deploy for
  # small handlers — a legitimate either/or.
  package_type = "Image"
  image_uri    = var.image_uri

  timeout     = var.timeout_seconds       # ingest: 60s. Textract polling: 300s.
  memory_size = var.memory_mb             # 512MB. CPU scales with memory in Lambda.
  architectures = ["arm64"]               # Graviton: ~20% cheaper per GB-second

  environment {
    variables = merge(var.env, {
      NODE_OPTIONS = "--enable-source-maps"
      # Keep connections warm between invocations on the same container.
      AWS_NODEJS_CONNECTION_REUSE_ENABLED = "1"
    })
  }

  # In a VPC only if it needs RDS. VPC attachment adds ENI setup on cold start
  # and requires NAT or endpoints for AWS API access — do not do it by default.
  dynamic "vpc_config" {
    for_each = var.subnet_ids == null ? [] : [1]
    content {
      subnet_ids         = var.subnet_ids
      security_group_ids = var.security_group_ids
    }
  }

  tracing_config {
    mode = "Active"                        # X-Ray
  }

  # Asynchronous invocation failures (not SQS-sourced) go here.
  dead_letter_config {
    target_arn = var.failure_dlq_arn
  }

  logging_config {
    log_format = "JSON"                    # structured logs without a shim
    application_log_level = "INFO"
    system_log_level      = "WARN"
  }
}

resource "aws_lambda_event_source_mapping" "sqs" {
  event_source_arn = var.queue_arn
  function_name    = aws_lambda_function.worker.arn

  batch_size = 10
  # Wait up to 5s to fill a batch: fewer invocations, better throughput.
  maximum_batching_window_in_seconds = 5

  # CRITICAL: without this, one bad message in a batch of 10 causes all 10 to
  # be retried and eventually all 10 to hit the DLQ. With it, the handler
  # reports individual failures and only those are retried.
  function_response_types = ["ReportBatchItemFailures"]

  scaling_config {
    # Bound concurrency so a 10k-document burst can't exhaust the RDS
    # connection limit or blow through a downstream API quota.
    maximum_concurrency = var.max_concurrency
  }
}

resource "aws_cloudwatch_log_group" "worker" {
  name              = "/aws/lambda/${aws_lambda_function.worker.function_name}"
  retention_in_days = var.environment == "prod" ? 30 : 7
  kms_key_id        = var.log_kms_key_arn
}
    Two settings people forget, and what happens

    No ReportBatchItemFailures: one poison message makes nine healthy documents fail with it, three times over, then all ten land in the DLQ. You'll spend an hour confused. No log group in Terraform: Lambda creates one implicitly with never expire retention, and your CloudWatch Logs bill grows forever. Declaring the log group explicitly is also what lets you set retention and encryption.

    §5The ingest worker

    This is the code that has to be right. Read the idempotency section twice.

    TypeScriptservices/ingest-worker/src/handler.ts
    import type { SQSEvent, SQSBatchResponse, SQSBatchItemFailure, Context } from 'aws-lambda';
import { HeadObjectCommand, S3Client } from '@aws-sdk/client-s3';
import { StartDocumentAnalysisCommand, TextractClient } from '@aws-sdk/client-textract';
import { and, eq, sql } from 'drizzle-orm';
import { documents, processingJobs } from '@idp/db/schema';
import { canTransition, IllegalTransitionError } from '@idp/shared/document-status';
import { getDb } from './db.js';
import { logger } from './logger.js';

const s3 = new S3Client({});
const textract = new TextractClient({});

/** EventBridge wraps the S3 event; SQS wraps that. Two layers of envelope. */
interface S3ObjectCreated {
  id: string;                 // EventBridge event id — our idempotency key
  time: string;
  detail: {
    bucket: { name: string };
    object: { key: string; size: number; etag: string; 'version-id'?: string };
  };
}

const RETRYABLE = new Set([
  'ThrottlingException', 'ProvisionedThroughputExceededException',
  'ServiceUnavailable', 'InternalServerError', 'TooManyRequestsException',
  'ECONNRESET', 'ETIMEDOUT', 'RequestTimeout',
]);

export async function handler(event: SQSEvent, ctx: Context): Promise<SQSBatchResponse> {
  const failures: SQSBatchItemFailure[] = [];
  const db = await getDb();

  // Sequential, not Promise.all: bounded database connections and predictable
  // Textract call rate matter more than shaving a few hundred milliseconds.
  for (const record of event.Records) {
    const log = logger.child({
      messageId: record.messageId,
      requestId: ctx.awsRequestId,
      receiveCount: record.attributes.ApproximateReceiveCount,
    });

    try {
      const envelope = JSON.parse(record.body) as S3ObjectCreated;
      await processObject(db, envelope, log);
    } catch (err) {
      const e = err as { name?: string; message?: string };

      if (err instanceof IllegalTransitionError) {
        // A duplicate or out-of-order event. Not an error worth retrying:
        // acknowledge it and move on, but record it so an unexpected spike
        // shows up in metrics.
        log.warn({ from: err.from, to: err.to }, 'skipping illegal transition');
        continue;
      }

      const retryable = RETRYABLE.has(e.name ?? '') ||
                        (e.message ?? '').includes('timeout');

      if (retryable) {
        log.warn({ err: e }, 'retryable failure — returning to queue');
        failures.push({ itemIdentifier: record.messageId });
      } else {
        // Permanent failure: mark the document failed so the UI shows why,
        // then ACK the message. Sending an unprocessable message back to the
        // queue just burns three attempts before the DLQ anyway.
        log.error({ err: e }, 'permanent failure — marking document failed');
        await markFailed(db, record, e.message ?? 'unknown error').catch((e2) =>
          log.error({ err: e2 }, 'could not mark document failed'));
      }
    }
  }

  // Only these message ids are redelivered. Everything else is acknowledged.
  return { batchItemFailures: failures };
}

async function processObject(db: Awaited<ReturnType<typeof getDb>>,
                             envelope: S3ObjectCreated,
                             log: typeof logger) {
  const key = decodeURIComponent(envelope.detail.object.key.replace(/\+/g, ' '));
  const bucket = envelope.detail.bucket.name;

  // ═══════════════════ IDEMPOTENCY GATE ═══════════════════
  // The EventBridge event id is stable across redeliveries of the same event,
  // so it is our dedupe key. INSERT ... ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING is atomic:
  // exactly one concurrent invocation gets rowCount 1 and proceeds. This is a
  // database-enforced lock, not a check-then-act race.
  const claimed = await db
    .insert(processingJobs)
    .values({ jobType: 'ingest', dedupeKey: envelope.id })
    .onConflictDoNothing()
    .returning({ dedupeKey: processingJobs.dedupeKey });

  if (claimed.length === 0) {
    log.info({ eventId: envelope.id, key }, 'duplicate event — already claimed');
    return;
  }
  // ════════════════════════════════════════════════════════

  // HEAD the object: authoritative size, content type, and our metadata.
  // Never trust the event payload alone for anything security-relevant.
  const head = await s3.send(new HeadObjectCommand({ Bucket: bucket, Key: key }));
  const documentId = head.Metadata?.['document-id'];
  const tenantId = head.Metadata?.['tenant-id'];

  if (!documentId || !tenantId) {
    // Object uploaded outside our flow. Quarantine rather than process.
    log.error({ key }, 'object missing required metadata — quarantining');
    throw new Error('missing document metadata');
  }

  const [doc] = await db.select().from(documents)
    .where(and(eq(documents.id, documentId), eq(documents.tenantId, tenantId)))
    .limit(1);
  if (!doc) throw new Error(`no document row for ${documentId}`);

  // Validate against what we authorised, not what arrived.
  if (head.ContentLength !== doc.byteSize && doc.byteSize != null) {
    log.warn({ expected: doc.byteSize, actual: head.ContentLength }, 'size mismatch');
  }

  // ── pending_upload -> uploaded
  if (!canTransition(doc.status as never, 'uploaded')) {
    throw new IllegalTransitionError(doc.status as never, 'uploaded');
  }
  await db.update(documents)
    .set({ status: 'uploaded', byteSize: head.ContentLength ?? doc.byteSize,
           s3VersionId: head.VersionId ?? null })
    .where(eq(documents.id, documentId));

  // ── uploaded -> extracting, and kick off Textract
  // ClientRequestToken makes StartDocumentAnalysis itself idempotent for 7
  // days: a retried call with the same token returns the same JobId rather
  // than starting (and billing) a second job.
  const started = await textract.send(new StartDocumentAnalysisCommand({
    DocumentLocation: { S3Object: { Bucket: bucket, Name: key, Version: head.VersionId } },
    FeatureTypes: ['FORMS', 'TABLES'],
    ClientRequestToken: documentId,
    JobTag: tenantId,
    NotificationChannel: {
      SNSTopicArn: process.env.TEXTRACT_SNS_TOPIC_ARN!,
      RoleArn: process.env.TEXTRACT_PUBLISH_ROLE_ARN!,
    },
    OutputConfig: {
      S3Bucket: process.env.DERIVED_BUCKET!,
      S3Prefix: `textract/${tenantId}/${documentId}`,
    },
  }));

  await db.update(documents)
    .set({ status: 'extracting' })
    .where(and(eq(documents.id, documentId), eq(documents.status, 'uploaded')));

  await db.update(processingJobs)
    .set({ succeededAt: sql`now()`, documentId })
    .where(and(eq(processingJobs.jobType, 'ingest'),
               eq(processingJobs.dedupeKey, envelope.id)));

  // EMF metric: one line of JSON becomes a CloudWatch custom metric with no
  // extra API call. See Sprint 08.
  log.info({
    _aws: {
      Timestamp: Date.now(),
      CloudWatchMetrics: [{
        Namespace: 'IDP/Ingest',
        Dimensions: [['Environment']],
        Metrics: [{ Name: 'DocumentsIngested', Unit: 'Count' }],
      }],
    },
    Environment: process.env.ENVIRONMENT,
    DocumentsIngested: 1,
    documentId, tenantId, textractJobId: started.JobId,
  }, 'textract job started');
}

    Why that idempotency gate works

    The obvious approach is "check whether this was processed, then process it". That is a race. Two invocations both check, both see nothing, both proceed. Making the claim atomic fixes it: a single INSERT ... ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING against a unique key. Postgres serialises the insert, so exactly one caller gets a row back and every other caller gets zero rows and returns early. The whole guarantee rests on that one statement, which is why it is the first thing the handler does.

    Three layers of idempotency here
    1. The claim row keyed on the EventBridge event id — stops duplicate processing.
    2. The status transition guard with a WHERE status = 'uploaded' conditional update — stops out-of-order state corruption.
    3. Textract's ClientRequestToken — stops duplicate billing, even if our own guards fail.

    Three layers, each preventing a different thing. If one has a bug, the other two still hold.

    §6Testing the pipeline

    TypeScriptservices/ingest-worker/test/handler.test.ts
    import { mockClient } from 'aws-sdk-client-mock';
import { S3Client, HeadObjectCommand } from '@aws-sdk/client-s3';
import { TextractClient, StartDocumentAnalysisCommand } from '@aws-sdk/client-textract';
import { handler } from '../src/handler.js';

const s3Mock = mockClient(S3Client);
const txMock = mockClient(TextractClient);

beforeEach(() => { s3Mock.reset(); txMock.reset(); });

function sqsRecord(eventId: string, key: string, receiveCount = '1') {
  return {
    messageId: `msg-${eventId}`,
    receiptHandle: 'rh',
    attributes: { ApproximateReceiveCount: receiveCount } as never,
    body: JSON.stringify({
      id: eventId,
      time: new Date().toISOString(),
      detail: { bucket: { name: 'raw' },
                object: { key, size: 1234, etag: 'abc' } },
    }),
    messageAttributes: {}, md5OfBody: '', eventSource: 'aws:sqs',
    eventSourceARN: 'arn', awsRegion: 'eu-west-1',
  };
}

it('starts exactly one Textract job for a duplicated event', async () => {
  const { docId, tenantId } = await seedPendingDocument();
  s3Mock.on(HeadObjectCommand).resolves({
    ContentLength: 1234, VersionId: 'v1',
    Metadata: { 'document-id': docId, 'tenant-id': tenantId },
  });
  txMock.on(StartDocumentAnalysisCommand).resolves({ JobId: 'job-1' });

  const rec = sqsRecord('evt-1', `tenant/${tenantId}/${docId}.pdf`);

  // Same event delivered twice — exactly what SQS at-least-once means.
  await handler({ Records: [rec] } as never, ctx());
  await handler({ Records: [rec] } as never, ctx());

  expect(txMock.commandCalls(StartDocumentAnalysisCommand)).toHaveLength(1);
  expect(await statusOf(docId)).toBe('extracting');
});

it('fails only the bad message in a mixed batch', async () => {
  const good = await seedPendingDocument();
  s3Mock.on(HeadObjectCommand).callsFake((input) => {
    if (String(input.Key).includes('boom')) {
      const e = new Error('slow down'); e.name = 'ThrottlingException'; throw e;
    }
    return { ContentLength: 10, Metadata: { 'document-id': good.docId,
                                            'tenant-id': good.tenantId } };
  });
  txMock.on(StartDocumentAnalysisCommand).resolves({ JobId: 'job-2' });

  const res = await handler({
    Records: [
      sqsRecord('evt-ok',   `tenant/${good.tenantId}/${good.docId}.pdf`),
      sqsRecord('evt-boom', `tenant/${good.tenantId}/boom.pdf`),
    ],
  } as never, ctx());

  // Precisely one retry, and it's the throttled one.
  expect(res.batchItemFailures).toEqual([{ itemIdentifier: 'msg-evt-boom' }]);
  expect(await statusOf(good.docId)).toBe('extracting');
});

it('acknowledges a permanently broken message instead of retrying it', async () => {
  s3Mock.on(HeadObjectCommand).resolves({ ContentLength: 5, Metadata: {} }); // no metadata
  const res = await handler({
    Records: [sqsRecord('evt-nometa', 'tenant/x/y.pdf')],
  } as never, ctx());
  expect(res.batchItemFailures).toHaveLength(0);   // do not waste 3 attempts
});

    End-to-end against LocalStack, then against real dev:

    Shellmanual pipeline verification
    # 1. Request an upload URL
RESP=$(curl -s -X POST https://api-dev.idp.example.com/v1/uploads \
  -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" -H 'content-type: application/json' \
  -d '{"filename":"invoice.pdf","mimeType":"application/pdf","byteSize":48213}')
URL=$(jq -r .uploadUrl <<<"$RESP"); DOC=$(jq -r .documentId <<<"$RESP")

# 2. Upload with EXACTLY the headers the API told us to send
curl -s -X PUT "$URL" \
  -H 'content-type: application/pdf' \
  -H 'x-amz-server-side-encryption: aws:kms' \
  -H "x-amz-server-side-encryption-aws-kms-key-id: $KMS_ARN" \
  --data-binary @invoice.pdf -w '%{http_code}\n'          # expect 200

# 3. Watch the status advance (should reach 'extracting' within ~5s)
for i in $(seq 1 12); do
  curl -s "https://api-dev.idp.example.com/v1/documents/$DOC" \
    -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" | jq -r .status
  sleep 2
done

# 4. Queue health
aws sqs get-queue-attributes --queue-url "$QUEUE_URL" \
  --attribute-names ApproximateNumberOfMessages \
    ApproximateNumberOfMessagesNotVisible ApproximateAgeOfOldestMessage

# 5. Anything in the DLQ? (should be zero)
aws sqs get-queue-attributes --queue-url "$DLQ_URL" \
  --attribute-names ApproximateNumberOfMessages

# 6. Replaying the DLQ after a fix — this is the runbook step
aws sqs start-message-move-task \
  --source-arn "$DLQ_ARN" --destination-arn "$QUEUE_ARN" \
  --max-number-of-messages-per-second 5

    §7Monitoring this pipeline

    AlarmMetricThresholdWhy it matters
    DLQ has anything in itApproximateNumberOfMessagesVisible on DLQ> 0 for 5 minDocuments are being lost. Highest-priority alarm in the system.
    Queue backing upApproximateAgeOfOldestMessage> 300 sBetter than queue depth: catches a slow consumer even at low volume. Depth alone can look fine while messages sit.
    Worker erroringLambda Errors / Invocations> 5% for 5 minUse the ratio, not the count — 10 errors means nothing without the denominator.
    Worker timing outLambda Duration p99> 80% of timeoutWarns before timeouts start, which is when duplicate delivery begins.
    Documents stuckCustom EMF metric from a scheduled queryany doc > 15 min in a non-terminal stateCatches the silent failure where nothing errored but nothing progressed. This is the alarm that catches what the others miss.
    ThrottlingLambda Throttles> 0Concurrency ceiling reached; capacity decision needed.

    resource "aws_cloudwatch_metric_alarm" "dlq_not_empty" {
  alarm_name          = "${local.name}-dlq-not-empty"
  alarm_description   = "Messages in the ingest DLQ — documents are failing permanently. Runbook: docs/runbooks/dlq.md"
  namespace           = "AWS/SQS"
  metric_name         = "ApproximateNumberOfMessagesVisible"
  dimensions          = { QueueName = aws_sqs_queue.dlq.name }
  statistic           = "Maximum"
  period              = 300
  evaluation_periods  = 1
  threshold           = 0
  comparison_operator = "GreaterThanThreshold"
  # A queue with no traffic reports no datapoints. notBreaching stops that
  # from showing as INSUFFICIENT_DATA forever.
  treat_missing_data  = "notBreaching"
  alarm_actions       = [var.alarm_topic_arn]
  ok_actions          = [var.alarm_topic_arn]
}

  namespace           = "AWS/SQS"

  dimensions          = { QueueName = aws_sqs_queue.main.name }
  statistic           = "Maximum"
  period              = 60
  evaluation_periods  = 5
  datapoints_to_alarm = 3          # 3 of 5: rides out a brief spike
  threshold           = 300
  comparison_operator = "GreaterThanThreshold"
  treat_missing_data  = "notBreaching"
  alarm_actions       = [var.alarm_topic_arn]
}

    §8Interview questions on this material

    Walk me through your upload flow and why bytes don't go through your API.

    The client POSTs metadata — filename, MIME type, size, and a client-computed SHA-256 — to /v1/uploads. The API validates the type against an allow-list, checks the tenant's storage quota, checks the checksum against existing documents for content dedupe, writes a pending_upload row, and returns a presigned PUT URL scoped to exactly one key under tenant/{id}/, valid for 15 minutes, with the encryption headers pre-signed. The browser PUTs the bytes straight to S3. S3 emits an event, EventBridge filters and routes it to SQS, and a Lambda advances the document to uploaded and starts Textract.

    Bytes bypass the API for four reasons. Bandwidth and memory don't touch my containers, so upload volume doesn't drive compute scaling. I'm not paying for Fargate CPU during a 50MB transfer over a slow connection. The ALB's idle timeout stops being a constraint. And S3 gives me multipart, resumability, and eleven-nines durability for free.

    The subtlety worth mentioning: I write the database row before issuing the URL, not after the upload completes. That means an abandoned upload leaves a visible pending_upload row I can sweep, rather than an orphan S3 object nobody knows about — and the S3 lifecycle rule cleans up incomplete multipart uploads. Getting the ordering the other way round is a common source of untracked storage cost.

    SQS delivers at least once. How do you make your consumer idempotent?

    Three independent layers, each preventing something different.

    One: an idempotency claim. Every EventBridge event carries a stable id that survives redelivery. My first action is INSERT INTO processing_jobs (job_type, dedupe_key) VALUES ('ingest', $eventId) ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING RETURNING dedupe_key. If zero rows come back, another invocation already claimed this event and I return immediately. This is atomic in the database — and it is not a check-then-act, which would race with a concurrent invocation. That distinction is the whole answer.

    Two: conditional state transitions. Status updates carry a WHERE status = 'uploaded' predicate, so an out-of-order event that tries to move a ready document backwards affects zero rows rather than corrupting state.

    Three: downstream idempotency tokens. StartDocumentAnalysis takes a ClientRequestToken; I pass the document id, so a duplicate call within seven days returns the existing job id instead of starting — and billing — a second OCR run. That layer protects the bill even when my own guards have a bug.

    And the operational detail that makes it hold: visibility timeout is set to six times the Lambda timeout. If it were shorter, a slow-but-succeeding invocation would have its message redelivered while still running — manufacturing the exact duplicates I'm defending against.

    Why EventBridge between S3 and SQS instead of a direct notification?

    Four reasons, and I'd concede up front that direct S3→SQS is the right call for a single consumer.

    Fan-out without bucket changes. S3 notifications are bucket configuration. Adding a second consumer means editing the bucket, and historically overlapping prefix rules conflicted. With EventBridge, a new consumer is a new rule — the bucket is never touched again, which matters because bucket config changes are riskier than adding a rule.

    Filtering at the bus. My rule matches prefix: "tenant/" and size > 0, so staging junk and zero-byte objects never reach the queue and never cost a Lambda invocation. S3 notification filters are limited to prefix and suffix only.

    Replay. An EventBridge archive lets me replay a time window after fixing a consumer bug. Without it, recovering from "the worker was broken for two hours" means reconstructing events from S3 inventory.

    A uniform envelope. Textract completions, scheduled sweeps, and my own domain events all flow through the same bus with the same shape, so one set of routing conventions covers everything.

    Costs: an extra hop of latency, $1 per million events, and one more misconfigurable thing. And a real gotcha — EventBridge targets need their own DLQ, because if EventBridge can't deliver to SQS the event is otherwise silently dropped. Most tutorials omit that.

    What happens when a document fails processing? Walk me through the recovery.

    It depends on the failure class, and separating them is the design.

    Transient — Textract throttling, a network reset, a brief database blip. The handler recognises the error name, returns that message id in batchItemFailures, and SQS redelivers it after the visibility timeout with exponential backoff. Three attempts. Critically, ReportBatchItemFailures means only the failing message retries — the other nine documents in the batch complete. Without that setting, one poison message fails all ten, three times, and then all ten land in the DLQ.

    Permanent — an encrypted PDF, a corrupt file, missing metadata. Retrying cannot help, so I mark the document failed with a reason the UI can display, and acknowledge the message. Burning three attempts on something unprocessable just delays every other document behind it.

    Unknown — anything unclassified retries three times and then lands in the DLQ, which is my highest-priority alarm. The DLQ retains for 14 days, the full maximum, because investigating and fixing can take days.

    Recovery is a documented runbook: read a few DLQ messages to find the pattern, fix the code, deploy, then StartMessageMoveTask to redrive the DLQ back into the main queue with a rate limit so I don't stampede Textract. If the documents were already marked failed, the failed → uploaded transition in my state machine exists precisely so an operator can requeue them. And because processing is idempotent, replaying something that actually succeeded is harmless — which is what makes the whole recovery path safe to run under pressure.

    How would this pipeline behave if 100,000 documents arrived at once?

    Let me walk the chain and name each limit. S3 is effectively unbounded — 3,500 PUTs per second per prefix, and my keys are spread across tenant prefixes. EventBridge handles the throughput; rules are evaluated per event. SQS is effectively unbounded and, importantly, acts as the shock absorber — depth grows, nothing breaks. That buffering is the reason the queue is there at all.

    Lambda is the first real constraint. The event source mapping scales up by adding pollers, but I've deliberately capped maximum_concurrency, because the two things behind Lambda don't scale the same way: RDS has a hard max_connections that a thousand concurrent Lambdas would exhaust, and Textract has an account-level concurrent-job quota. So the cap is protecting downstream, not Lambda itself.

    Textract becomes the bottleneck, and that's fine — the queue absorbs it and drains at whatever rate Textract allows. What I'd monitor is ApproximateAgeOfOldestMessage, because that directly tells me user-visible delay. If p99 time-to-ready exceeded the product's promise, my options are a quota increase, splitting into a priority queue plus a bulk queue so interactive uploads don't queue behind a 50,000-document backfill, or degrading gracefully by running cheap DetectDocumentText instead of full AnalyzeDocument under load.

    The failure mode I'd specifically watch for is retry amplification: if Textract starts throttling, every message retries, which increases the call rate, which increases throttling. The mitigations are jittered exponential backoff in the SDK config, the concurrency cap, and treating throttling as a scale-down signal rather than just a retry.

    §9Definition of done

    Sprint 05 · The product

    Textract: turning pixels into structure

    OCR is the step that makes everything else possible. The engineering content here is less about calling an API and more about the asynchronous callback pattern, parsing Textract's graph-shaped output, handling confidence honestly, and not accidentally spending $50 on a thousand pages you only needed plain text from.

    Time
    2 days
    You'll produce
    SNS callback wiring, block parser, extraction persistence, cost controls
    Running cost
    $1.50–$65 per 1k pages depending on features
    Key idea
    Pick the cheapest API that answers the question you actually have

    §1Which Textract API, and what it costs

    This table is the whole cost story of the platform. Learn it — the numbers come up in interviews.

    APIReturnsSync / async≈ cost / 1,000 pagesUse when
    DetectDocumentTextLines and words with geometryBoth~$1.50You only need searchable text. Default choice.
    AnalyzeDocument FORMS+ key/value pairsBoth~$50Structured fields matter and layout varies
    AnalyzeDocument TABLES+ table cells with row/columnBoth~$15Line items, statements
    AnalyzeDocument FORMS+TABLESBoth of the aboveBoth~$65Invoices where you need both
    AnalyzeExpensePurpose-built invoice/receipt fieldsBoth~$10Invoices and receipts specifically — cheaper and more accurate than FORMS for this
    AnalyzeIDPassport / driving licence fieldsSync only~$2.50 per 1kIdentity documents

    Prices are order-of-magnitude for eu-west-1 and tier down with volume — always check current pricing. The ratios are the durable insight.

    The 40× decision

    Running FORMS+TABLES on everything costs roughly 40× more than detecting text. So the pipeline does a two-stage classification: cheap DetectDocumentText first, ask the LLM to classify from that text, and only run the expensive structured analysis on document classes where the fields are genuinely needed (invoices, statements). For a contract, plain text plus an LLM summary is better and ~40× cheaper. One routing decision, roughly a fortyfold difference in the OCR line of your bill.

    Sync vs async — not a preference, a constraint

    SynchronousAsynchronous
    CallDetectDocumentText / AnalyzeDocumentStartDocumentTextDetection / StartDocumentAnalysis + Get…
    Input limitsSingle-page only: JPEG, PNG, or a 1-page PDF/TIFF; ≤ 10 MBMulti-page PDF/TIFF up to 3,000 pages, ≤ 500 MB, must be in S3
    LatencySeconds, inline responseSeconds to minutes; you're notified
    CompletionReturns blocks directlySNS notification, or poll GetDocument…
    We use it forThumbnails / single-image quick pathEverything real

    Because a multi-page PDF is the normal case for a document platform, async is mandatory. That's what forces the SNS callback architecture — a good thing, because it's the same pattern as any long-running job.

    §1bFundamentals: what OCR does, and what confidence means

    Optical character recognition takes an image of text and returns characters. Modern OCR runs several stages, and knowing them explains most of the failures you will see.

    1. Rasterisation. A PDF may already contain a text layer, or it may be a scanned image. Textract handles both, but a scanned page is an image and everything downstream is inference rather than lookup.
    2. Preprocessing. Deskewing a page photographed at an angle, correcting rotation, normalising contrast. This is why a phone photo of a receipt on a dark desk performs worse than a flatbed scan.
    3. Detection. Finding regions that contain text and drawing bounding boxes around words and lines.
    4. Recognition. Converting each region to characters, with a confidence score.
    5. Layout analysis. Deciding that this word is a form label and that word is its value, or that these boxes form a table with rows and columns. This is the hard part, and it is what the more expensive Textract features are actually paying for.

    Geometry is normalised, which is more useful than it sounds

    Every block comes with a bounding box whose coordinates run from 0 to 1 as fractions of the page, not pixels. So a box at Left: 0.1, Top: 0.05 is a tenth of the way across and a twentieth down, whatever the page resolution. That means you can render highlight overlays on a page image of any size without rescaling, and you can reason about position (is this in the top-right, where invoice numbers usually live?) without knowing the DPI.

    Confidence is a per-block probability, and the average lies

    Each word carries a confidence from 0 to 100, which is the model's own estimate that it read the characters correctly. Two things to understand about using it:

    • It is per-block, so it composes badly. A form field is a key block and a value block. Taking the mean of the two overstates reliability; taking the minimum is honest, because a perfectly-read label attached to a misread value is a wrong field.
    • The mean over a page hides the distribution. A page where 70% of words score 98 and 30% score 40 averages around 80, which looks acceptable. It is not acceptable; nearly a third of the page is noise. That is why this project gates on the proportion of words below 80 rather than on the mean. A distribution measure catches the failure that a central-tendency measure smooths away.

    What causes low confidence in practice: handwriting, stamps and signatures overlapping printed text, low-resolution faxes, skew, photocopies of photocopies, unusual fonts and text over background images. Most of these are properties of how the customer scans documents, which is why a sudden drop in confidence for one tenant is a conversation rather than an incident.

    §2The callback architecture

    ingest LambdaStartDocumentAnalysis
    →
    Textractasync job
    →
    SNS topicjob complete
    →
    SQS extract+ DLQ
    →
    extract LambdaGetDocumentAnalysis
    →
    RDSextractions
    SNS → SQS rather than SNS → Lambda directly: the queue gives buffering, retries, a DLQ, and replay. SNS → Lambda has none of those.
    Why not poll?

    You can poll GetDocumentAnalysis until JobStatus is SUCCEEDED. It's simpler, and it's wrong at scale: you either burn Lambda duration sleeping (billed) or need Step Functions to orchestrate the wait. The SNS callback costs nothing while waiting. Mention polling as the naive alternative and explain why you rejected it — that contrast is the answer, not the choice itself.

    HCLinfra/modules/textract-callback/main.tf
    resource "aws_sns_topic" "textract_complete" {
  name              = "${local.name}-textract-complete"
  kms_master_key_id = var.kms_key_id
}

# Textract needs a role it can assume in order to publish to our topic.
# This is a service-to-service pattern you will meet repeatedly.
resource "aws_iam_role" "textract_publish" {
  name = "${local.name}-textract-publish"

  assume_role_policy = jsonencode({
    Version = "2012-10-17"
    Statement = [{
      Effect    = "Allow"
      Action    = "sts:AssumeRole"
      Principal = { Service = "textract.amazonaws.com" }
      Condition = {
        StringEquals = { "aws:SourceAccount" = data.aws_caller_identity.current.account_id }
      }
    }]
  })
}

resource "aws_iam_role_policy" "textract_publish" {
  role = aws_iam_role.textract_publish.id
  policy = jsonencode({
    Version = "2012-10-17"
    Statement = [
      {
        Effect   = "Allow"
        Action   = "sns:Publish"
        Resource = aws_sns_topic.textract_complete.arn
      },
      {
        Effect   = "Allow"
        Action   = ["kms:GenerateDataKey", "kms:Decrypt"]
        Resource = var.kms_key_arn
      },
    ]
  })
}

resource "aws_sns_topic_subscription" "to_queue" {
  topic_arn = aws_sns_topic.textract_complete.arn
  protocol  = "sqs"
  endpoint  = aws_sqs_queue.extract.arn

  # Deliver the SNS message body directly rather than wrapped in the SNS
  # envelope. One less layer to unwrap in the handler.
  raw_message_delivery = true
}

data "aws_iam_policy_document" "extract_queue" {
  statement {
    effect  = "Allow"
    actions = ["sqs:SendMessage"]
    resources = [aws_sqs_queue.extract.arn]

    principals {
      type        = "Service"
      identifiers = ["sns.amazonaws.com"]
    }
    condition {
      test     = "ArnEquals"
      variable = "aws:SourceArn"
      values   = [aws_sns_topic.textract_complete.arn]
    }
  }
}

# The worker's own permissions. Note Textract needs no resource ARN — its
# actions are account-scoped, which is a real limitation worth knowing.
data "aws_iam_policy_document" "extract_worker" {
  statement {
    effect = "Allow"
    actions = [
      "textract:GetDocumentAnalysis",
      "textract:GetDocumentTextDetection",
      "textract:StartDocumentAnalysis",
      "textract:StartDocumentTextDetection",
      "textract:AnalyzeExpense",
    ]
    resources = ["*"]
  }

  statement {
    effect    = "Allow"
    actions   = ["iam:PassRole"]
    resources = [aws_iam_role.textract_publish.arn]
    # Only lets the worker pass this role TO Textract, not anywhere else.
    condition {
      test     = "StringEquals"
      variable = "iam:PassedToService"
      values   = ["textract.amazonaws.com"]
    }
  }
}
    Non-obvious IAM detail

    iam:PassRole is required whenever your code hands a role to an AWS service — here, giving Textract the publish role. Forgetting it produces a confusing AccessDeniedException that names your own role, not the pass. Always scope it with the iam:PassedToService condition; without that, the permission lets the holder pass that role to any service, which is a privilege-escalation path.

    §3Understanding Textract's block model

    Textract does not return a document tree. It returns a flat list of blocks with typed relationships — a graph you have to walk. This trips up everyone the first time, so here is the mental model.

    BlockTypeWhat it isRelationships
    PAGEOne pageCHILD → LINE, TABLE, KEY_VALUE_SET blocks
    LINEA line of textCHILD → WORD blocks
    WORDA single word + confidence + geometrynone
    KEY_VALUE_SETHalf of a form field. EntityTypes tells you which half: KEY or VALUEKEY has CHILD → WORDs and VALUE → the VALUE block
    TABLEA tableCHILD → CELL blocks
    CELLOne cell, with RowIndex/ColumnIndexCHILD → WORDs / SELECTION_ELEMENTs
    SELECTION_ELEMENTA checkboxhas SelectionStatus: SELECTED / NOT_SELECTED

    So extracting "Invoice Number: INV-4417" means: find the KEY_VALUE_SET whose EntityTypes includes KEY, gather its CHILD words to get the key text, follow its VALUE relationship to the VALUE block, gather that block's CHILD words. Two graph hops per field.

    TypeScriptservices/extract-worker/src/parse-blocks.ts
    import type { Block } from '@aws-sdk/client-textract';

export interface ParsedPage {
  page: number;
  text: string;
  confidence: number;
}
export interface ParsedKeyValue {
  key: string;
  value: string;
  confidence: number;   // the lower of the key and value confidences
  page: number;
}
export interface ParsedTable {
  page: number;
  rows: string[][];
}
export interface ParsedDocument {
  fullText: string;
  pages: ParsedPage[];
  keyValues: ParsedKeyValue[];
  tables: ParsedTable[];
  avgConfidence: number;
  lowConfidenceWordRatio: number;
}

/**
 * Walks Textract's flat block list into something we can store and query.
 * Written to be pure and synchronous so it is trivially unit-testable against
 * recorded fixtures — worth doing, because this is the fiddliest code here.
 */
export function parseBlocks(blocks: Block[]): ParsedDocument {
  const byId = new Map<string, Block>();
  for (const b of blocks) {
    if (b.Id) byId.set(b.Id, b);
  }

  const children = (b: Block | undefined, type: 'CHILD' | 'VALUE' = 'CHILD'): Block[] =>
    (b?.Relationships ?? [])
      .filter((r) => r.Type === type)
      .flatMap((r) => r.Ids ?? [])
      .map((id) => byId.get(id))
      .filter((x): x is Block => Boolean(x));

  /** Concatenate the WORD (and checkbox) text under a block. */
  const textOf = (b: Block | undefined): string =>
    children(b)
      .map((c) => {
        if (c.BlockType === 'WORD') return c.Text ?? '';
        if (c.BlockType === 'SELECTION_ELEMENT') {
          return c.SelectionStatus === 'SELECTED' ? '[x]' : '[ ]';
        }
        return '';
      })
      .filter(Boolean)
      .join(' ')
      .trim();

  // ── pages and full text ────────────────────────────────────────────
  const pages: ParsedPage[] = [];
  const pageBlocks = blocks.filter((b) => b.BlockType === 'PAGE');

  for (const pb of pageBlocks) {
    const lines = children(pb).filter((c) => c.BlockType === 'LINE');
    const confidences = lines.map((l) => l.Confidence ?? 0);
    pages.push({
      page: pb.Page ?? pages.length + 1,
      text: lines.map((l) => l.Text ?? '').join('\n'),
      confidence: confidences.length
        ? confidences.reduce((a, b) => a + b, 0) / confidences.length
        : 0,
    });
  }

  // ── form fields: KEY -> VALUE, two hops ────────────────────────────
  const keyValues: ParsedKeyValue[] = [];
  for (const b of blocks) {
    if (b.BlockType !== 'KEY_VALUE_SET') continue;
    if (!b.EntityTypes?.includes('KEY')) continue;      // skip the VALUE halves

    const valueBlock = children(b, 'VALUE')[0];
    const key = textOf(b);
    if (!key) continue;

    keyValues.push({
      key,
      value: textOf(valueBlock),
      // A field is only as trustworthy as its weakest half.
      confidence: Math.min(b.Confidence ?? 0, valueBlock?.Confidence ?? 0),
      page: b.Page ?? 1,
    });
  }

  // ── tables: sparse cells -> dense 2D array ─────────────────────────
  const tables: ParsedTable[] = [];
  for (const t of blocks.filter((b) => b.BlockType === 'TABLE')) {
    const cells = children(t).filter((c) => c.BlockType === 'CELL');
    // Textract omits empty cells entirely, so size from the max index rather
    // than assuming a rectangular list.
    const maxRow = Math.max(0, ...cells.map((c) => c.RowIndex ?? 0));
    const maxCol = Math.max(0, ...cells.map((c) => c.ColumnIndex ?? 0));
    const grid: string[][] = Array.from({ length: maxRow }, () => Array(maxCol).fill(''));

    for (const c of cells) {
      const r = (c.RowIndex ?? 1) - 1;
      const col = (c.ColumnIndex ?? 1) - 1;
      if (grid[r] && col < maxCol) grid[r][col] = textOf(c);
    }
    tables.push({ page: t.Page ?? 1, rows: grid });
  }

  // ── confidence signals we act on ───────────────────────────────────
  const words = blocks.filter((b) => b.BlockType === 'WORD');
  const avgConfidence = words.length
    ? words.reduce((a, w) => a + (w.Confidence ?? 0), 0) / words.length
    : 0;
  // A better signal than the mean: what fraction of words is Textract unsure
  // about? A crumpled fax has a decent average and a terrible tail.
  const lowConfidenceWordRatio = words.length
    ? words.filter((w) => (w.Confidence ?? 0) < 80).length / words.length
    : 1;

  return {
    fullText: pages.map((p) => p.text).join('\n\n'),
    pages,
    keyValues,
    tables,
    avgConfidence,
    lowConfidenceWordRatio,
  };
}

    §4The extract worker

    Two things to get right: pagination (Textract returns at most ~1,000 blocks per call) and treating PARTIAL_SUCCESS as a real, distinct outcome.

    TypeScriptservices/extract-worker/src/handler.ts
    import type { SQSEvent, SQSBatchResponse, SQSBatchItemFailure } from 'aws-lambda';
import {
  TextractClient, GetDocumentAnalysisCommand, type Block,
} from '@aws-sdk/client-textract';
import { SendMessageCommand, SQSClient } from '@aws-sdk/client-sqs';
import { and, eq } from 'drizzle-orm';
import { documents, extractions } from '@idp/db/schema';
import { canTransition } from '@idp/shared/document-status';
import { parseBlocks } from './parse-blocks.js';
import { getDb } from './db.js';
import { logger } from './logger.js';

const textract = new TextractClient({
  maxAttempts: 5,                     // SDK adaptive retry with jitter
  retryMode: 'adaptive',
});
const sqs = new SQSClient({});

/** The SNS message Textract publishes on completion. */
