  # CANNOT BE CHANGED LATER. Get this right now.
  storage_encrypted = true
  kms_key_id        = var.kms_key_arn

  db_name  = "idp"
  username = "idp_admin"
  password = random_password.master.result

  db_subnet_group_name   = aws_db_subnet_group.main.name
  vpc_security_group_ids = [var.db_security_group_id]
  parameter_group_name   = aws_db_parameter_group.main.name

  # Synchronous replication to a standby in another AZ, with automatic
  # failover in roughly 60-120 seconds. Doubles the instance cost.
  multi_az = var.environment == "prod"

  backup_retention_period = var.environment == "prod" ? 14 : 1
  backup_window           = "02:00-03:00"
  maintenance_window       = "sun:03:30-sun:04:30"
  copy_tags_to_snapshot    = true

  # Guardrails against accidental deletion.
  deletion_protection       = var.environment == "prod"
  skip_final_snapshot       = var.environment != "prod"
  final_snapshot_identifier = var.environment == "prod" ? "${local.name}-final" : null

  performance_insights_enabled          = true
  performance_insights_retention_period  = 7        # 7 days is free
  enabled_cloudwatch_logs_exports        = ["postgresql", "upgrade"]
  monitoring_interval                    = 60
  monitoring_role_arn                    = aws_iam_role.rds_monitoring.arn

  auto_minor_version_upgrade = true
  apply_immediately          = var.environment != "prod"
}

# Automatic rotation. The Lambda creates a new password, updates the secret,
# and RDS accepts it. Applications re-read the secret on their next reconnect.
resource "aws_secretsmanager_secret_rotation" "db" {
  count = var.environment == "prod" ? 1 : 0

  secret_id           = aws_secretsmanager_secret.db.id
  rotation_lambda_arn = var.rotation_lambda_arn

  rotation_rules {
    automatically_after_days = 30
  }
}
    Rotation has an application requirement

    Automatic rotation only works if your application can pick up a new password. A pool created once at boot with a password read once at boot will keep using the old credential and start failing when the old one is retired. Two fixes: read the secret on pool creation and add a reconnect handler that re-reads it, or use the Secrets Manager alternating users strategy where two users exist and rotation swaps between them, giving every client a grace period. Mention the requirement whenever you say the word rotation.

    The Dockerfile

    Dockerfileapps/api/Dockerfile
    # Multi-stage build. The final image contains no compiler, no dev dependencies,
# and no source maps of things we do not ship.
FROM node:22-alpine AS deps
WORKDIR /app
COPY package*.json ./
COPY apps/api/package*.json apps/api/
COPY packages/db/package*.json packages/db/
COPY packages/shared/package*.json packages/shared/
RUN npm ci --workspaces --include-workspace-root

FROM node:22-alpine AS build
WORKDIR /app
COPY --from=deps /app/node_modules ./node_modules
COPY . .
RUN npm run -w apps/api build

FROM node:22-alpine AS runtime
# dumb-init gives us correct signal forwarding so SIGTERM reaches Node and
# our graceful shutdown handler actually runs.
RUN apk add --no-cache dumb-init
ENV NODE_ENV=production

WORKDIR /app
# Run as a non-root user. The node image ships one already.
COPY --from=build --chown=node:node /app/apps/api/dist ./dist
COPY --from=build --chown=node:node /app/node_modules ./node_modules
COPY --from=build --chown=node:node /app/package.json ./

USER node
EXPOSE 8080
ENTRYPOINT ["dumb-init", "--"]
CMD ["node", "dist/server.js"]

    Graceful shutdown is what turns a rolling deploy into a rolling deploy with no dropped requests:

    TypeScriptapps/api/src/server.ts
    import { buildApp } from './app.js';
import { config } from './config.js';

const app = await buildApp();
await app.listen({ port: config.PORT, host: '0.0.0.0' });   // 0.0.0.0, not localhost

let shuttingDown = false;

/**
 * ECS sends SIGTERM, waits (default 30s), then sends SIGKILL.
 *
 * The ordering below matters. The load balancer needs time to notice we are
 * going away and stop sending new requests. If we close the server instantly,
 * requests already in flight toward us get connection resets.
 */
async function shutdown(signal: string) {
  if (shuttingDown) return;
  shuttingDown = true;
  app.log.info({ signal }, 'shutdown started');

  // 1. Fail readiness so the ALB deregisters us, while still serving traffic.
  app.get('/readyz', async (_r, reply) => reply.code(503).send({ ready: false }));

  // 2. Wait out the ALB's deregistration delay plus a margin.
  await new Promise((r) => setTimeout(r, 6000));

  // 3. Stop accepting new connections, finish in-flight requests, close the pool.
  try {
    await app.close();
    app.log.info('shutdown complete');
    process.exit(0);
  } catch (err) {
    app.log.error({ err }, 'shutdown failed');
    process.exit(1);
  }
}

process.on('SIGTERM', () => void shutdown('SIGTERM'));
process.on('SIGINT', () => void shutdown('SIGINT'));

// An unhandled rejection means unknown state. Crash and let ECS replace us.
process.on('unhandledRejection', (reason) => {
  app.log.fatal({ reason }, 'unhandled rejection');
  process.exit(1);
});

    §6Deployment and verification

    Shellfirst deploy, by hand
    ACCOUNT=$(aws sts get-caller-identity --query Account --output text)
REGION=eu-west-1
REPO="$ACCOUNT.dkr.ecr.$REGION.amazonaws.com/idp-dev-api"
TAG="sha-$(git rev-parse --short HEAD)"

aws ecr get-login-password --region $REGION \
  | docker login --username AWS --password-stdin "$ACCOUNT.dkr.ecr.$REGION.amazonaws.com"

# --provenance=false avoids a multi-arch manifest that older ECS agents dislike
docker buildx build --platform linux/arm64 --provenance=false \
  -f apps/api/Dockerfile -t "$REPO:$TAG" --push .

# Pin by digest, not tag. A digest is content-addressed, so the running task
# is provably the bytes you tested.
DIGEST=$(aws ecr describe-images --repository-name idp-dev-api \
  --image-ids imageTag="$TAG" --query 'imageDetails[0].imageDigest' --output text)

cd infra/envs/dev
terraform apply -var="api_image_uri=$REPO@$DIGEST" -var="git_sha=$TAG"

# Watch it roll out
aws ecs wait services-stable --cluster idp-dev --services idp-dev-api
aws ecs describe-services --cluster idp-dev --services idp-dev-api \
  --query 'services[0].{running:runningCount,desired:desiredCount,deployments:length(deployments)}'
    Shellwhen a task will not start: the debugging order
    # 1. Why did the task stop? This field usually contains the whole answer.
TASK=$(aws ecs list-tasks --cluster idp-dev --desired-status STOPPED \
  --query 'taskArns[0]' --output text)
aws ecs describe-tasks --cluster idp-dev --tasks "$TASK" \
  --query 'tasks[0].{stopped:stoppedReason,containers:containers[].{name:name,reason:reason,exit:exitCode}}'

# Common stoppedReason values and what they mean:
#   "CannotPullContainerError"        -> execution role, or no ECR endpoint/NAT
#   "ResourceInitializationError ... secretsmanager" -> execution role lacks
#                                       secretsmanager:GetSecretValue or kms:Decrypt
#   "Essential container in task exited" -> your app crashed. Read the logs.
#   "Task failed ELB health checks"   -> app is up but /healthz is not answering
#                                       on the port the target group expects

# 2. Application logs
aws logs tail /ecs/idp-dev-api --since 15m --follow

# 3. Target health, with the reason string
aws elbv2 describe-target-health --target-group-arn "$TG_ARN" \
  --query 'TargetHealthDescriptions[].{ip:Target.Id,state:TargetHealth.State,reason:TargetHealth.Reason,desc:TargetHealth.Description}'

# 4. Get a shell inside the running task. Needs ECS Exec enabled on the
# service and ssmmessages permissions on the TASK role.
aws ecs execute-command --cluster idp-dev --task "$TASK" \
  --container api --interactive --command "/bin/sh"

# 5. Is it a network problem? Check from inside.
#    (in the exec shell)
#    nc -zv  5432
#    node -e "fetch('https://s3.eu-west-1.amazonaws.com').then(r=>console.log(r.status))"

    §7Trade-offs

    DecisionChosenAlternativeReasoning
    API computeECS FargateLambda behind API Gateway; EC2; EKSFargate keeps warm database pools and has no cold starts, with no servers to patch. Lambda would suit a very spiky read API and cost less at low traffic, but connection management gets awkward and 15 minutes is a hard ceiling. EKS brings a Kubernetes control plane and a large operational surface for one service. EC2 means owning AMIs and patching.
    CPU architectureARM64 / Gravitonx86_64Around 20% cheaper for the same work, and Node has good ARM support. The cost is that your build must target ARM, which matters if a dependency ships x86-only binaries.
    Image referenceDigest, with immutable tags:latestA digest is content-addressed. The task provably runs the bytes you tested, and rollback means pointing at a previous digest.
    Autoscaling signalALB request count per targetCPU utilisationThe API waits on the database and on S3, so it is IO-bound. CPU stays flat while latency climbs, which means CPU-based scaling reacts late or not at all.
    DatabaseRDS Postgres, Multi-AZ in prodAurora Serverless v2; single-AZRDS is predictable and cheap at this size. Aurora Serverless v2 is the right answer for spiky load, with faster failover and storage-level replication, at a higher floor cost. Single-AZ halves the bill and gives up automatic failover, which is the correct trade in dev and the wrong one in prod.
    Secret deliveryECS secrets block, injected by the agentSDK call at startup; env var in the task definitionThe agent fetches it with the execution role, so the value never appears in the task definition, in Terraform state, or in the console. An env var in the task definition is visible to anyone with read access to ECS.

    §8Interview questions on this material

    Design the network for this application. Why is each piece where it is?

    A /16 VPC spanning two Availability Zones, carved into three tiers of subnets. Public subnets hold only the load balancer and the NAT gateways. Private app subnets hold the Fargate tasks and the Lambda workers. Private data subnets hold RDS, and their route table has no default route, so the database has no path to the internet in either direction.

    Traffic flows one way through the tiers. The ALB security group allows 443 from anywhere. The API security group allows 8080 from the ALB's security group, referenced by ID rather than by CIDR, so the rule keeps working when addresses change. The database security group allows 5432 from the API and worker security groups. Nothing else in the account can open a connection to the database.

    Two AZs because an AZ is AWS's unit of correlated failure, and losing one should halve capacity rather than cause an outage. For egress, I put a free S3 gateway endpoint on the private route tables, and interface endpoints for ECR, Secrets Manager, and CloudWatch Logs. That keeps a lot of traffic off the NAT gateway, which charges per gigabyte processed. In dev I run one NAT to save about $32 a month and accept that an AZ failure affects outbound traffic.

    For observability I enable VPC Flow Logs on rejected traffic only. Logging accepts in a busy VPC is expensive and mostly confirms that things worked; rejects answer the question you actually have when a connection fails.

    What's the difference between the ECS task role and the execution role?

    The execution role belongs to the ECS agent and is used before your code runs. It pulls the image from ECR, fetches any secrets listed in the task definition, and creates the CloudWatch log stream. The task role belongs to your application and is used at runtime for whatever the app calls: S3, SQS, Bedrock.

    The diagnostic value is that the symptoms differ. If the task never starts and the stopped reason mentions pulling the image or a resource initialisation error, that is the execution role or a missing network path to ECR. If the task starts and then throws AccessDenied on its first AWS call, that is the task role.

    The separation is also a real security boundary. My execution role can read the database secret; my application's task role cannot. So a remote code execution bug in the API cannot enumerate secrets, because the credentials available to the process do not have that permission. The password arrives as an environment variable that the agent injected, which is a different thing from the app being able to fetch arbitrary secrets.

    Why Fargate rather than Lambda for the API, when you used Lambda for the workers?

    Different shapes of work, so different compute.

    The API is long-lived and holds state that is expensive to establish: a warm Postgres connection pool, cached JWKS keys, an in-process user cache. Fargate keeps those alive across requests. On Lambda, every cold start rebuilds them, and a hundred concurrent Lambdas each wanting a database connection will exhaust a small instance's max_connections, which is why RDS Proxy exists. Fargate also has no cold starts to explain to a user waiting on a page load.

    The workers are the opposite shape. Ingest is bursty and idle most of the time, so scale-to-zero is worth real money. Each invocation is independent and short. Retries, dead-lettering, and concurrency limits come for free from the SQS event source mapping instead of being code I write.

    Where I would change my mind: if the API traffic were extremely spiky with long idle periods, Lambda plus RDS Proxy would be cheaper. If a worker needed more than fifteen minutes, it would move to Fargate or Step Functions. The general rule I apply is that Lambda suits event-driven bursts and containers suit persistent connection-heavy services, and I try to say which property of the workload drove the choice rather than asserting one is better.

    How do you deploy without downtime, and what happens if a deploy is bad?

    The deploy itself is a rolling update with minimum_healthy_percent at 100 and maximum_percent at 200. ECS starts new tasks alongside the old ones, waits for them to pass their target group health check, then drains and stops the old ones. Capacity never drops below the desired count.

    Two things make it actually seamless. The target group has a deregistration delay so in-flight requests to a draining task complete. And the container handles SIGTERM by first failing its readiness endpoint, waiting past that deregistration delay, and only then closing the server and the database pool. Without the wait, requests already on their way to the task get connection resets, and you see a small error spike on every deploy that nobody can explain.

    For bad deploys, the ECS deployment circuit breaker is enabled with rollback. If the new tasks cannot become healthy, ECS stops the deployment and returns to the last known-good task definition without anyone being paged. Beyond that, images are pinned by digest and task definitions are immutable revisions, so a manual rollback is registering the previous revision, which takes seconds. Migrations are backward compatible by rule, so rolling application code back never requires rolling the schema back.

    The thing I would add for a system with real traffic is progressive delivery: CodeDeploy blue/green with a canary listener rule, shifting 10% of traffic and watching error rate and p99 latency before shifting the rest. My current setup catches "the new version is broken" but not "the new version is 30% slower".

    Your database is in a private subnet with no internet route. How do you run migrations?

    As a one-off ECS task inside the same VPC, using the same image as the API but with a different command. The pipeline runs aws ecs run-task with an override, waits for it to stop, and checks the exit code before deploying the application. If the migration fails, the deploy does not happen.

    The migration task uses a different IAM role and a different database user from the API. That user has DDL rights; the API's user does not. So a compromised API cannot alter the schema, and row-level security still applies to the API's role because it is not the table owner.

    For interactive access when something has gone wrong, I use Session Manager port forwarding through a bastion, or ECS Exec into a running task and connect from there. No public database endpoint, no SSH keys, no bastion host with an open port 22. Every session is logged in CloudTrail, which is a better audit story than a shared jump box.

    §9Definition of done

    Sprint 08 · Production

    Observability, alarms and being on call for yourself

    You now have six services that can fail independently. This sprint makes them explicable. The test of success is narrow and practical: when a user says "my document is stuck", can you find out why in under two minutes using only what you built?

    Time
    2 days
    You'll produce
    Structured logs, custom metrics, tracing, dashboards, alarms, runbooks
    Running cost
    $5–20/mo mostly log ingestion
    Key idea
    Alarm on symptoms users feel, not on causes you happen to have a metric for

    §1Fundamentals: the three signals, and what each is for

    People say "logs, metrics, traces" as if it were one thing. They answer different questions, cost wildly different amounts, and confusing them leads to expensive mistakes.

    SignalAnswersCardinalityCost shapeRetention
    LogsWhat exactly happened to this one request?Unlimited. Put the document id, the tenant id, anything.Per GB ingested, then per GB stored. Volume grows with traffic.Days to weeks
    MetricsHow is the system behaving in aggregate, right now and over time?Low. Every dimension combination is a separate billed metric.Per metric per month, plus per API call to publish.15 months, aggregated
    TracesWhere did the time go across service boundaries?Medium, usually sampledPer trace recorded and retrievedDays
    The mistake that generates a shocking bill

    Putting a high-cardinality value in a metric dimension. If you add documentId as a dimension, every document creates its own custom metric, and CloudWatch charges per metric per month. Ten thousand documents becomes ten thousand metrics. Tenant id is usually acceptable if you have tens of tenants; document id, user id, and request id never are. High cardinality belongs in logs, where you can query it, or in trace attributes.

    §2Structured logging, and why the shape matters

    A log line like Processing document abc-123 for tenant xyz is readable by a human and useless to a machine. You cannot filter it, count it, or graph it without writing a regex, and the regex breaks the first time someone edits the message.

    Emit JSON instead. One object per line, with consistent field names. CloudWatch Logs Insights parses JSON automatically, so every field becomes queryable with no configuration.

    TypeScriptpackages/shared/src/logger.ts
    import pino from 'pino';

/**
 * Field naming is a contract. Every service uses the same names for the same
 * concepts, so one Logs Insights query works across all of them. Agreeing
 * this list on day one is worth more than any clever tooling later.
 *
 *   tenantId, documentId, requestId, jobId  - correlation
 *   durationMs                              - timing
 *   err                                     - serialised error
 *   event                                   - a short machine-readable name
 */
export const logger = pino({
  level: process.env.LOG_LEVEL ?? 'info',

  // CloudWatch shows "level" as a string; a number is unreadable in the console.
  formatters: {
    level: (label) => ({ level: label }),
    bindings: () => ({
      service: process.env.SERVICE_NAME ?? 'unknown',
      version: process.env.GIT_SHA ?? 'dev',
      env: process.env.ENVIRONMENT ?? 'dev',
    }),
  },

  // CloudWatch wants epoch millis, not pino's default.
  timestamp: pino.stdTimeFunctions.isoTime,

  redact: {
    paths: [
      'req.headers.authorization',
      'req.headers.cookie',
      '*.password',
      '*.secret',
      '*.uploadUrl',        // presigned URLs contain a valid signature
      '*.accessToken',
      '*.embedding',        // 1024 floats per line is not a log entry
    ],
    censor: '[redacted]',
  },

  serializers: {
    err: pino.stdSerializers.err,
  },
});

/**
 * Emit a CloudWatch metric by writing a log line. This is the Embedded Metric
 * Format. No PutMetricData call, no extra latency, no API cost, and the metric
 * arrives with the log entry that produced it so you can pivot between them.
 */
export function metric(opts: {
  namespace: string;
  metrics: { name: string; value: number; unit: string }[];
  dimensions?: Record<string, string>;
  context?: Record<string, unknown>;
}) {
  const dims = opts.dimensions ?? {};
  logger.info({
    _aws: {
      Timestamp: Date.now(),
      CloudWatchMetrics: [{
        Namespace: opts.namespace,
        // Keep this list short. Every combination of dimension values is a
        // separate billed metric.
        Dimensions: [Object.keys(dims)],
        Metrics: opts.metrics.map((m) => ({ Name: m.name, Unit: m.unit })),
      }],
    },
    ...dims,
    ...Object.fromEntries(opts.metrics.map((m) => [m.name, m.value])),
    ...opts.context,
  });
}

    Queries to save before you need them

    Write these now, while you are calm. During an incident you will not want to be composing query syntax.

    SQL-ishCloudWatch Logs Insights
    -- 1. Everything that happened to one document, across every service.
--    This is the query you will run most. Select all log groups.
fields @timestamp, service, level, event, msg, err.message
| filter documentId = 'abc-123-def'
| sort @timestamp asc

-- 2. Error rate by service over the last hour, in 5-minute buckets.
fields @timestamp, service
| filter level in ['error', 'fatal']
| stats count() as errors by bin(5m), service
| sort @timestamp desc

-- 3. Slowest API routes, p50 to p99. Note that percentiles need enough
--    samples to mean anything; do not read p99 off twelve requests.
fields @timestamp, req.url, durationMs
| filter ispresent(durationMs) and service = 'api'
| stats count() as n,
        pct(durationMs, 50) as p50,
        pct(durationMs, 95) as p95,
        pct(durationMs, 99) as p99
  by req.url
| sort p99 desc
| limit 20

-- 4. Which tenant is generating the errors? Often the fastest way to
--    understand an incident is to notice it is one customer.
fields @timestamp, tenantId, err.message
| filter level = 'error'
| stats count() as errors by tenantId, err.message
| sort errors desc

-- 5. Bedrock token spend by model, for the cost conversation.
fields @timestamp, modelId, inputTokens, outputTokens
| filter ispresent(inputTokens)
| stats sum(inputTokens) as in_tok, sum(outputTokens) as out_tok, count() as calls
  by modelId, bin(1d)

-- 6. Find the requestId for a user complaint, then use query 1.
fields @timestamp, requestId, tenantId, req.url, res.statusCode
| filter tenantId = 'tenant-uuid' and res.statusCode >= 400
| sort @timestamp desc
| limit 50

    §3Metrics that describe the product, not the plumbing

    AWS gives you infrastructure metrics for free: Lambda duration, ECS CPU, SQS depth, RDS connections. Those tell you how components behave. They do not tell you whether the product works.

    The metrics that matter here are the ones a user would recognise:

    MetricWhy it existsAlarm on it?
    TimeToReadySecondsUpload to searchable. The number a customer would quote back at you.Yes, on p95
    DocumentsStuckCount in a non-terminal state for over 15 minutes. Catches silent stalls where nothing errored.Yes
    OcrConfidenceData quality. A drop usually means a customer changed their scanner.Warn only
    ExtractionSchemaFailuresThe model returned something unusable. Prompt or model regression.Yes, on rate
    BedrockTokensCost. Anomaly detection catches retry loops.Anomaly band
    SearchLatencyMsThe other user-facing path. Vector search degrades as the index grows.Yes, on p95
    TypeScriptservices/monitor/src/handler.ts
    /**
 * Runs every 5 minutes on an EventBridge schedule. Publishes the metrics that
 * no AWS service can know about, because they are properties of our data
 * rather than of our infrastructure.
 *
 * A scheduled query like this is how you detect the failure mode where
 * nothing threw an exception and nothing is in a dead-letter queue, but work
 * silently stopped progressing.
 */
export async function handler() {
  const db = await getDb();

  const [stuck] = await db.execute(sql`
    SELECT
      count(*) FILTER (WHERE status = 'pending_upload'
                       AND created_at < now() - interval '1 hour')  AS abandoned_uploads,
      count(*) FILTER (WHERE status IN ('uploaded','extracting','extracted','enriching')
                       AND updated_at < now() - interval '15 minutes') AS stuck,
      count(*) FILTER (WHERE status = 'failed'
                       AND updated_at > now() - interval '15 minutes') AS recent_failures
    FROM documents
    WHERE deleted_at IS NULL
  `);

  // Pipeline latency, measured from the data rather than from a timer.
  const [latency] = await db.execute(sql`
    SELECT
      percentile_cont(0.50) WITHIN GROUP (
        ORDER BY EXTRACT(EPOCH FROM (updated_at - created_at))) AS p50,
      percentile_cont(0.95) WITHIN GROUP (
        ORDER BY EXTRACT(EPOCH FROM (updated_at - created_at))) AS p95,
      count(*) AS n
    FROM documents
    WHERE status = 'ready'
      AND updated_at > now() - interval '1 hour'
  `);

  metric({
    namespace: 'IDP/Pipeline',
    dimensions: { Environment: process.env.ENVIRONMENT! },
    metrics: [
      { name: 'DocumentsStuck',        value: Number(stuck.stuck),             unit: 'Count' },
      { name: 'AbandonedUploads',      value: Number(stuck.abandoned_uploads), unit: 'Count' },
      { name: 'RecentFailures',        value: Number(stuck.recent_failures),   unit: 'Count' },
      { name: 'TimeToReadyP50Seconds', value: Number(latency.p50 ?? 0),        unit: 'Seconds' },
      { name: 'TimeToReadyP95Seconds', value: Number(latency.p95 ?? 0),        unit: 'Seconds' },
    ],
    context: { sampleSize: Number(latency.n) },
  });

  // Per-tenant, but only for tenants above a volume threshold, so the metric
  // count stays bounded. Cardinality discipline is a cost decision.
  const busy = await db.execute(sql`
    SELECT tenant_id, count(*) AS n
    FROM documents
    WHERE created_at > now() - interval '1 hour' AND deleted_at IS NULL
    GROUP BY tenant_id
    HAVING count(*) > 10
    ORDER BY n DESC
    LIMIT 20
  `);

  for (const row of busy.rows) {
    metric({
      namespace: 'IDP/Tenant',
      dimensions: { Environment: process.env.ENVIRONMENT!, TenantId: String(row.tenant_id) },
      metrics: [{ name: 'DocumentsPerHour', value: Number(row.n), unit: 'Count' }],
    });
  }
}

    §4Alarms: what to wake up for

    An alarm that fires and gets ignored is worse than no alarm, because it teaches you to ignore alarms. So split them into two tiers with different destinations.

    TierMeaningGoes toExamples here
    PageUsers are affected right now and it will not fix itselfPhone, SMS, PagerDutyDLQ not empty; API 5xx over 1%; database unreachable; documents stuck
    TicketSomething needs attention this weekEmail, Slack channelOCR confidence trending down; storage at 80%; cost anomaly; certificate expiring

    The rule underneath is to alarm on symptoms, not causes. "CPU is at 80%" might be fine. "p95 latency is above two seconds" is never fine. Symptom alarms catch causes you did not predict, which is most of them.

    HCLinfra/modules/observability/alarms.tf
    resource "aws_sns_topic" "page" {
  name              = "${local.name}-page"
  kms_master_key_id = "alias/aws/sns"
}

resource "aws_sns_topic" "ticket" {
  name              = "${local.name}-ticket"
  kms_master_key_id = "alias/aws/sns"
}

# ── Symptom alarm 1: are we serving errors?
# Metric math on the ratio, because "50 errors" means nothing without
# knowing whether that was out of 100 requests or 100,000.
resource "aws_cloudwatch_metric_alarm" "api_error_rate" {
  alarm_name          = "${local.name}-api-5xx-rate"
  alarm_description   = "API 5xx rate above 1%. Runbook: docs/runbooks/api-errors.md"
  comparison_operator = "GreaterThanThreshold"
  threshold           = 1
  evaluation_periods  = 2
  datapoints_to_alarm = 2
  treat_missing_data  = "notBreaching"
  alarm_actions       = [aws_sns_topic.page.arn]
  ok_actions          = [aws_sns_topic.page.arn]

  metric_query {
    id          = "rate"
    expression  = "100 * errors / MAX([requests, 1])"
    label       = "5xx percentage"
    return_data = true
  }

  metric_query {
    id = "errors"
    metric {
      namespace   = "AWS/ApplicationELB"
      metric_name = "HTTPCode_Target_5XX_Count"
      dimensions  = { LoadBalancer = var.alb_suffix }
      period      = 300
      stat        = "Sum"
    }
  }

  metric_query {
    id = "requests"
    metric {
      namespace   = "AWS/ApplicationELB"
      metric_name = "RequestCount"
      dimensions  = { LoadBalancer = var.alb_suffix }
      period      = 300
      stat        = "Sum"
    }
  }
}

# ── Symptom alarm 2: is the product slow?
resource "aws_cloudwatch_metric_alarm" "api_latency" {
  alarm_name          = "${local.name}-api-p95-latency"
  alarm_description   = "API p95 above 2s for 10 minutes"
  namespace           = "AWS/ApplicationELB"
  metric_name         = "TargetResponseTime"
  dimensions          = { LoadBalancer = var.alb_suffix }
  # Percentile, not Average. An average hides the tail, and the tail is what
  # users complain about.
  extended_statistic  = "p95"
  period              = 300
  evaluation_periods  = 2
  threshold           = 2
  comparison_operator = "GreaterThanThreshold"
  treat_missing_data  = "notBreaching"
  alarm_actions       = [aws_sns_topic.page.arn]
}

# ── Symptom alarm 3: has the pipeline silently stalled?
# This is the alarm that catches what the others miss. Nothing errored,
# nothing is in a DLQ, and yet documents are not progressing.
resource "aws_cloudwatch_metric_alarm" "documents_stuck" {
  alarm_name          = "${local.name}-documents-stuck"
  alarm_description   = "Documents in a non-terminal state for over 15 minutes. Runbook: docs/runbooks/stuck-documents.md"
  namespace           = "IDP/Pipeline"
  metric_name         = "DocumentsStuck"
  dimensions          = { Environment = var.environment }
  statistic           = "Maximum"
  period              = 300
  evaluation_periods  = 2
  threshold           = 5
  comparison_operator = "GreaterThanThreshold"
  treat_missing_data  = "breaching"   # the monitor itself failing is a problem
  alarm_actions       = [aws_sns_topic.page.arn]
}

# ── Composite alarm: page once for a correlated failure, not five times.
# When the database goes down, error rate, latency, and stuck documents all
# fire together. Five pages for one incident is how alerting gets muted.
resource "aws_cloudwatch_composite_alarm" "api_unhealthy" {
  alarm_name        = "${local.name}-api-unhealthy"
  alarm_description = "One or more user-facing symptoms are present"

  alarm_rule = join(" OR ", [
    "ALARM(${aws_cloudwatch_metric_alarm.api_error_rate.alarm_name})",
    "ALARM(${aws_cloudwatch_metric_alarm.api_latency.alarm_name})",
    "ALARM(${aws_cloudwatch_metric_alarm.unhealthy_hosts.alarm_name})",
  ])

  alarm_actions = [aws_sns_topic.page.arn]
  ok_actions    = [aws_sns_topic.page.arn]
}

# ── Cost anomaly detection on LLM spend. A retry loop against Bedrock is an
# expensive bug, and a static threshold either alarms constantly or never.
resource "aws_cloudwatch_metric_alarm" "bedrock_token_anomaly" {
  alarm_name          = "${local.name}-bedrock-token-anomaly"
  alarm_description   = "Daily Bedrock token usage outside its normal band"
  comparison_operator = "GreaterThanUpperThreshold"
  evaluation_periods  = 1
  threshold_metric_id = "band"
  alarm_actions       = [aws_sns_topic.ticket.arn]

  metric_query {
    id          = "tokens"
    return_data = true
    metric {
      namespace   = "IDP/Enrich"
      metric_name = "BedrockInputTokens"
      dimensions  = { Environment = var.environment }
      period      = 86400
      stat        = "Sum"
    }
  }

  metric_query {
    id          = "band"
    expression  = "ANOMALY_DETECTION_BAND(tokens, 2)"
    label       = "expected range"
    return_data = true
  }
}
    treat_missing_data is not a detail

    Four options, and picking the wrong one breaks the alarm silently. notBreaching suits a metric that legitimately has no data when idle, like a DLQ with no messages. breaching suits a heartbeat where absence of data is itself the failure, like our stuck-document monitor: if the monitor Lambda dies, no data arrives, and you want that to alarm. missing keeps the previous state. ignore leaves the alarm where it is. The default is missing, which is why so many alarms sit in INSUFFICIENT_DATA forever and nobody notices.

    §5Tracing across the pipeline

    Logs tell you what happened in one service. A trace tells you where the time went across all of them. For a document that took four minutes to become ready, a trace answers whether that was Textract, the queue, Bedrock, or the database.

    Two things make tracing work in an asynchronous pipeline, and the second is the one people miss:

    1. Enable X-Ray on the Lambdas and the ECS tasks, and instrument the AWS SDK clients so calls appear as subsegments.
    2. Propagate the trace id through the queue. A trace normally follows an HTTP header. Your pipeline hops through S3, EventBridge, SQS, and SNS, and the default propagation does not survive all of that. So carry your own correlation id in the message body and log it everywhere. Even without a full X-Ray trace, one grep across all log groups then reconstructs the whole journey.
    TypeScriptcarrying correlation across an async boundary
    // Producer: put the correlation id in the message, not just in the log.
await sqs.send(new SendMessageCommand({
  QueueUrl: process.env.ENRICH_QUEUE_URL!,
  MessageBody: JSON.stringify({
    documentId,
    tenantId,
    // This is what lets one query span six services and four hops.
    correlationId: currentCorrelationId(),
    // X-Ray's own header, for the cases where propagation does work.
    traceHeader: process.env._X_AMZN_TRACE_ID,
  }),
}));

// Consumer: rebind the logger so every subsequent line carries it.
const { correlationId, documentId, tenantId } = JSON.parse(record.body);
const log = logger.child({ correlationId, documentId, tenantId });
log.info({ event: 'enrich.start' }, 'starting enrichment');

    §6Runbooks, and why they belong in the repo

    Every alarm description in this project ends with a path to a runbook. That is deliberate. At 3am you do not want to think; you want a checklist. Writing one also forces you to notice which failures you have not actually thought through.

    Markdowndocs/runbooks/stuck-documents.md
    # Runbook: documents stuck in processing

**Alarm:** `idp-prod-documents-stuck`
**Severity:** page
**Means:** more than 5 documents have sat in a non-terminal state for 15+ minutes.

## 1. Establish scope (2 min)

```sql
SELECT status, count(*), min(updated_at) AS oldest
FROM documents
WHERE deleted_at IS NULL
  AND status NOT IN ('ready','failed','quarantined')
GROUP BY status ORDER BY count(*) DESC;
```

The status they are stuck in tells you which stage broke.

| Stuck in | Stage | Go to |
|---|---|---|
| `pending_upload` | Client never completed the PUT | §2 |
| `uploaded` | Ingest worker not consuming | §3 |
| `extracting` | Textract, or the SNS callback | §4 |
| `extracted` | Enrichment worker or Bedrock | §5 |

## 2. pending_upload

Usually benign: users abandon uploads. Only escalate if the count is unusual
or growing fast, which suggests presigned URLs are being rejected.

- Check the API for a spike in 5xx on `POST /v1/uploads`.
- Confirm bucket CORS still lists the current web origin.
- Reproduce with the manual curl flow in `docs/testing/upload.md`.

## 3. uploaded (ingest not consuming)

```bash
aws sqs get-queue-attributes --queue-url "$INGEST_QUEUE" \
  --attribute-names ApproximateNumberOfMessages \
    ApproximateAgeOfOldestMessage ApproximateNumberOfMessagesNotVisible
```

- Messages present, age high, Lambda invocations flat: the event source
  mapping is disabled or the function is throttled. Check `Throttles`, and
  check whether the reserved concurrency was changed.
- No messages at all: the break is upstream. Check the EventBridge rule's
  `FailedInvocations` and `TriggeredRules` metrics, and the rule's own DLQ.
- Lambda erroring: `aws logs tail /aws/lambda/idp-prod-ingest --since 30m`.

## 4. extracting (Textract or callback)

```bash
# Is Textract itself finishing the jobs?
aws textract get-document-analysis --job-id "$JOB_ID" --query JobStatus
```

- `SUCCEEDED` but our status is still `extracting`: the callback path is
  broken. Check the SNS topic's `NumberOfNotificationsFailed` and the extract
  queue depth.
- `IN_PROGRESS` for a long time on a large document: usually normal. Check the
  Textract service health dashboard before assuming otherwise.
- `FAILED`: read `StatusMessage`. Password-protected PDFs are the usual cause.

## 5. extracted (enrichment)

- `ThrottlingException` from Bedrock in the logs: expected under burst. Confirm
  the queue is draining rather than growing. Consider requesting a quota
  increase, or fall back to the smaller model.
- Schema validation failures: a prompt or model regression. Roll back the
  prompt version and run the golden-set eval.

## 6. Recovery

Once the cause is fixed, requeue:

```sql
-- Documents that failed during the incident window
UPDATE documents SET status = 'uploaded', failure_reason = NULL
WHERE status = 'failed' AND updated_at BETWEEN $start AND $end;
```

```bash
# Redrive anything in the DLQ, rate-limited so we do not stampede Textract
aws sqs start-message-move-task \
  --source-arn "$DLQ_ARN" --destination-arn "$QUEUE_ARN" \
  --max-number-of-messages-per-second 5
```

Processing is idempotent, so replaying a document that actually succeeded is
harmless. That property is what makes this step safe to run under pressure.

## 7. Afterwards

Write the timeline into `docs/incidents/YYYY-MM-DD.md`: what broke, how long,
what the detection gap was, and one change that would have caught it sooner.

    §7Interview questions on this material

    How would you debug "my document has been processing for an hour"?

    I would start from the data rather than from the infrastructure, because the document's status tells me which of six stages to look at, and that turns a broad search into a narrow one.

    First query: group non-terminal documents by status. Stuck in uploaded means the ingest worker is not consuming. Stuck in extracting means Textract or the SNS callback. Stuck in extracted means enrichment. That is thirty seconds of work and it eliminates most of the system.

    Then one Logs Insights query filtered on that document id across every log group, sorted ascending. Because all six services log a consistent documentId field, that returns the document's whole journey in one result set, including the exact error and the service that raised it.

    From there it is stage-specific: queue age and Lambda throttles for a consumer problem, GetDocumentAnalysis job status for a Textract problem, throttling exceptions for a Bedrock problem. The runbook has the commands so I am not inventing them under pressure.

    The design decision that makes all of this work is agreeing field names on day one. documentId, tenantId, correlationId, spelled identically in every service. Without that, correlating across an asynchronous pipeline means writing regexes per service, and you do not want to be doing that while a customer waits.

    What do you alarm on, and how do you avoid alert fatigue?

    I alarm on symptoms a user would notice, not on causes I happen to have a metric for. High CPU might be perfectly fine; p95 latency above two seconds never is. Symptom alarms also catch causes I did not predict, which is most of them.

    My page-worthy set is small: 5xx rate above 1%, p95 latency above two seconds, anything in a dead-letter queue, documents stuck in a non-terminal state, and the database unreachable. Everything else is a ticket: OCR confidence trending down, storage at 80%, a cost anomaly, a certificate expiring.

    Three techniques keep the noise down. Ratios, not counts, using metric math, because fifty errors means nothing without the denominator. Multiple datapoints to alarm, so three of five periods must breach and a single spike does not page anyone. And composite alarms, so when the database goes down and error rate, latency, and stuck documents all fire together, I get one page about one incident rather than five.

    The setting people get wrong is treat_missing_data. For a DLQ it should be notBreaching, since an empty queue reports nothing. For my stuck-document monitor it should be breaching, because absence of data means the monitor itself died and that is a real failure. The default is missing, which is why so many alarms sit in INSUFFICIENT_DATA and nobody notices.

    Every alarm description ends with a runbook path. If I cannot write a runbook for an alarm, I do not understand the failure well enough to page someone about it.

    Logs, metrics, or traces: how do you decide where something belongs?

    Cardinality decides it, and cost follows cardinality.

    Anything with unbounded distinct values goes in logs: document ids, request ids, user ids, error messages, the tokens for one specific call. Logs are billed per gigabyte, so cardinality is free but volume is not, which is why I redact embeddings and never log a presigned URL.

    Metrics are for low-cardinality aggregates. CloudWatch bills per metric per month, and every unique combination of dimension values is a separate metric. Putting a document id in a dimension turns ten thousand documents into ten thousand metrics. Tenant id is borderline, so I only emit per-tenant metrics for tenants above a volume threshold, which bounds the count deliberately.

    Traces are for latency attribution across service boundaries. Sampled, because full tracing is expensive and rarely necessary.

    The technique I would highlight is the Embedded Metric Format. I write a specially shaped JSON log line and CloudWatch extracts a metric from it. No PutMetricData call, so no added latency in the request path and no API cost, and the metric arrives attached to the log entry that produced it, so I can pivot from a spike straight to the lines behind it.

    §8Definition of done

    Sprint 09 · Production

    CI/CD with GitHub Actions, and zero stored AWS keys

    Up to now you have deployed by hand. That works exactly until the day you forget a step. This sprint automates it, and does so without ever putting an AWS access key into GitHub. The mechanism is OIDC federation, and understanding it properly is one of the higher-value things in this manual.

    Time
    1–2 days
    You'll produce
    OIDC trust, test/build/deploy pipeline, plan-on-PR, drift detection
    Running cost
    $0 free minutes cover a portfolio
    Key idea
    CI proves the identity of the workflow, not the possession of a secret

    §1Fundamentals: how OIDC federation replaces access keys

    The old way: create an IAM user, generate an access key, paste it into GitHub secrets. That key is a permanent credential sitting in a third-party system. If it leaks, someone has your account until you notice and rotate. Nobody rotates it.

    The new way relies on a chain of trust that has no shared secret at all:

    1. GitHub runs an OIDC identity provider. For each workflow run it mints a short-lived, signed JWT describing that run: which repository, which branch or tag, which environment, which workflow file.
    2. You register GitHub's provider in your AWS account once, giving AWS the provider URL so it can fetch GitHub's public keys.
    3. You create a role whose trust policy says "GitHub's provider may assume me, but only when the token's sub claim matches this exact repository and branch".
    4. The workflow calls AssumeRoleWithWebIdentity, presenting its token. AWS verifies the signature against GitHub's published keys, checks the conditions, and returns temporary credentials valid for an hour.

    Nothing is stored. Nothing can leak, because there is nothing to leak. And the trust policy conditions mean a token from a different repository, or from a pull request on a fork, cannot assume the role even though it came from the same identity provider.

    The condition that makes or breaks this

    The sub claim looks like repo:you/idp-platform:ref:refs/heads/main. If you write the condition as repo:you/idp-platform:* using StringLike, then any branch in that repo can assume the role, including a branch someone opens via a pull request. Anyone who can open a PR can then run arbitrary code with your deployment permissions. Pin the exact ref with StringEquals, or scope to an environment: claim and use GitHub environment protection rules as the gate. This misconfiguration is common enough that it has its own class of security write-ups.

    HCLinfra/modules/cicd/oidc.tf
    # One provider per account. The thumbprint list is no longer meaningful for
# GitHub (AWS validates against the library of trusted CAs), but the argument
# is still required by the API.
resource "aws_iam_openid_connect_provider" "github" {
  url             = "https://token.actions.githubusercontent.com"
  client_id_list  = ["sts.amazonaws.com"]
  thumbprint_list = ["6938fd4d98bab03faadb97b34396831e3780aea1"]
}

# ── Role 1: plan only. Assumable from any branch, because a plan is read-only
# and we want plans on pull requests.
data "aws_iam_policy_document" "plan_trust" {
  statement {
    effect  = "Allow"
    actions = ["sts:AssumeRoleWithWebIdentity"]

    principals {
      type        = "Federated"
      identifiers = [aws_iam_openid_connect_provider.github.arn]
    }

    condition {
      test     = "StringEquals"
      variable = "token.actions.githubusercontent.com:aud"
      values   = ["sts.amazonaws.com"]
    }

    # Any ref in THIS repository only.
    condition {
      test     = "StringLike"
      variable = "token.actions.githubusercontent.com:sub"
      values   = ["repo:${var.github_org}/${var.github_repo}:*"]
    }
  }
}

resource "aws_iam_role" "terraform_plan" {
  name                 = "${local.name}-gha-plan"
  assume_role_policy   = data.aws_iam_policy_document.plan_trust.json
  max_session_duration = 3600
}

resource "aws_iam_role_policy_attachment" "plan_read" {
  role       = aws_iam_role.terraform_plan.name
  policy_arn = "arn:aws:iam::aws:policy/ReadOnlyAccess"
}

# Plan still needs to read and lock state, which ReadOnlyAccess does not cover.
resource "aws_iam_role_policy" "plan_state" {
  role = aws_iam_role.terraform_plan.id
  policy = jsonencode({
    Version = "2012-10-17"
    Statement = [{
      Effect   = "Allow"
      Action   = ["s3:GetObject", "s3:PutObject", "s3:DeleteObject", "s3:ListBucket"]
      Resource = [var.state_bucket_arn, "${var.state_bucket_arn}/*"]
    }]
  })
}

# ── Role 2: apply. Locked to one environment claim, so only a workflow run
# that has passed the GitHub environment's protection rules can use it.
data "aws_iam_policy_document" "apply_trust" {
  statement {
    effect  = "Allow"
    actions = ["sts:AssumeRoleWithWebIdentity"]

    principals {
      type        = "Federated"
      identifiers = [aws_iam_openid_connect_provider.github.arn]
    }

    condition {
      test     = "StringEquals"
      variable = "token.actions.githubusercontent.com:aud"
      values   = ["sts.amazonaws.com"]
    }

    # StringEquals, not StringLike. Exactly this environment, nothing else.
    condition {
      test     = "StringEquals"
      variable = "token.actions.githubusercontent.com:sub"
      values   = ["repo:${var.github_org}/${var.github_repo}:environment:${var.environment}"]
    }
  }
}

resource "aws_iam_role" "terraform_apply" {
  name                 = "${local.name}-gha-apply"
  assume_role_policy   = data.aws_iam_policy_document.apply_trust.json
  max_session_duration = 3600
}

# Broad in dev so you can iterate; in prod, narrow this to the services you
# actually manage and add a permissions boundary so the role cannot grant
# itself more than it has.
resource "aws_iam_role_policy_attachment" "apply" {
  role       = aws_iam_role.terraform_apply.name
  policy_arn = var.environment == "prod" ? aws_iam_policy.scoped_apply[0].arn : "arn:aws:iam::aws:policy/PowerUserAccess"
}

    §2Pipeline design

    Four workflows, each with one job. Splitting them means a documentation change does not run a Terraform plan, and a failed lint does not leave a half-built image in the registry.

    WorkflowTriggers onDoesNeeds AWS?
    ci.ymlEvery push and PRLint, typecheck, unit and integration tests against a Postgres service containerNo
    infra-plan.ymlPR touching infra/fmt, validate, tfsec, plan; posts the plan as a PR commentRead-only role
    deploy.ymlPush to mainBuild image, push to ECR, apply Terraform, run migrations, update service, smoke testApply role, gated by environment
    drift.ymlDaily schedulePlan against prod, open an issue if anything differsRead-only role
    YAML.github/workflows/ci.yml
    name: CI

on:
  push:
    branches: [main]
  pull_request:

# Cancel superseded runs on the same branch. Saves minutes and avoids
# confusing results from a stale commit.
concurrency:
  group: ci-${{ github.ref }}
  cancel-in-progress: true

permissions:
  contents: read

jobs:
  test:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    timeout-minutes: 15

    # A real Postgres with pgvector, so integration tests exercise actual SQL,
    # actual migrations, and actual row-level security rather than a mock.
    services:
      postgres:
        image: pgvector/pgvector:pg16
        env:
          POSTGRES_USER: idp
          POSTGRES_PASSWORD: test
          POSTGRES_DB: idp_test
        ports: ['5432:5432']
        options: >-
          --health-cmd "pg_isready -U idp"
          --health-interval 5s
          --health-timeout 5s
          --health-retries 10

    env:
      DATABASE_URL: postgres://idp:test@localhost:5432/idp_test

    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4

      - uses: actions/setup-node@v4
        with:
          node-version: 22
          cache: npm

      - run: npm ci

      - name: Lint and typecheck
        run: npm run lint

      - name: Migrations apply from empty
        run: npm run dev:migrate

      - name: Migrations roll back cleanly
        run: |
          node-pg-migrate down -m packages/db/migrations --count 999
          npm run dev:migrate

      - name: Unit tests
        run: npm run test -- --coverage

      - name: Integration tests
        run: npm run test:it

      - name: Golden-set eval for the AI prompts
        # Only on main: it calls Bedrock and costs money, and PR authors
        # should not need Bedrock credentials.
        if: github.ref == 'refs/heads/main'
        run: npm run eval:enrich

      - uses: actions/upload-artifact@v4
        if: always()
        with:
          name: coverage
          path: coverage/
    YAML.github/workflows/infra-plan.yml
    name: Terraform plan

on:
  pull_request:
    paths: ['infra/**']

permissions:
  contents: read
  id-token: write        # REQUIRED for OIDC. Without it, no token is minted.
  pull-requests: write   # to post the plan as a comment

jobs:
  plan:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    strategy:
      fail-fast: false
      matrix:
        env: [dev, prod]

    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4

      - uses: aws-actions/configure-aws-credentials@v4
        with:
          role-to-assume: arn:aws:iam::${{ secrets.AWS_ACCOUNT_ID }}:role/idp-${{ matrix.env }}-gha-plan
          aws-region: eu-west-1
          role-session-name: gha-plan-${{ github.run_id }}

      - uses: hashicorp/setup-terraform@v3
        with:
          terraform_version: 1.10.5
          terraform_wrapper: false

      - name: Format check
        run: terraform fmt -check -recursive

      - name: Security scan
        uses: aquasecurity/tfsec-action@v1
        with:
          soft_fail: false      # a HIGH finding fails the PR

      - name: Init and validate
        working-directory: infra/envs/${{ matrix.env }}
        run: |
          terraform init -input=false
          terraform validate

      - name: Plan
        id: plan
        working-directory: infra/envs/${{ matrix.env }}
        run: |
          set +e
          terraform plan -input=false -no-color -out=tf.plan -detailed-exitcode
          code=$?
          echo "exitcode=$code" >> "$GITHUB_OUTPUT"
          terraform show -no-color tf.plan > plan.txt
          # 0 = no changes, 2 = changes present, 1 = error
          [ $code -eq 1 ] && exit 1 || exit 0

      - name: Flag destructive changes
        working-directory: infra/envs/${{ matrix.env }}
        run: |
          terraform show -json tf.plan > plan.json
          DESTROYS=$(jq '[.resource_changes[]
            | select(.change.actions | index("delete"))] | length' plan.json)
          echo "Resources to be destroyed: $DESTROYS"
          if [ "$DESTROYS" -gt 0 ]; then
            echo "::warning::This plan destroys $DESTROYS resource(s) in ${{ matrix.env }}"
            jq -r '.resource_changes[]
              | select(.change.actions | index("delete")) | "  - " + .address' plan.json
          fi

      - uses: actions/github-script@v7
        with:
          script: |
            const fs = require('fs');
            const plan = fs.readFileSync(
              'infra/envs/${{ matrix.env }}/plan.txt', 'utf8'
            ).slice(0, 60000);
            const changed = '${{ steps.plan.outputs.exitcode }}' === '2';
            await github.rest.issues.createComment({
              issue_number: context.issue.number,
              owner: context.repo.owner,
              repo: context.repo.repo,
              body: `### Terraform plan: \`${{ matrix.env }}\`\n` +
                    `${changed ? 'Changes detected.' : 'No changes.'}\n\n` +
                    '<details><summary>Plan output</summary>\n\n' +
                    '```hcl\n' + plan + '\n```\n</details>'
            });
    YAML.github/workflows/deploy.yml
    name: Deploy

on:
  push:
    branches: [main]
  workflow_dispatch:
    inputs:
      environment:
        type: choice
        options: [dev, prod]
        default: dev

# Never run two deploys to the same environment at once. cancel-in-progress
# is false on purpose: killing a half-finished deploy is worse than queueing.
concurrency:
  group: deploy-${{ inputs.environment || 'dev' }}
  cancel-in-progress: false

permissions:
  contents: read
  id-token: write

jobs:
  build:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    outputs:
      digest: ${{ steps.push.outputs.digest }}
      tag: ${{ steps.meta.outputs.tag }}

    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4

      - uses: aws-actions/configure-aws-credentials@v4
        with:
          role-to-assume: arn:aws:iam::${{ secrets.AWS_ACCOUNT_ID }}:role/idp-${{ inputs.environment || 'dev' }}-gha-apply
          aws-region: eu-west-1

      - id: login
        uses: aws-actions/amazon-ecr-login@v2

      - id: meta
        run: echo "tag=sha-${GITHUB_SHA::12}" >> "$GITHUB_OUTPUT"

      - uses: docker/setup-buildx-action@v3

      - id: push
        uses: docker/build-push-action@v6
        with:
          context: .
          file: apps/api/Dockerfile
          platforms: linux/arm64
          push: true
          provenance: false
          tags: ${{ steps.login.outputs.registry }}/idp-${{ inputs.environment || 'dev' }}-api:${{ steps.meta.outputs.tag }}
          # Cache layers in the registry so a dependency-only rebuild is fast.
          cache-from: type=gha
          cache-to: type=gha,mode=max

      - name: Fail on HIGH or CRITICAL vulnerabilities
        uses: aquasecurity/trivy-action@master
        with:
          image-ref: ${{ steps.login.outputs.registry }}/idp-${{ inputs.environment || 'dev' }}-api@${{ steps.push.outputs.digest }}
          severity: HIGH,CRITICAL
          exit-code: '1'
          ignore-unfixed: true

  deploy:
    needs: build
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    # GitHub environment protection: required reviewers, wait timers, and
    # branch restrictions live here. This is also what makes the OIDC
    # `environment:` sub claim meaningful.
    environment: ${{ inputs.environment || 'dev' }}

    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4

      - uses: aws-actions/configure-aws-credentials@v4
        with:
          role-to-assume: arn:aws:iam::${{ secrets.AWS_ACCOUNT_ID }}:role/idp-${{ inputs.environment || 'dev' }}-gha-apply
          aws-region: eu-west-1

      - uses: hashicorp/setup-terraform@v3
        with:
          terraform_version: 1.10.5
          terraform_wrapper: false

      # 1. Infrastructure first. The image URI is an input, so the task
      #    definition revision is created here.
      - name: Terraform apply
        working-directory: infra/envs/${{ inputs.environment || 'dev' }}
        run: |
          terraform init -input=false
          terraform apply -input=false -auto-approve \
            -var="api_image_uri=${{ needs.build.outputs.digest_uri }}" \
            -var="git_sha=${{ needs.build.outputs.tag }}"

      # 2. Migrations BEFORE the new code serves traffic. Backward-compatible
      #    by rule, so the currently-running old version keeps working.
      - name: Run migrations as a one-off task
        run: |
          TASK_ARN=$(aws ecs run-task \
            --cluster idp-${{ inputs.environment || 'dev' }} \
            --task-definition idp-${{ inputs.environment || 'dev' }}-migrate \
            --launch-type FARGATE \
            --network-configuration "awsvpcConfiguration={subnets=[${{ vars.APP_SUBNETS }}],securityGroups=[${{ vars.MIGRATE_SG }}],assignPublicIp=DISABLED}" \
            --query 'tasks[0].taskArn' --output text)

          aws ecs wait tasks-stopped \
            --cluster idp-${{ inputs.environment || 'dev' }} --tasks "$TASK_ARN"

          EXIT=$(aws ecs describe-tasks \
            --cluster idp-${{ inputs.environment || 'dev' }} --tasks "$TASK_ARN" \
            --query 'tasks[0].containers[0].exitCode' --output text)

          echo "Migration exit code: $EXIT"
          if [ "$EXIT" != "0" ]; then
            aws logs tail /ecs/idp-${{ inputs.environment || 'dev' }}-migrate --since 10m
            exit 1
          fi

      # 3. Roll the service. The circuit breaker handles a bad image for us.
      - name: Deploy service
        run: |
          aws ecs update-service \
            --cluster idp-${{ inputs.environment || 'dev' }} \
            --service idp-${{ inputs.environment || 'dev' }}-api \
            --task-definition idp-${{ inputs.environment || 'dev' }}-api \
            --force-new-deployment > /dev/null

          aws ecs wait services-stable \
            --cluster idp-${{ inputs.environment || 'dev' }} \
            --services idp-${{ inputs.environment || 'dev' }}-api

      # 4. Prove it works before calling the deploy successful.
      - name: Smoke test
        run: |
          BASE="https://api-${{ inputs.environment || 'dev' }}.idp.example.com"

          for i in $(seq 1 20); do
            CODE=$(curl -s -o /dev/null -w '%{http_code}' "$BASE/healthz" || true)
            [ "$CODE" = "200" ] && break
            sleep 5
          done
          [ "$CODE" = "200" ] || { echo "healthz never became healthy"; exit 1; }

          # Confirm the deployed version is the commit we just built.
          DEPLOYED=$(curl -s "$BASE/healthz" | jq -r .version)
          echo "Deployed version: $DEPLOYED"
          [ "$DEPLOYED" = "${{ needs.build.outputs.tag }}" ] \
            || { echo "version mismatch: expected ${{ needs.build.outputs.tag }}"; exit 1; }

          # readyz proves dependencies are reachable, not just the process.
          curl -sf "$BASE/readyz" | jq -e '.ready == true'

      - name: Notify on failure
        if: failure()
        run: |
          aws sns publish --topic-arn "${{ vars.PAGE_TOPIC_ARN }}" \
            --subject "Deploy failed: ${{ github.sha }}" \
            --message "Workflow: ${{ github.server_url }}/${{ github.repository }}/actions/runs/${{ github.run_id }}"
    YAML.github/workflows/drift.yml
    name: Drift detection

# Someone will change something in the console. This finds out.
on:
  schedule:
    - cron: '0 7 * * 1-5'
  workflow_dispatch:

permissions:
  contents: read
  id-token: write
  issues: write

jobs:
  drift:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4
      - uses: aws-actions/configure-aws-credentials@v4
        with:
          role-to-assume: arn:aws:iam::${{ secrets.AWS_ACCOUNT_ID }}:role/idp-prod-gha-plan
          aws-region: eu-west-1
      - uses: hashicorp/setup-terraform@v3
        with: { terraform_version: 1.10.5, terraform_wrapper: false }

      - id: plan
        working-directory: infra/envs/prod
        run: |
          terraform init -input=false
          set +e
          terraform plan -input=false -no-color -detailed-exitcode > drift.txt 2>&1
          echo "code=$?" >> "$GITHUB_OUTPUT"
          set -e
          tail -100 drift.txt

      - if: steps.plan.outputs.code == '2'
        uses: actions/github-script@v7
        with:
          script: |
            const fs = require('fs');
            const body = fs.readFileSync('infra/envs/prod/drift.txt', 'utf8').slice(0, 55000);
            await github.rest.issues.create({
              owner: context.repo.owner,
              repo: context.repo.repo,
              title: `Infrastructure drift detected in prod (${new Date().toISOString().slice(0,10)})`,
              labels: ['drift', 'infrastructure'],
              body: 'Terraform plan is not empty against prod. Either someone ' +
                    'changed something outside Terraform, or a provider ' +
                    'upgrade changed a default.\n\n```hcl\n' + body + '\n```'
            });

    §3Interview questions on this material

    How does your pipeline authenticate to AWS?

    OIDC federation, so there is no AWS credential stored in GitHub at all. GitHub mints a short-lived signed JWT for each workflow run describing that run: the repository, the ref, the environment, the workflow. I registered GitHub's OIDC provider in my account, and my deployment role's trust policy says that provider may assume the role only when the token's sub claim matches an exact value. The workflow calls AssumeRoleWithWebIdentity and gets credentials valid for one hour.

    Two roles, with different trust conditions. The plan role is assumable from any branch in the repo, because a plan is read-only and I want plans posted on pull requests. The apply role uses StringEquals against repo:org/repo:environment:prod, which means only a run that has passed the GitHub environment's protection rules can assume it.

    The failure mode I would call out: if you write that condition with StringLike and a wildcard, any branch can assume the role, and on a public repo anyone who can open a pull request can then run code with your deploy permissions. Pin the ref or the environment exactly. Also, the workflow needs permissions: id-token: write, and forgetting that produces a confusing error about no identity token being available.

    How do database migrations fit into the deployment?

    They run as a separate step before the new application version serves traffic, as a one-off ECS task using the same image with a different command, inside the VPC, with a role that has DDL rights the API's role does not have. The pipeline waits for the task to stop and checks the container exit code. A non-zero exit fails the deploy and dumps the migration logs into the workflow output.

    The property that makes this safe is that migrations are backward compatible with the currently deployed code, by rule. During a rolling ECS deploy, old and new tasks run against the same schema for a minute or two, so a migration that breaks the old version breaks production for the duration of the rollout. That rule gives concrete constraints: add columns nullable or with a default, never rename in a single step, never drop a column in the same release that stops using it.

    It also simplifies rollback. Because the schema change is compatible with both versions, rolling application code back is just redeploying the previous image digest. I never need to run a down migration against production, which is a genuinely risky operation under load.

    How do you review infrastructure changes safely?

    Plan on the pull request, apply on merge, and never re-plan at apply time. A pull request touching infra/ runs format check, validate, tfsec, and a plan for each environment, then posts the plan as a collapsed comment so the reviewer sees exactly what will change.

    One addition I would highlight: the workflow parses terraform show -json and counts resources with a delete action, then emits a GitHub warning annotation listing them. Reading a long plan diff, it is easy to miss that a change forces replacement of a database. Making destroys loud is cheap and has caught real mistakes.

    Applies are gated by a GitHub environment with required reviewers, which also ties into the OIDC condition, so the credential and the approval are the same gate rather than two separate ones. And a scheduled drift workflow plans against prod every weekday morning, opening an issue if the plan is not empty. That catches console changes and provider upgrades that quietly alter a default, which is the class of problem you otherwise discover during an unrelated deploy.

    §4Definition of done

    Sprint 10 · Production

    The frontend, CloudFront, and making async feel fast

    A React app served from S3 through CloudFront, with the interesting problems being uploads with progress, telling the user about work that takes two minutes, and cache headers that let you deploy without users seeing a stale bundle.

    Time
    2–3 days
    You'll produce
    Vite SPA, CloudFront with OAC, upload UX, status polling, search UI
    Running cost
    ~$1/mo at portfolio traffic
    Key idea
    Hash your assets, never cache your HTML

    §1Fundamentals: what a CDN does, and the three things to get right

    CloudFront is a content delivery network. It has hundreds of edge locations worldwide; a user's request hits the nearest one, and if that edge has the file cached it responds immediately without touching your origin. If not, it fetches from the origin, caches it according to your rules, and serves it.

    For a static single-page app there are exactly three things to configure correctly. Get them right and you never think about the CDN again.

    1. Origin Access Control, so the bucket stays private

    The naive setup makes the S3 bucket a public website endpoint. Now anyone can bypass CloudFront and hit S3 directly, which skips your security headers, your WAF, and your logging. Origin Access Control is the fix: CloudFront signs its requests to S3 with SigV4, and the bucket policy allows only that specific distribution. The bucket stays completely private.

    You may see Origin Access Identity in older tutorials. That is the previous generation. OAC supports SSE-KMS and all HTTP methods; prefer it.

    2. SPA routing, which is a 403 problem not a 404 problem

    Your app uses client-side routing, so /documents/abc-123 is a route React handles. But the browser asks CloudFront for that path, CloudFront asks S3, and S3 has no such object. With OAC and a private bucket, S3 returns 403 Forbidden, not 404, because the caller has no permission to know whether the key exists.

    So the custom error response must map both 403 and 404 to /index.html with a 200 status. Almost every "my routes 404 in production" question comes down to mapping only 404.

    3. Cache headers, which are the whole deploy story

    This is the part that determines whether users see a stale app after you deploy.

    FileCache-ControlWhy
    assets/index-a3f9c2.jspublic, max-age=31536000, immutableThe filename contains a content hash. New content means a new filename, so this file can be cached forever. Vite generates these hashes for you.
    index.htmlno-cache, must-revalidateIt references the hashed filenames. It must always be revalidated, or users load an old HTML pointing at deleted assets.
    /api/*No caching at allAuthenticated, per-user, dynamic.

    Get this pairing right and deploys are safe with a single invalidation of /index.html rather than /*. It also means an in-flight user keeps working from their cached assets while a new visitor gets the new version.

    Deploy order matters

    Upload the hashed assets first, then index.html. If you upload HTML first, a user who loads it in the next few seconds asks for asset filenames that do not exist yet and gets a blank page. Also keep the previous build's assets around rather than syncing with --delete immediately: a user who loaded the old HTML a minute ago will still request the old chunks.

    HCLinfra/modules/frontend/main.tf
    resource "aws_s3_bucket" "web" {
  bucket = "${local.name}-web-${data.aws_caller_identity.current.account_id}"
}

resource "aws_s3_bucket_public_access_block" "web" {
  bucket                  = aws_s3_bucket.web.id
  block_public_acls       = true
  block_public_policy     = true
  ignore_public_acls      = true
  restrict_public_buckets = true
}

# OAC: CloudFront signs requests to S3. The bucket has no public access.
resource "aws_cloudfront_origin_access_control" "web" {
  name                              = "${local.name}-web-oac"
  origin_access_control_origin_type = "s3"
  signing_behavior                  = "always"
  signing_protocol                  = "sigv4"
}

data "aws_iam_policy_document" "web_bucket" {
  statement {
    effect    = "Allow"
    actions   = ["s3:GetObject"]
    resources = ["${aws_s3_bucket.web.arn}/*"]

    principals {
      type        = "Service"
      identifiers = ["cloudfront.amazonaws.com"]
    }

    # Only THIS distribution. Not CloudFront in general.
    condition {
      test     = "StringEquals"
      variable = "AWS:SourceArn"
      values   = [aws_cloudfront_distribution.web.arn]
    }
  }
}

resource "aws_s3_bucket_policy" "web" {
  bucket = aws_s3_bucket.web.id
  policy = data.aws_iam_policy_document.web_bucket.json
}

# Security headers applied at the edge, so they cannot be forgotten in app code.
resource "aws_cloudfront_response_headers_policy" "security" {
  name = "${local.name}-security-headers"

  security_headers_config {
    strict_transport_security {
      access_control_max_age_sec = 63072000
      include_subdomains         = true
      preload                    = true
      override                   = true
    }
    content_type_options {
      override = true            # nosniff
    }
    frame_options {
      frame_option = "DENY"
      override     = true
    }
    referrer_policy {
      referrer_policy = "strict-origin-when-cross-origin"
      override        = true
    }
    content_security_policy {
      override = true
      # No unsafe-inline on script-src. Vite's production build does not need it.
      content_security_policy = join("; ", [
        "default-src 'self'",
        "script-src 'self'",
        "style-src 'self' 'unsafe-inline'",
        "img-src 'self' data: blob:",
        "font-src 'self'",
        "connect-src 'self' https://${var.api_domain} https://${var.cognito_domain} https://cognito-idp.${var.region}.amazonaws.com",
        "frame-ancestors 'none'",
        "base-uri 'self'",
        "form-action 'self'",
      ])
    }
  }
}

resource "aws_cloudfront_distribution" "web" {
  enabled             = true
  is_ipv6_enabled     = true
  default_root_object = "index.html"
  aliases             = [var.domain]
  # PriceClass_100 is North America and Europe only, and is noticeably cheaper.
  price_class         = "PriceClass_100"
  web_acl_id          = var.waf_acl_arn

  origin {
    origin_id                = "s3-web"
    domain_name              = aws_s3_bucket.web.bucket_regional_domain_name
    origin_access_control_id = aws_cloudfront_origin_access_control.web.id
  }

  # The API as a second origin, so the SPA and the API share one domain and
  # there is no CORS preflight on every request.
  origin {
    origin_id   = "alb-api"
    domain_name = var.alb_domain

    custom_origin_config {
      http_port              = 80
      https_port             = 443
      origin_protocol_policy = "https-only"
      origin_ssl_protocols   = ["TLSv1.2"]
    }
  }

  default_cache_behavior {
    target_origin_id       = "s3-web"
    viewer_protocol_policy = "redirect-to-https"
    allowed_methods        = ["GET", "HEAD", "OPTIONS"]
    cached_methods         = ["GET", "HEAD"]
    compress               = true

    # Managed CachingOptimized policy. Cache-Control from S3 does the work.
    cache_policy_id            = "658327ea-f89d-4fab-a63d-7e88639e58f6"
    response_headers_policy_id = aws_cloudfront_response_headers_policy.security.id
  }

  ordered_cache_behavior {
    path_pattern           = "/api/*"
    target_origin_id       = "alb-api"
    viewer_protocol_policy = "https-only"
    allowed_methods        = ["GET", "HEAD", "OPTIONS", "PUT", "POST", "PATCH", "DELETE"]
    cached_methods         = ["GET", "HEAD"]
    compress               = true

    # CachingDisabled, and AllViewerExceptHostHeader so Authorization
    # reaches the origin. Forgetting the origin request policy means the API
    # sees no auth header and returns 401 for everything.
    cache_policy_id          = "4135ea2d-6df8-44a3-9df3-4b5a84be39ad"
    origin_request_policy_id = "b689b0a8-53d0-40ab-baf2-68738e2966ac"
  }

  # BOTH 403 and 404. With a private bucket S3 returns 403 for a missing key,
  # and mapping only 404 is why client-side routes appear broken in production.
  custom_error_response {
    error_code            = 403
    response_code         = 200
    response_page_path    = "/index.html"
    error_caching_min_ttl = 10
  }

  custom_error_response {
    error_code            = 404
    response_code         = 200
    response_page_path    = "/index.html"
    error_caching_min_ttl = 10
  }

  viewer_certificate {
    # ACM certificates for CloudFront MUST live in us-east-1, whatever
    # region everything else is in.
    acm_certificate_arn      = var.acm_certificate_arn
    ssl_support_method       = "sni-only"
    minimum_protocol_version = "TLSv1.2_2021"
  }

  restrictions {
    geo_restriction {
      restriction_type = "none"
    }
  }

  logging_config {
    bucket = var.log_bucket_domain
    prefix = "cloudfront/"
  }
}
    Shellthe deploy script, in the right order
    #!/usr/bin/env bash
set -euo pipefail
BUCKET="$1"; DIST="$2"

npm -w apps/web run build

# 1. Hashed assets first, cached for a year. No --delete: users mid-session
#    may still request the previous build's chunks.
aws s3 sync apps/web/dist/assets "s3://$BUCKET/assets" \
  --cache-control 'public, max-age=31536000, immutable' \
  --exclude '*.map'

# 2. Other static files with a moderate TTL.
aws s3 sync apps/web/dist "s3://$BUCKET" \
  --exclude 'assets/*' --exclude 'index.html' --exclude '*.map' \
  --cache-control 'public, max-age=3600'

# 3. index.html LAST, never cached. It is the pointer to everything above.
aws s3 cp apps/web/dist/index.html "s3://$BUCKET/index.html" \
  --cache-control 'no-cache, no-store, must-revalidate' \
  --content-type 'text/html; charset=utf-8'

# 4. Invalidate only the HTML. Invalidating /* is slower and, past 1,000
#    paths a month, costs money for no benefit.
aws cloudfront create-invalidation --distribution-id "$DIST" \
  --paths '/index.html' '/' --query 'Invalidation.Id' --output text

    §2The upload experience

    Three steps for the user, and the middle one needs a progress bar. fetch cannot report upload progress, so this is one of the few places where XMLHttpRequest is still the right tool.

    TypeScriptapps/web/src/features/upload/useUpload.ts
    import { useState, useCallback } from 'react';
import { api } from '../../lib/api';

export type UploadState =
  | { phase: 'idle' }
  | { phase: 'hashing' }
  | { phase: 'requesting' }
  | { phase: 'uploading'; percent: number; bytesPerSecond: number }
  | { phase: 'processing'; documentId: string; status: string }
  | { phase: 'done'; documentId: string }
  | { phase: 'error'; message: string; retryable: boolean };

/** SHA-256 in the browser. Gives the server dedupe and gives S3 integrity
 *  verification. For a 50MB file this takes well under a second. */
async function sha256Base64(file: File): Promise<string> {
  const digest = await crypto.subtle.digest('SHA-256', await file.arrayBuffer());
  return btoa(String.fromCharCode(...new Uint8Array(digest)));
}

export function useUpload() {
  const [state, setState] = useState<UploadState>({ phase: 'idle' });

  const upload = useCallback(async (file: File) => {
    try {
      // Client-side validation first, so an obviously wrong file never costs
      // a round trip. The server validates again; this is UX, not security.
      const ALLOWED = ['application/pdf', 'image/png', 'image/jpeg', 'image/tiff'];
      if (!ALLOWED.includes(file.type)) {
        setState({ phase: 'error', message: `${file.type || 'This file type'} is not supported. Use PDF, PNG, JPEG or TIFF.`, retryable: false });
        return;
      }
      if (file.size > 50 * 1024 * 1024) {
        setState({ phase: 'error', message: `That file is ${(file.size / 1e6).toFixed(1)} MB. The limit is 50 MB.`, retryable: false });
        return;
      }

      setState({ phase: 'hashing' });
      const checksum = await sha256Base64(file);

      setState({ phase: 'requesting' });
      const grant = await api.post('/v1/uploads', {
        filename: file.name,
        mimeType: file.type,
        byteSize: file.size,
        checksumSha256: checksum,
      });

      // 409 means we already have these exact bytes. Treat it as success and
      // show the existing document rather than as an error.
      if (grant.status === 409) {
        setState({ phase: 'done', documentId: grant.body.error.documentId });
        return;
      }

      const { documentId, uploadUrl, requiredHeaders } = grant.body;

      // XHR, because fetch has no upload progress event.
      await new Promise<void>((resolve, reject) => {
        const xhr = new XMLHttpRequest();
        xhr.open('PUT', uploadUrl, true);

        // Send EXACTLY the headers the API signed. A mismatch is the cause of
        // almost every 403 SignatureDoesNotMatch.
        for (const [k, v] of Object.entries(requiredHeaders as Record<string, string>)) {
          xhr.setRequestHeader(k, v);
        }

        const started = Date.now();
        xhr.upload.onprogress = (e) => {
          if (!e.lengthComputable) return;
          const elapsed = (Date.now() - started) / 1000;
          setState({
            phase: 'uploading',
            percent: Math.round((e.loaded / e.total) * 100),
            bytesPerSecond: elapsed > 0 ? e.loaded / elapsed : 0,
          });
        };

        xhr.onload = () => (xhr.status >= 200 && xhr.status < 300
          ? resolve()
          : reject(new Error(`S3 rejected the upload (${xhr.status})`)));
        xhr.onerror = () => reject(new Error('Network error during upload'));
        xhr.ontimeout = () => reject(new Error('Upload timed out'));
        xhr.timeout = 120_000;
