        xhr.send(file);
      });

      setState({ phase: 'processing', documentId, status: 'uploaded' });
    } catch (err) {
      setState({
        phase: 'error',
        message: err instanceof Error ? err.message : 'Upload failed',
        retryable: true,
      });
    }
  }, []);

  return { state, upload, reset: () => setState({ phase: 'idle' }) };
}

    Telling the user about work that takes two minutes

    Processing runs asynchronously, so the frontend has to learn about status changes somehow. Four options, and the right choice depends on how long the work takes and how many users are watching.

    ApproachProsConsVerdict here
    Polling with backoffTrivial to build, works everywhere, survives reconnects for freeWasted requests, latency up to one intervalChosen. Processing takes 30–120s, so a few polls is fine.
    Server-Sent EventsReal-time, one-way, plain HTTP, auto-reconnect built into the browserHolds a connection per viewer, which costs a Fargate slot; ALB idle timeout needs raisingGood upgrade if users watch many documents at once
    WebSocketsBidirectionalWe have nothing to send upstream; more infrastructureOver-engineering for this
    API Gateway WebSocket + DynamoDB connectionsScales without holding connections on your computeA whole extra subsystem to build and pay forOnly at much larger scale
    TypeScriptapps/web/src/features/documents/useDocumentStatus.ts
    import { useQuery } from '@tanstack/react-query';
import { api } from '../../lib/api';

const TERMINAL = new Set(['ready', 'failed', 'quarantined']);

/**
 * Polls a document's status, backing off as it goes and stopping once the
 * status is terminal. The backoff matters: a hundred tabs polling every second
 * is a self-inflicted load test.
 */
export function useDocumentStatus(documentId: string | null) {
  return useQuery({
    queryKey: ['document', documentId],
    enabled: Boolean(documentId),
    queryFn: () => api.get(`/v1/documents/${documentId}`).then((r) => r.body),

    refetchInterval: (query) => {
      const status = query.state.data?.status;
      if (status && TERMINAL.has(status)) return false;   // stop entirely

      // 2s for the first 30s, then 5s, then 15s after two minutes.
      const elapsed = Date.now() - (query.state.dataUpdatedAt || Date.now());
      const attempts = query.state.dataUpdateCount ?? 0;
      if (attempts < 15) return 2000;
      if (attempts < 40) return 5000;
      return 15_000;
    },

    // Keep polling when the tab is hidden? No. Users leave tabs open for days.
    refetchIntervalInBackground: false,

    // Give up after roughly ten minutes and tell the user something is wrong,
    // rather than spinning forever.
    retry: (failureCount) => failureCount < 3,
  });
}

/** Human-readable stage labels. "extracting" means nothing to a user. */
export const STATUS_COPY: Record<string, { label: string; detail: string; percent: number }> = {
  pending_upload: { label: 'Waiting for upload', detail: 'The file has not finished uploading.', percent: 5 },
  uploaded:       { label: 'Queued',             detail: 'Your document is in the queue.',      percent: 20 },
  extracting:     { label: 'Reading text',       detail: 'Running optical character recognition.', percent: 45 },
  extracted:      { label: 'Text extracted',     detail: 'Now analysing the content.',          percent: 65 },
  enriching:      { label: 'Analysing',          detail: 'Classifying and pulling out key fields.', percent: 85 },
  ready:          { label: 'Ready',              detail: 'Fully processed and searchable.',      percent: 100 },
  failed:         { label: 'Failed',             detail: 'Something went wrong. See details below.', percent: 100 },
  quarantined:    { label: 'Rejected',           detail: 'This file could not be accepted.',     percent: 100 },
};

    §3Interview questions on this material

    How do you serve a single-page app from S3 and CloudFront?

    Private S3 bucket with Origin Access Control, so CloudFront signs its requests with SigV4 and the bucket policy allows only that distribution. The bucket has no public access at all, which means nobody can bypass CloudFront and skip the security headers, the WAF, and the access logs.

    For client-side routes, a custom error response maps both 403 and 404 to /index.html with a 200 status. Mapping only 404 is the usual bug: with a private bucket, a missing key returns 403 rather than 404, because the caller has no permission to know whether it exists. That one detail is behind most "my routes work locally but 404 in production" reports.

    Caching is the part that determines whether deploys are safe. Vite emits content-hashed asset filenames, so those get max-age=31536000, immutable. The index.html that points at them gets no-cache, must-revalidate. Deploy order is assets first, HTML last, and I invalidate only /index.html rather than /*. That also means a user mid-session keeps working from their cached chunks, which is why I do not sync with --delete straight away.

    I also serve the API through the same distribution as a second origin with a caching-disabled behaviour, which removes CORS preflights entirely. The gotcha there is needing the AllViewerExceptHostHeader origin request policy, or the Authorization header never reaches the origin and every request comes back 401.

    How does the frontend know when an asynchronous document is ready?

    Polling with backoff, which I chose deliberately over the more sophisticated options.

    Processing takes thirty seconds to two minutes, so polling every two seconds for the first thirty seconds, then every five, then every fifteen, converges quickly without many wasted requests. Polling stops entirely once the status is terminal, and does not run while the tab is hidden, because people leave tabs open for days. TanStack Query handles the interval and the caching.

    Server-Sent Events would be the natural upgrade. They are one-way, plain HTTP, and the browser reconnects automatically. The reason I did not start there is that each viewer holds an open connection on a Fargate task, which changes how the service scales, and the ALB idle timeout needs raising. Worth it when a user watches many documents at once; not worth it for a single upload.

    The user-facing part I would emphasise is the status copy. Internal statuses like extracting and enriching mean nothing to a customer, so each one maps to a human label, a sentence of explanation, and a rough percentage. A progress bar that moves through named stages makes ninety seconds feel like progress instead of a hang.

    Where do you store the auth tokens in the browser, and why?

    Access token in memory only, in a module-scoped variable. Refresh token in an httpOnly, Secure, SameSite=Strict cookie set by a small backend endpoint that performs the code exchange.

    The reasoning is about what an XSS payload can reach. Anything in localStorage or sessionStorage is readable by any script on the page, so a single injected script can exfiltrate a refresh token and hold a persistent session. An httpOnly cookie is not readable by JavaScript at all, so the same XSS can make requests as the user while the page is open but cannot steal a durable credential.

    The trade-off I accept: cookies bring CSRF into scope, which SameSite=Strict plus the API requiring an Authorization header rather than accepting cookie-only auth addresses. And it costs me a small backend route, so it is no longer a pure static SPA.

    What I would not do is put the refresh token in localStorage because it is simpler. That is a very common pattern and it converts one XSS into account takeover. If the trade were forced on me by architecture, I would shorten refresh lifetimes hard and enable rotation so a stolen token is detectable on reuse.

    Sprint 11 · Production

    Hardening, cost, disaster recovery, teardown

    The platform works. This sprint asks harder questions of it: what happens when someone attacks it, what happens when a Region has a bad day, what it actually costs, and how you switch it off when you are not demoing. It also produces the artefacts that make the project defensible in a conversation.

    Time

    §1Threat modelling, in one page

    Threat modelling sounds heavyweight. Done simply, it is four questions asked about each part of the system, and it takes an afternoon. The framework worth knowing by name is STRIDE.

    ThreatConcretely, hereMitigation in place
    Spoofing identitySomeone forges a token to read another tenant's documentsRS256 signature verified against the pool's JWKS; token_use, issuer, and audience all checked; tenant read from a signed claim only
    TamperingModifying a document after upload, or altering extracted totalsS3 versioning; SHA-256 checksum verified on upload; append-only audit log; API role explicitly denied DeleteObject
    Repudiation"I never uploaded that" or "I did not approve that"Audit log with actor and timestamp; CloudTrail for control-plane actions; S3 access logs
    Information disclosureCross-tenant leakage; a leaked presigned URL; secrets in logsRow-level security failing closed; presigned URLs scoped to one key with a 15-minute expiry; pino redaction of tokens and URLs; KMS with a restricted key policy
    Denial of serviceUpload flood; expensive-query abuse; a bill-driven attackRate limiting per token; WAF rate rules; per-tenant storage quota; Lambda concurrency caps; budget alarms
    Elevation of privilegeViewer performs an owner action; prompt injection escalating into system accessRole hierarchy enforced server-side; model output is only ever data, never an instruction the system executes; task role has no IAM write permissions

    Write this table into docs/threat-model.md. When someone asks how you thought about security, having a document is a stronger answer than reciting practices.

    §2WAF, and the rules that matter

    A Web Application Firewall inspects requests before they reach your application. Managed rule groups cover the common categories, and two custom rules do the work specific to this platform.

    HCLinfra/modules/security/waf.tf
    # CLOUDFRONT scope means this WAF must be created in us-east-1, regardless of
# where the rest of the stack lives.
resource "aws_wafv2_web_acl" "main" {
  name  = "${local.name}-waf"
  scope = "CLOUDFRONT"

  default_action {
    allow {}
  }

  # ── Managed rules. Start every one of these in COUNT mode, watch the metrics
  # for a week, then switch to block. Turning them straight to block is how you
  # discover that a legitimate feature looks like an attack.
  rule {
    name     = "AWSManagedCommonRuleSet"
    priority = 1

    override_action {
      none {}
    }

    statement {
      managed_rule_group_statement {
        vendor_name = "AWS"
        name        = "AWSManagedRulesCommonRuleSet"

        # 50MB uploads legitimately exceed the default body size rule, and
        # our uploads go straight to S3 anyway, so this check is not useful here.
        rule_action_override {
          name = "SizeRestrictions_BODY"
          action_to_use {
            count {}
          }
        }
      }
    }

    visibility_config {
      cloudwatch_metrics_enabled = true
      metric_name                = "common-rules"
      sampled_requests_enabled   = true
    }
  }

  rule {
    name     = "KnownBadInputs"
    priority = 2

    override_action {
      none {}
    }

    statement {
      managed_rule_group_statement {
        vendor_name = "AWS"
        name        = "AWSManagedRulesKnownBadInputsRuleSet"
      }
    }

    visibility_config {
      cloudwatch_metrics_enabled = true
      metric_name                = "bad-inputs"
      sampled_requests_enabled   = true
    }
  }

  # ── Custom rule: rate limit by IP across the whole API.
  rule {
    name     = "RateLimitPerIp"
    priority = 10

    action {
      block {}
    }

    statement {
      rate_based_statement {
        limit              = 2000    # requests per 5-minute window per IP
        aggregate_key_type = "IP"

        scope_down_statement {
          byte_match_statement {
            positional_constraint = "STARTS_WITH"
            search_string         = "/api/"
            field_to_match {
              uri_path {}
            }
            text_transformation {
              priority = 0
              type     = "NONE"
            }
          }
        }
      }
    }

    visibility_config {
      cloudwatch_metrics_enabled = true
      metric_name                = "rate-limit-ip"
      sampled_requests_enabled   = true
    }
  }

  # ── Custom rule: a much tighter limit on the endpoint that costs us money.
  # Every upload grant leads to Textract and Bedrock spend, so abuse here is
  # a financial attack rather than a availability one.
  rule {
    name     = "RateLimitUploads"
    priority = 11

    action {
      block {}
    }

    statement {
      rate_based_statement {
        limit              = 100
        aggregate_key_type = "IP"

        scope_down_statement {
          byte_match_statement {
            positional_constraint = "EXACTLY"
            search_string         = "/api/v1/uploads"
            field_to_match {
              uri_path {}
            }
            text_transformation {
              priority = 0
              type     = "LOWERCASE"
            }
          }
        }
      }
    }

    visibility_config {
      cloudwatch_metrics_enabled = true
      metric_name                = "rate-limit-uploads"
      sampled_requests_enabled   = true
    }
  }

  visibility_config {
    cloudwatch_metrics_enabled = true
    metric_name                = "${local.name}-waf"
    sampled_requests_enabled   = true
  }
}

# ── Account-level detection. GuardDuty analyses CloudTrail, VPC flow logs, and
# DNS logs for known-bad patterns. It costs a few dollars a month and catches
# things you would never write a rule for, like credential use from an
# unexpected geography.
resource "aws_guardduty_detector" "main" {
  enable = true

  datasources {
    s3_logs {
      enable = true
    }
  }
}

# IAM Access Analyzer: finds resources reachable from outside the account, and
# reports permissions granted but never used.
resource "aws_accessanalyzer_analyzer" "account" {
  analyzer_name = "${local.name}-external-access"
  type          = "ACCOUNT"
}
    Count mode first, always

    A managed rule group in block mode on day one will break something legitimate, and you will find out from a user rather than from a dashboard. Deploy in count mode, look at the CountedRequests metric and the sampled requests for a week, add rule action overrides for the false positives, then switch to block. This sequencing is the difference between WAF being useful and WAF being the thing everyone disabled after an incident.

    §3Data protection and the right to erasure

    A document platform holding customer data has legal obligations. Two mechanisms cover most of it.

    Retention is per-tenant configuration, enforced by a scheduled job rather than by an S3 lifecycle rule, because the rule for "delete 90 days after the tenant's retention setting" cannot be expressed in bucket configuration.

    Erasure is harder than it sounds, and being able to explain why is a good sign. A GDPR erasure request has to reach every copy: the S3 object and all its versions, the database rows, the derived artefacts, the search embeddings, the CloudWatch logs that mentioned it, and any backup. Backups are the awkward one, because you cannot surgically edit a snapshot.

    TypeScriptservices/erasure/src/handler.ts
    /**
 * Executes a verified erasure request. Ordering is deliberate: remove the
 * ability to access the data first, then remove the data, then record that we
 * did it. If the process dies halfway, the document is already inaccessible.
 */
export async function eraseDocument(documentId: string, tenantId: string, requestedBy: string) {
  const db = await getDb();
  const log = logger.child({ documentId, tenantId, action: 'erasure' });

  const [doc] = await db.select().from(documents)
    .where(and(eq(documents.id, documentId), eq(documents.tenantId, tenantId)))
    .limit(1);
  if (!doc) throw new Error('document not found');

  // 1. Make it inaccessible immediately.
  await db.update(documents)
    .set({ status: 'quarantined', deletedAt: new Date() })
    .where(eq(documents.id, documentId));

  // 2. Every S3 version, plus the delete markers. A plain DeleteObject on a
  //    versioned bucket only adds a marker; the bytes remain.
  let keyMarker: string | undefined;
  do {
    const versions = await s3.send(new ListObjectVersionsCommand({
      Bucket: doc.s3Bucket,
      Prefix: doc.s3Key,
      KeyMarker: keyMarker,
    }));

    const ids = [
      ...(versions.Versions ?? []),
      ...(versions.DeleteMarkers ?? []),
    ].map((v) => ({ Key: v.Key!, VersionId: v.VersionId! }));

    if (ids.length) {
      await s3.send(new DeleteObjectsCommand({
        Bucket: doc.s3Bucket,
        Delete: { Objects: ids, Quiet: true },
      }));
    }
    keyMarker = versions.NextKeyMarker;
  } while (keyMarker);

  // 3. Derived artefacts: Textract raw blocks, thumbnails, exports.
  await deletePrefix(process.env.DERIVED_BUCKET!, `textract/${tenantId}/${documentId}`);
  await deletePrefix(process.env.DERIVED_BUCKET!, `thumbnails/${tenantId}/${documentId}`);

  // 4. Database rows. ON DELETE CASCADE removes extractions, enrichments,
  //    entities, and chunks, which is exactly why the foreign keys were
  //    declared that way in Sprint 01.
  await db.delete(documents).where(eq(documents.id, documentId));

  // 5. Record the erasure itself, with no content. The audit trail must
  //    survive the data it describes.
  await db.insert(auditLog).values({
    tenantId,
    actor: requestedBy,
    action: 'document.erased',
    subjectId: documentId,
    detail: { filename: hashForAudit(doc.filename), erasedAt: new Date().toISOString() },
  });

  log.info({ event: 'erasure.complete' }, 'document erased');

  // 6. Backups are the honest gap. RDS snapshots and S3 versions inside the
  //    retention window may still contain the data. Documented policy: the
  //    data is erased from production immediately and ages out of backups
  //    within the retention period, which is disclosed in the privacy notice.
  //    Pretending otherwise would be worse than stating it.
}

    §4Backup and restore, actually tested

    Two numbers define your recovery posture, and knowing them by name is expected of a senior engineer.

    • RPO, recovery point objective: how much data you can afford to lose, measured in time. Ours is five minutes, because RDS continuous backup gives point-in-time recovery to any second within the retention window.
    • RTO, recovery time objective: how long recovery may take. Ours is about one hour, dominated by how long an RDS restore takes to become available.

    Both numbers are claims until you have run the drill. Do it once, write down what actually happened, and put the timings in the repo.

    Shelldocs/runbooks/restore-drill.sh
    #!/usr/bin/env bash
# Quarterly restore drill. Restores prod to a NEW instance, verifies the data,
# then destroys it. Never restores over the live database.
set -euo pipefail

SOURCE="idp-prod"
TARGET="idp-restore-drill-$(date +%Y%m%d)"
START=$(date +%s)

echo "== Latest restorable time"
aws rds describe-db-instances --db-instance-identifier "$SOURCE" \
  --query 'DBInstances[0].LatestRestorableTime' --output text

echo "== Restoring to a new instance"
aws rds restore-db-instance-to-point-in-time \
  --source-db-instance-identifier "$SOURCE" \
  --target-db-instance-identifier "$TARGET" \
  --use-latest-restorable-time \
  --db-subnet-group-name idp-prod \
  --vpc-security-group-ids "$DB_SG" \
  --no-multi-az \
  --db-instance-class db.t4g.micro   # cheap: this instance exists for minutes

echo "== Waiting (this is the RTO you are measuring)"
aws rds wait db-instance-available --db-instance-identifier "$TARGET"
RESTORE_SECONDS=$(( $(date +%s) - START ))
echo "Restore completed in ${RESTORE_SECONDS}s"

echo "== Verifying the data is actually usable, not just present"
ENDPOINT=$(aws rds describe-db-instances --db-instance-identifier "$TARGET" \
  --query 'DBInstances[0].Endpoint.Address' --output text)

psql "postgres://idp_admin:$PW@$ENDPOINT:5432/idp" <<'SQL'
\timing on
-- Row counts in the right ballpark?
SELECT 'documents' AS t, count(*) FROM documents
UNION ALL SELECT 'extractions', count(*) FROM extractions
UNION ALL SELECT 'doc_chunks',  count(*) FROM doc_chunks;

-- Is the newest data present? This is the real RPO measurement.
SELECT max(created_at) AS newest_document, now() - max(created_at) AS data_loss
FROM documents;

-- Do the extensions and indexes survive a restore? (They do, but verify.)
SELECT extname FROM pg_extension WHERE extname IN ('vector', 'pgcrypto');
SELECT indexname FROM pg_indexes WHERE indexname = 'doc_chunks_embedding_hnsw';

-- Does a vector query still work?
SELECT count(*) FROM doc_chunks WHERE embedding IS NOT NULL;
SQL

echo "== Cleaning up"
aws rds delete-db-instance --db-instance-identifier "$TARGET" \
  --skip-final-snapshot --delete-automated-backups

cat <<EOF

Record this in docs/dr-drills.md:
  date:             $(date -I)
  restore seconds:  ${RESTORE_SECONDS}
  measured RPO:     (from the data_loss column above)
  problems found:
EOF
    What the drill teaches you

    People discover real things the first time they run this. That the restored instance comes up with the source's parameter group but the default security group. That a restore does not carry over the Multi-AZ setting. That your application's connection string is hard-coded somewhere you forgot. That the restore took forty minutes, not the ten you assumed. Each of those is a bug you found in daylight rather than during an outage.

    §5What this actually costs

    Order-of-magnitude monthly figures for eu-west-1, for a portfolio-scale environment. The numbers move; the shape does not.

    ServiceConfigurationDevProdNotes
    NAT Gateway1 dev / 2 prod$33$66Largest fixed cost. Endpoints reduce the data charge, not the hourly one.
    RDS Postgrest4g.micro single-AZ / t4g.small Multi-AZ$13$50Multi-AZ doubles the instance cost
    Fargate1 × 0.25 vCPU / 2 × 0.5 vCPU$9$36ARM64 saves about 20%
    ALB1$18$20Hourly charge plus capacity units
    VPC endpoints3 interface, 2 AZ$0$44Skip in dev; they pay for themselves in prod
    S350 GB with tiering$1$2Lifecycle rules do the work
    KMS1 key$1$1Bucket Keys keep request costs near zero
    CloudWatchLogs, metrics, alarms$5$18Log retention is the lever
    CloudFront + WAFLow traffic$1$11WAF is $5 base plus per-rule
    Cognito< 50k MAU$0$0Free tier
    SQS, SNS, EventBridge, LambdaLow volume$0$1Free tiers cover it
    Textract1,000 pages$2$12Depends entirely on which API
    Bedrock1,000 documents$3$15Model routing is the lever
    Total~$86~$276

    How to run this for under $15 a month

    1. Destroy dev when you are not using it. The single biggest saving. terraform destroy on the dev root takes about eight minutes and brings you to near zero.
    2. Drop the NAT entirely in dev. Put the Fargate task in a public subnet with a security group that allows no inbound except from the ALB. Less clean, saves $33.
    3. Stop the RDS instance when idle. RDS allows a stop for up to seven days, after which it restarts on its own. Storage is still billed; compute is not.
    4. Set log retention to 7 days in dev. The default is never expire, and that quietly accumulates.
    5. Use Cost Explorer weekly. Group by tag, look at the last seven days. Ten seconds, and it builds the intuition that shows up in cost conversations.
    Shellscripts/teardown-dev.sh
    #!/usr/bin/env bash
# Destroys the dev environment. Written so that using it is frictionless,
# because a teardown you hesitate over is a teardown you skip.
set -euo pipefail

ACCOUNT=$(aws sts get-caller-identity --query Account --output text)
echo "Account: $ACCOUNT"
read -rp "Destroy the DEV environment in this account? (type: destroy dev) " CONFIRM
[ "$CONFIRM" = "destroy dev" ] || { echo "Aborted."; exit 1; }

cd infra/envs/dev

# S3 buckets must be emptied, including all object versions, before destroy.
for B in $(terraform output -json bucket_names | jq -r '.[]'); do
  echo "Emptying $B"
  aws s3api delete-objects --bucket "$B" --delete "$(
    aws s3api list-object-versions --bucket "$B" \
      --query '{Objects: Versions[].{Key:Key,VersionId:VersionId}}' \
      --max-items 1000 --output json
  )" 2>/dev/null || true
  aws s3api delete-objects --bucket "$B" --delete "$(
    aws s3api list-object-versions --bucket "$B" \
      --query '{Objects: DeleteMarkers[].{Key:Key,VersionId:VersionId}}' \
      --max-items 1000 --output json
  )" 2>/dev/null || true
done

# ECR images block repository deletion unless force_delete is set.
aws ecr batch-delete-image --repository-name idp-dev-api \
  --image-ids "$(aws ecr list-images --repository-name idp-dev-api \
    --query 'imageIds[*]' --output json)" 2>/dev/null || true

terraform destroy -auto-approve

echo
echo "Done. Confirm nothing is left:"
echo "  aws resourcegroupstaggingapi get-resources \\"
echo "    --tag-filters Key=Environment,Values=dev \\"
echo "    --query 'ResourceTagMappingList[].ResourceARN'"

    §6A game day, to find out what you do not know

    Break things on purpose while you are watching, and time how long detection takes. Every one of these has taught someone something.

    ExperimentHowWhat good looks like
    Bad deployPush an image whose entrypoint exits 1Circuit breaker rolls back; service stays healthy; nobody intervenes
    Poison messagePut malformed JSON on the ingest queueThree retries, lands in DLQ, alarm fires within 5 minutes, other documents unaffected
    Database failoveraws rds reboot-db-instance --force-failoverErrors for 60–120 seconds, pool reconnects, no manual action, alarm fires and clears
    Downstream throttlingSet Lambda reserved concurrency to 1 during a burstQueue depth grows, age alarm fires, nothing is lost, drains on its own
    Expired credentialRotate the database secret without the app re-reading itYou discover whether rotation actually works before it happens unattended
    Lost AZDeregister one AZ's targets from the target groupTraffic shifts, capacity halves, no outage

    Write the results into docs/game-days/. Findings from these exercises are the most convincing material you can bring to a conversation about operational maturity, because they are things that actually happened.

    §7Interview questions on this material

    How would you handle a GDPR erasure request in this system?

    The mechanics first, then the awkward part.

    Order matters: mark the document inaccessible before deleting anything, so a process that dies halfway leaves it hidden rather than partially deleted. Then delete every S3 object version and delete marker, because a plain delete on a versioned bucket only adds a marker and leaves the bytes. Then the derived artefacts: Textract raw blocks, thumbnails, exports. Then the database rows, where ON DELETE CASCADE removes extractions, enrichments, entities, and embeddings in one statement. Finally, write an audit record of the erasure containing no content, because the audit trail has to outlive the data it describes.

    Two places people forget. Embeddings are derived from the text and can leak information about it, so they must go, which is why chunks cascade. Logs may contain filenames or extracted values, which is why I redact aggressively at the logger and keep retention short.

    The honest gap is backups. RDS automated snapshots and S3 noncurrent versions inside the retention window still contain the data, and you cannot surgically edit a snapshot. My position is that the data is erased from production immediately and ages out of backups within the documented retention period, and that this is disclosed in the privacy notice. Regulators generally accept that; claiming instant erasure from backups would be false. Being able to say which parts are hard is a better answer than claiming the whole thing is easy.

    What are your RPO and RTO, and how do you know?

    RPO five minutes, RTO about one hour, and I know because I have run the restore.

    The RPO comes from RDS continuous backup, which lets me restore to any point in time within the retention window, so the exposure is the replication lag rather than the gap between snapshots. S3 versioning means object data has effectively no RPO. RTO is dominated by how long an RDS point-in-time restore takes to become available, plus updating the connection secret and rolling the service.

    The part I would emphasise is that both numbers were assumptions until the drill. I restore prod to a new instance quarterly, on a small instance class so it costs pennies, verify row counts and the newest timestamp, confirm the pgvector extension and the HNSW index survived, run a vector query, then destroy it. The first run surfaced real issues: the restored instance came up with the default security group rather than the source's, and Multi-AZ was not carried over. Both would have cost me time during an actual incident.

    For a Region-wide failure, my honest answer is that this single-Region design does not survive one, and the recovery would be re-applying Terraform in a second Region and restoring from a cross-Region snapshot copy, measured in hours. Making that faster means active-passive with cross-Region replication and a Route 53 health-check failover, which roughly doubles infrastructure cost. That is a business decision about how much a Region outage costs, and I would want the business to make it rather than assume it.

    Where does the money go in this architecture, and what would you cut first?

    The surprise for most people is that the NAT Gateway is the largest fixed cost, ahead of the database. Two gateways for AZ redundancy is about $66 a month before any traffic, plus roughly $0.045 per gigabyte processed. RDS Multi-AZ is next at around $50 because the standby doubles the instance charge. Fargate and the ALB follow.

    What I would cut, in order. First, add the free S3 gateway endpoint and interface endpoints for ECR, Secrets Manager, and CloudWatch Logs, which removes a large share of NAT data processing; pulling container images on every deploy adds up faster than you would expect. Second, run one NAT in dev and accept the reduced availability. Third, set CloudWatch log retention explicitly, because the implicit default is never expire and it accumulates silently. Fourth, S3 lifecycle tiering, since original scans are read for a few days and then almost never.

    On the AI side, the biggest lever is choosing the right Textract API. Plain text detection is around $1.50 per thousand pages; forms and tables analysis is around $65. Running the expensive one on everything would be roughly forty times the cost for no product benefit on documents where the fields do not matter. Same principle for Bedrock: a small model for a six-way classification, a larger one only for field extraction.

    And the thing I would do before any of that is turn on cost allocation tags and look at Cost Explorer weekly, because optimising without measurement is guessing.

    Someone reports that another tenant's document appeared in their search results. Walk me through your response.

    Treat it as a confirmed breach until proven otherwise, and work in two parallel tracks: contain, then diagnose.

    Contain. Disable the search endpoint via a feature flag rather than a deploy, because a deploy takes minutes and a flag takes seconds. Capture the reporting user's request id and tenant id from their session.

    Diagnose. The first question is which layer failed, and my design gives me a fast way to narrow it. Query the audit log and API logs for that request id to get the exact SQL path. Then check, in order: did the token carry the right tenant claim, did the request go through withTenant, was the transaction-scoped session variable set, and are the RLS policies still enabled on every table. The plausible causes are a route that bypassed the tenant helper, a policy accidentally dropped by a migration, or the application connecting as the table owner, which silently bypasses RLS entirely. That last one is the most likely and the easiest to introduce by accident.

    Verify the fix. The test I already have is the one that proves it: query the documents table with no tenant context and assert zero rows. If that test passes and the leak still happened, the bug is above the database, in the application layer.

    Afterwards. Determine scope from the logs, notify affected tenants, and under GDPR notify the supervisory authority within 72 hours if personal data was exposed. Then a written post-mortem, and one new automated control: a CI check asserting RLS is enabled on every tenant-scoped table, so a migration can never quietly remove it again.

    The reason I can answer this concretely is that the isolation was designed in layers, so the investigation is a short list of specific checks rather than an open-ended search.

    §8Definition of done

    Appendix · Reference

    Interview bank, glossary, and how to talk about this

    Everything that does not belong to a single sprint: the question bank organised by theme, a glossary of every AWS term used in the manual, a debugging index, and guidance on presenting the project without over-claiming.

    §1How to present this project

    The trap with a portfolio project is describing what you built. What earns respect is describing what you decided, and being straight about the limits.

    A 90-second opener

    "It's a multi-tenant document platform. Users upload documents, and an asynchronous pipeline runs OCR through Textract, then uses Bedrock to classify them, pull out structured fields, and generate embeddings so the corpus is semantically searchable.

    The API is Fastify on ECS Fargate; the pipeline is Lambda workers behind SQS queues; storage is S3 with a customer-managed KMS key, and Postgres on RDS with pgvector for the embeddings. All of it is Terraform, deployed by GitHub Actions using OIDC, so there is no AWS access key stored anywhere.

    The two decisions I'd most want to talk about are tenant isolation, which is enforced at four independent layers, and cost control in the OCR stage, where picking the right Textract API per document class is a forty-times difference in price."

    That gives an interviewer three or four threads to pull. Let them pick.

    Say these things out loud

    • "I chose a single AWS account with environment separation by naming, because multi-account tripled the setup for a solo build. In a team I'd start multi-account, since retrofitting it means migrating every resource."
    • "My revocation window is fifteen minutes, because JWTs are stateless. Here is what I would do if the requirement were immediate."
    • "pgvector is right up to a few million chunks. Past that I would move to OpenSearch, and the signal would be vector queries competing with transactional load for buffer cache."
    • "There is no cross-Region disaster recovery. Recovery from a Region failure would be re-applying Terraform elsewhere and restoring from a copied snapshot, measured in hours. Making that faster roughly doubles infrastructure cost, which is a business decision."
    • "I have not load-tested this past a few hundred requests per second, so my scaling claims past that are reasoning rather than measurement."

    Naming a limitation before being asked reads as judgement. Being caught without one reads as inexperience.

    §2Question bank by theme

    Architecture and design

    Why asynchronous processing rather than doing the work in the request?

    OCR on a fifty-page PDF takes tens of seconds, and Bedrock adds more. Holding an HTTP connection open for that ties up a Fargate task per document, hits load balancer and browser timeouts, and gives the user a spinner with no information. Worse, a failure anywhere in that chain loses the work entirely, because there is nowhere to retry from.

    Decoupling through a queue changes the failure model. The upload returns in milliseconds. The queue absorbs bursts, so a thousand simultaneous uploads produce a growing queue rather than a thousand timeouts. Retries and dead-lettering come from the platform rather than from code I write. Each stage scales independently, which matters because OCR and enrichment have different rate limits. And a poison document affects one message instead of one user's request.

    The cost is complexity that has to be designed rather than assumed: idempotency because delivery is at-least-once, a status model the frontend can poll, and observability that spans several services. Those are the parts worth talking about, because they are where the engineering actually is.

    How would this design change at 100× the volume?

    I would go through it layer by layer and name what breaks first.

    Database. Single-primary Postgres is the first ceiling. Reads move to replicas, and the connection count from Lambda becomes a real problem, so RDS Proxy goes in. Past that, Aurora for storage-level replication and faster failover, then partitioning documents by tenant, then sharding by tenant if a single writer is saturated.

    Vector search. pgvector competing with transactional load for buffer cache is the signal to move embeddings to OpenSearch Serverless, which also gives better hybrid relevance tuning.

    Pipeline. Textract and Bedrock account quotas become the bottleneck, so quota increases, and a split into priority and bulk queues so an interactive upload does not sit behind a fifty-thousand-document backfill. At sustained high volume, Bedrock Provisioned Throughput starts to make sense.

    Storage. S3 scales fine, but per-prefix request rates matter, and tenant-prefixed keys already distribute well. Lifecycle tiering becomes a much bigger lever in absolute terms.

    Operations. One account stops being viable. Multi-account with Organizations and SCPs, and the pipeline gains progressive delivery with canaries because the cost of a bad deploy has changed.

    The general point I would make is that the shape of the architecture survives, and what changes is where state lives and how work is prioritised. That is usually the sign the initial decomposition was reasonable.

    What would you do differently if you started again?

    Four things.

    Step Functions for the pipeline orchestration. My chain of queues and Lambdas works, but the sequencing lives implicitly in code across four services. A state machine would make the flow explicit, give visual execution history per document, and provide retry and catch semantics declaratively. I did not start there because queues were simpler for two stages, and by four stages the balance had shifted.

    UUIDv7 instead of v4. Random UUIDs fragment B-tree inserts. Time-ordered UUIDs keep insert locality while remaining non-enumerable. Cheap to choose on day one, annoying to change later.

    An eval harness before the prompts. I wrote the golden-set evaluation after the prompts, which meant my early iterations were spot-checks. Building the measurement first would have made the prompt work faster and more honest.

    Multi-account from the start if this were a team project, for the reasons in the isolation discussion.

    What I would keep: the state machine on document status, which caught real ordering bugs; row-level security as a backstop, which is cheap and fails closed; and recording model id and prompt version on every enrichment, which made A/B comparison possible later.

    Security

    Walk me through every place a secret exists in this system.

    Starting from the outside. No AWS credentials in GitHub, because CI uses OIDC federation to assume a role. No credentials on my laptop beyond an IAM Identity Center session, which is short-lived. No credentials in container images or task definitions: the database password lives in Secrets Manager, and the ECS agent fetches it with the execution role and injects it as an environment variable, so it never appears in the task definition, in Terraform state, or in the console.

    Application to AWS is all IAM roles, so no keys at all: task role for the API, execution roles per Lambda, and a role Textract assumes to publish to SNS.

    The places a secret does exist. Terraform state contains the generated database password, which is why the state bucket is encrypted, versioned, TLS-only, and access-restricted. Secrets Manager holds the password with rotation in prod. The running container's environment holds it in memory, which is unavoidable.

    What I do to keep it that way: pino redacts authorization headers, cookies, tokens, and presigned URLs, since a presigned URL contains a working signature. Secret scanning runs in CI. And the API's task role cannot read Secrets Manager, only the execution role can, so a code execution bug in the application cannot enumerate secrets.

    How do you know your IAM policies are not over-permissive?

    Three mechanisms, and none of them is reading the policy carefully, because that does not scale.

    Before deploying, aws iam simulate-principal-policy answers "would this role be allowed to do X" without doing X. I use it in tests to assert negatives, for example that the API role is denied s3:DeleteObject. Asserting what a role cannot do is more valuable than asserting what it can, because the can-do path gets exercised by the application anyway.

    Continuously, IAM Access Analyzer reports both external access and unused access. The unused-access findings are the useful ones: they tell you which permissions a role has held for months without using, which is exactly the list to remove.

    In CI, tfsec fails the build on over-broad policies, and a plan diff that widens a policy is visible in code review.

    The process I follow when writing a new policy is to start with nothing, run the workload, read the AccessDenied events in CloudTrail, and add precisely what appears. That produces a tighter policy than reasoning about what might be needed, and it documents itself.

    Data and databases

    A query that was fast is now slow. How do you diagnose it?

    Start with data, not theory. pg_stat_statements ranks statements by total time, which usually identifies the culprit immediately, and I have it preloaded via the parameter group. Performance Insights gives the same picture with wait-event attribution, so I can tell CPU from IO from lock waits.

    Then EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS) on the actual query. The things I look for: a sequential scan where an index existed, a row-count estimate wildly different from the actual, which means stale statistics and a VACUUM ANALYZE, or a nested loop chosen because the planner underestimated cardinality.

    The specific causes I have seen in a system like this. Table growth crossing a planner threshold, so an index that was ignored at ten thousand rows starts being used at a million, or the reverse. Index bloat from heavy updates, fixed with a concurrent reindex. A missing index on a new access pattern introduced by a feature. And specific to this platform, HNSW index size exceeding available memory, at which point vector queries start hitting disk and latency changes character rather than degrading smoothly. At 1024 dimensions each vector is about four kilobytes, so a million chunks is around four gigabytes before index overhead, which directly sizes the instance.

    Lock contention is worth checking early too, because it looks like slowness but has a completely different fix: pg_locks joined against pg_stat_activity shows who is blocking whom.

    Why keyset pagination rather than OFFSET?

    Two reasons, one about performance and one about correctness.

    Performance: OFFSET 10000 makes Postgres produce and discard ten thousand rows before returning any, so cost grows linearly with depth. A keyset query with WHERE created_at < $cursor ORDER BY created_at DESC LIMIT 25 uses the index to jump straight to the position, so page one thousand costs the same as page one.

    Correctness is the reason people underweight. With OFFSET, if a row is inserted while a user is paging, every subsequent page shifts, so they see a duplicate or miss a row entirely. A cursor anchored to a value is stable under concurrent writes.

    The trade-offs I accept: no jumping to an arbitrary page number, which is fine for an infinite-scroll document list and would not be fine for a report with numbered pages. And the cursor column needs a unique tiebreaker, so for high-volume inserts I would use (created_at, id) as a composite cursor to avoid ambiguity when timestamps collide.

    Operations

    You are paged at 3am: the API is returning 503. What do you do?

    First establish blast radius, because that determines urgency and direction. Is it all requests or some, all tenants or one, and did it start with a deploy? The dashboard answers that in about thirty seconds.

    The single most useful question is "what changed", so I check the deployment history first. If a deploy correlates, roll it back before investigating further. Rollback is registering the previous task definition revision, which takes seconds, and diagnosing a fire you have already put out is much more pleasant.

    If no deploy correlates, 503 from an ALB usually means no healthy targets, so I go straight to target health. The reason string is specific and tells me whether tasks are failing health checks, being killed, or never starting. From there: are tasks running at the desired count, is the service events log showing placement failures, and what do the application logs say. A task that cannot start points at the execution role or the image; a task that starts and dies points at configuration or a dependency.

    The dependency I check next is the database, because it is the one that takes everything down at once. Connection count against max_connections, CPU, and whether a failover happened.

    Throughout, I write timestamps into an incident channel as I go, because reconstructing a timeline afterwards from memory is unreliable and the post-mortem is where the real value is. And I would rather mitigate than diagnose: restore service first, understand it properly in the morning.

    How do you decide what to alarm on?

    I ask whether a user could tell. If yes, it is page-worthy. If no, it is a ticket at most.

    That rules out most infrastructure metrics as paging signals. High CPU might be a healthy service under load. High latency is never fine. So my paging set is small: error rate, p95 latency, anything in a dead-letter queue, documents stuck in a non-terminal state, and the database unreachable.

    The technique that matters most is alarming on ratios via metric math rather than counts, because fifty errors is meaningless without the denominator. After that, requiring multiple datapoints so a single spike does not page anyone, and composite alarms so a correlated failure produces one page rather than five.

    The test I apply to every proposed alarm: can I write a runbook for it? If I cannot describe what a responder should do, the alarm is not actionable and it will train people to ignore alarms. Every alarm description in my project ends with a runbook path.

    §3Glossary

    TermWhat it means here
    ALBApplication Load Balancer. Layer 7, understands HTTP, routes on path and host, terminates TLS.
    Availability ZoneOne or more data centres with independent power and networking, within a Region. AWS's unit of correlated failure.
    CIDRNotation for an IP range. 10.0.0.0/16 fixes the first 16 bits and leaves 65,536 addresses.
    Cognito user poolA managed user directory that issues OIDC tokens. Distinct from an identity pool, which exchanges tokens for AWS credentials.
    DLQDead-letter queue. Where messages go after exhausting retries, so they are inspectable rather than lost.
    EMFEmbedded Metric Format. A specially shaped JSON log line that CloudWatch turns into a metric with no API call.
    Envelope encryptionKMS gives S3 a data key, S3 encrypts your object with it locally, and stores the wrapped key alongside. Bucket Keys let one data key cover many objects.
    Execution roleECS: the role the agent uses to pull images and fetch secrets, before your code runs.
    HNSWHierarchical Navigable Small World. A graph-based approximate nearest-neighbour index used by pgvector.
    IdempotencyThe property that performing an operation twice has the same effect as once. Mandatory with at-least-once delivery.
    Inference profileBedrock: an identifier that routes across several Regions in a geography, reducing throttling. Prefixed eu. or us..
    JWKSJSON Web Key Set. The public keys an identity provider publishes so you can verify its tokens offline.
    NAT GatewayLets private subnets reach the internet outbound only. Charged hourly plus per gigabyte processed.
    OACOrigin Access Control. CloudFront signs requests to S3 so the bucket can stay private. Supersedes OAI.
    OIDCOpenID Connect. Identity layer over OAuth 2.0. Also how GitHub Actions authenticates to AWS without stored keys.
    PITRPoint-in-time recovery. Restore a database to any second within the backup retention window.
    PKCEProof Key for Code Exchange. Replaces a client secret for apps that cannot keep one, such as browsers.
    RLSRow-level security. Postgres policies filtering rows per session. Fails closed when the session variable is unset.
    RPO / RTOHow much data you may lose; how long recovery may take.
    RRFReciprocal Rank Fusion. Combines two rankings using 1/(k+rank), avoiding the need to normalise incomparable scores.
    SCPService Control Policy. An Organizations guardrail that caps what an account can do, even for its administrators.
    SSE-KMSServer-side encryption with a KMS key, giving you a key policy and CloudTrail records of every decrypt.
    Task roleECS: the role your application code uses at runtime.
    Visibility timeoutSQS: how long a received message is hidden from other consumers. Set it to at least 6× the consumer timeout.

    §5A twelve-week schedule

    Assuming eight to ten hours a week alongside a job. Adjust freely, but keep the ordering, because each sprint depends on the previous one.

    WeekSprintMilestone you can demo
    100An AWS account you trust, with budgets that work
    2–301API and database running locally with tests
    402Real buckets, real IAM, all from Terraform
    503Login works and the tenant comes from a token claim
    6–704Upload a file and watch a Lambda react to it
    805A scanned PDF becomes searchable text
    906Documents classified, fields extracted, semantic search working
    1007The whole thing running in AWS behind a load balancer
    1108 + 09Dashboards, alarms, and push-to-deploy
    1210 + 11A UI you can show, plus the security and cost story
    If you only have four weeks

    Do sprints 00, 01, 02, 04, and 05, then jump to 07. That gives you a deployed API, a working asynchronous pipeline, and real OCR, which is enough to have a substantial conversation. Add Bedrock next, because it is the part people ask about most. Leave the frontend last: it is the least differentiating part of the project, and a well-crafted README with architecture diagrams carries more weight than a rushed UI.

    §6Documents to write as you go

    • README.md with an architecture diagram, the decision list, and honest limitations. Most readers will not get past this file, so it deserves real effort.
    • docs/adr/ with one record per significant decision: context, options, choice, consequences.
    • docs/threat-model.md, the STRIDE table from Sprint 11.
    • docs/runbooks/, one per alarm.
    • docs/dr-drills.md with actual measured timings.
    • docs/game-days/ with what broke and what you learned.
    • docs/cost.md with a breakdown from Cost Explorer, not estimates.

    That documentation set is unusual in a portfolio project, and it does more to signal seniority than another feature would.
